# Project Handoff: Limit Order Book (LOB) Simulation

## 1. Executive Summary

This project is an interactive, educational **Limit Order Book (LOB) Simulation & Visualization Platform** built in Python and modern web technologies. It is based on the foundational research paper:

> **Gould, M.D., Porter, M.A., Williams, S., McDonald, M., Fenn, D.J. and Howison, S.D.** (2013).  
> *Limit order books.* Quantitative Finance, 13(11), 1709–1742.  
> [doi:10.1080/14697688.2013.803148](https://doi.org/10.1080/14697688.2013.803148) | [arXiv:1012.0349](https://arxiv.org/abs/1012.0349)

The platform pairs a pure-Python matching engine and stochastic market simulation models with sixteen teaching notebooks and a real-time web dashboard (FastAPI, Server-Sent Events, HTMX, Tailwind CSS, and TradingView Lightweight Charts). It lets researchers and students study market microstructure, order queues, emergent bid-ask spreads, and stylized facts (heavy tails, volatility clustering, price impact) through interactive experimentation.

The project is under git; Section 6 lists the changes since the baseline snapshot of 2026-11-01.

---

## 2. System Architecture

```
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        Web Browser Frontend                            │
│  - Tailwind CSS, four themes   - TradingView Lightweight Charts        │
│  - One EventSource per tab     - Five views, two with their own market │
│  - DOM ladder, depth chart     - Heatmap canvas, guided lessons        │
│  - One-click trading, drag     - Model parameter panel (HTMX)          │
│  - Study tiles, footprint, tape - Trigger rules, informed Reveal       │
└───────────────────────────────────▲────────────────────────────────────┘
                                    │  /events: panels (HTML) + bars (JSON)
┌───────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┐
│                          FastAPI Web Layer                             │
│  - app/main.py: Routes, validation, the event stream, threadpool       │
│  - app/sessions.py: One simulation per browser session (cookie)        │
│  - app/manager.py: LOBManager, simulation clock, trade-based bars      │
│  - app/model_specs.py: Dashboard models, parameters and ranges         │
│  - app/pairs.py: The Pairs view's two books, traders and stream        │
│  - app/statarb.py: The Stat-arb view's many books, traders and stream  │
│  - app/studies.py, app/triggers.py: study board, trigger rules         │
└───────────────────────────────────▲────────────────────────────────────┘
                                    │
┌───────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┐
│                       Core Simulation & Engine                         │
│  - lob/book.py: Limit order book, validation, cached depths            │
│  - lob/matching.py: PriceTimeEngine (FIFO) & ProRataEngine             │
│  - lob/models/: ZI, Hawkes ZI, two Mike-Farmer, Parlour, informed ZI   │
│  - lob/simulation.py: Event loop, event log, per-fill trade log        │
│  - lob/studies.py: Order flow, VPIN, depth and other studies of events │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Key Modules & Files

- **`lob/book.py`**:
  - `OrderBook`: Dual-sided order book with tick size and lot size validation.
  - Price levels live in `SortedDict`s (bid keys negated so iteration runs best-first); each level is a FIFO `deque`.
  - O(1) order lookup, depth-at-price queries and uniform random order sampling. Canceling removes the order from its level's queue, which is linear in that queue's length.
  - `validate(order)` checks tick size, lot size and order-ID uniqueness. Both engines call it *before* matching, so a rejected order never leaves the book half-changed.
  - `orders_at(price, side)` returns a level's queue in priority order.
  - Iceberg orders (`display_size`): while an order rests, `size` is its visible part, which is all that depths, depth profiles, snapshots and matching see, and `hidden` is the reserve (`hidden_depth_at_price` sums reserves at a price). An arriving iceberg trades its full size and is split when it comes to rest.
- **`lob/matching.py`**:
  - `PriceTimeEngine`: Price-time priority (FIFO within each price level).
  - `ProRataEngine`: Allocates proportionally to resting order sizes in whole lots, rounding down and handing leftover lots out in time priority, so fills and remainders stay multiples of the lot size.
  - Engines reduce resting orders' `size` in place as they fill.
  - When an iceberg's peak is filled, the next comes from its reserve and joins the back of its price level (losing time priority), so an incoming order can fill more than the visible depth; pro-rata shares follow visible sizes and the level is allocated again after refills. Ordinary orders behave exactly as before (every model's output hashes identically).
- **`lob/models/`** (all models continue from the caller's clock when it is ahead of their own):
  - `zero_intelligence.SmithFarmerModel`: Zero-intelligence model of Daniels et al. (2003) / Smith et al. (2003), Gould et al. Section 5.2.4. Limit orders land uniformly on the `price_range` ticks next to the *opposite* best quote, so they fall inside the spread with probability proportional to its width; market orders and per-order cancellations are Poisson. `_sample_side` / `_sample_size` hooks let ablations change signs and sizes before an order is priced, and `_sample_market_side` (`_sample_side` by default) is the side of a market order alone, for a subclass whose market orders have another rule (ZI's own output is unchanged).
  - `hawkes.HawkesZIModel`: The ZI model with market orders arriving as a self-exciting (Hawkes) process. The baseline rate is scaled to `mu * (1 - branching)` so the long-run arrival rate stays `mu`; every arrival excites, even one that finds the opposite side empty. Simulated exactly by thinning.
  - `mike_farmer.MikeFarmerModel`: Simplified Mike & Farmer (2008): power-law relative prices measured from the mid-price (always at least one tick), long-memory order signs, log-normal sizes, constant-rate cancellation.
  - `mike_farmer.MikeFarmer2008Model`: Mike and Farmer's structure: prices set relative to the same-side best quote with Student's t offsets (orders can land inside the spread or cross it and execute as effective market orders; no separate market order process), long-memory signs, and state-dependent cancellation simulated by thinning. Parameter values are illustrative, not their LSE calibration.
  - `long_memory.LongMemorySigns`: A continuous stream of long-memory signs (signs of FARIMA(0, H − 1/2, 0) noise, applied by overlap-save FFT convolution) whose memory never resets.
  - `strategic.ParlourModel`: A hand-tuned rule inspired by Parlour (1998): the probability of a market order rises with a narrow spread and a long queue at the best quotes. It is not a game-theoretic equilibrium model.
  - `informed.InformedZIModel` (3T.8): `SmithFarmerModel` plus informed traders who know a hidden value V, the dashboard's sixth model. V starts at the initial mid and moves as a random walk (`sigma_v` ticks per square root of a unit of time) with jumps (`jump_rate`, `jump_size`), advanced exactly over the model's own time steps and held at or above `value_floor` (20 ticks, or `price_range + 5` if that is more, and never above the start; it draws no random number). Informed traders send market orders of the model's size at a rate `kappa` times the gap in ticks between V and the quote they would hit, at most `cap` a unit of time, and only when V lies beyond that quote (Glosten and Milgrom's condition; no informed limit orders, no splitting). It is simulated exactly by thinning, so every informed order is profitable at the V it is sent at. The ground truth is kept on the model: `informed_lots` and `taker_lots` (their ratio, `informed_share`, is an outcome of `kappa`, `cap` and `sigma_v` and not a setting), `informed_ids` (an array copy of 8 bytes an order), `last_informed`, `value()`, and with `record_path` `value_at`, `maker_loss` (V less the price for a taker buy, the price less V for a sell) and `is_informed`. `persistence` above 0.5 makes the sides of the uninformed market orders a Markov chain (lag-1 sign autocorrelation 2 x persistence - 1, runs of about 1 / (1 - persistence) orders; limit orders and informed orders do not draw from it): the control for a flow that is one-sided without being toxic. With `kappa` or `cap` 0 and `persistence` 0.5 a run is `SmithFarmerModel`'s, event for event from the same seed, whatever V does.
- **`lob/simulation.py`**: `Simulation.run()` returns the event log as a DataFrame (stored internally in typed arrays: a 400k-event run takes about 190 MB); `Simulation.trades` has one row per fill; `Simulation.snapshots` holds book snapshots. The log's `bid_size` and `ask_size` are the visible depth at the best quotes. `Simulation(observer=...)` calls `observer(t, kind, side, price, size, fills, aggressor, quotes_before, quotes_after, book)` for each event of the run, warm-up included (for example `StudySet().observe`): a cancel is reported with the side, price and size of the order it removed (and one of an order that is not there not at all), `size` is an order's whole size on arrival, the quotes before are the previous event's quotes after, and the observer changes nothing in the log (a test holds the log equal).
- **`lob/calibration.py`**: maximum-likelihood estimates of every `MikeFarmer2008Model` parameter from an event log that starts from an empty book (arrival rate; rounded Student's t offsets; the signs' Hurst exponent; the state-dependent cancellation rate and imbalance offset, from replaying the log through a book). Notebook 08 checks them by recovering known parameters.
- **`lob/lobster.py`**: LOBSTER (Nasdaq) data: `read_lobster`/`save_lobster` for the message and order-book files, `lobster_orders` (messages to orders: executions at one time against one side are one aggressive order, a new order of its side at the same time is its resting part), and `calibrate_lobster`, the same estimates allowing for what such data hold back. An order that executes in full leaves no limit price, only a bound (its worst fill), so the price fit treats its offset as censored (`fit_rounded_t_censored`, exact for the model); the cancellation replay follows only orders the file shows placed, with quotes from the order-book file, after a burn-in (`last_unknown` says how long one needs). `simulated_lobster` writes the model's runs in LOBSTER's format, hiding what Nasdaq's feed hides.
- **`lob/strategies.py`**: the quantitative strategies the dashboard runs for the user (see 3M). Each `decide`s every `interval` of simulation time from a `View` (the best quotes, the lots at them, its own position) and answers with a `Plan`: quotes to rest (`MarketMaking`; `AvellanedaStoikov`, around a reservation price with a volatility estimated online; `PennyJumping`, a tick ahead of the best quotes), or a position to hold (`Momentum`: fast against slow exponential averages of the mid; `MeanReversion`: z-score of the mid over a window, with running sums; `Breakout`: a Donchian channel; `QueueImbalance`: the best queues' imbalance, after Gould & Bonart 2016, flat when it tips back; `OrderFlowImbalance`: Cont, Kukanov & Stoikov's OFI between decisions over a window, in average queue lengths; `RandomTrader`: a benchmark with its own xorshift64* generator, so it repeats exactly; `QuoteJoiner`: the null for making markets, at the best bid and ask and nothing else). A strategy keeps only its signal's state (`state`/`restore`, for recordings); its runner keeps the account. One that `keeps_queue` leaves a resting quote alone while it is at the price wanted.
- **`lob/pairs.py`**: two order books whose prices move together (notebooks 10 and 11). `FairValues`: a common random walk and a part of each book's own, which order flow moves (impact) and which, with a half-life, decays back, so the gap mean-reverts (`gap_sd`); without one the gap wanders. Two options, off by default: `beta_sd` makes beta drift (B moves beta_t times the factor's moves, added up in `b_common`; `gap_of` is the gap of two prices in these terms), and `stress_share` puts the common factor in a stressed regime, more volatile and perhaps drifting, the calm regime drifting the other way. `AnchoredZIModel`: zero-intelligence order flow around a book's fair value, so the book reprices as it moves. `Arbitrageur`: buys the cheap book and sells the dear one past a threshold, through its impact the only reversion when there is no half-life. Three traders, sharing `decide` (which trades on `observe`, the signal, and `want`, the rule), `learn` (past mids, with no trades), `warming`, `deviation` and `width` (its levels on the gap) and `signals`: `PairsTrader`, the gap's z-score over a window at a fixed hedge (`record` keeps each decision, for an exact daily record); `KalmanTrader`, on `KalmanHedge`, whose slope is the hedge and whose error, over its usual size, is the z-score; `CopulaTrader`, on `lob/copulas.py`, the rule of Rad et al. (2016). `PairMarket`: both books on one clock, `submit` for outside orders, `mark` for valuing positions while a side is empty, `run` sampling everything.
- **`lob/copulas.py`**: bivariate copulas for pairs trading: Gaussian, Student t, Clayton, Gumbel, Frank, `Rotated` (the survival copula of another) and `Independence`, each with `h` (the conditional probability), `cdf`, `logpdf`, `tail_dependence` and `sample`. `fit_copula` fits each family by canonical maximum likelihood (from the value Kendall's tau implies; the t's nu on a grid) and takes the lowest AIC. `pseudo_observations` and `Margin` rank returns, ties sharing their mean rank, as prices on half ticks tie.
- **`lob/statarb.py`**: many books with several common factors, and traders of many relations (notebook 12, see 3R). `FactorValues`: common factors through a table of loadings, and each book's own part, on a grid of time from a random stream of their own. `BookMarket` runs the books on one clock. `RelationTrader` trades the z-score of each of a set of portfolios, fixed or chosen by a function of past mids (`johansen_relations`, `best_baskets`, `pairs_by_distance`, `pairs_by_pvalue`); `ResidualTrader` is stat-arb on PCA residuals (Avellaneda and Lee 2010); `CopulaPortfolio` runs `lob.pairs.CopulaTrader` on each of many pairs. `score_portfolio` measures a trader over a span alone; `sector_loadings` and `mixed_loadings` make the two kinds of market. `BookMarket.flow` and `volume` keep, a book each, the lots of the models' own orders that traded on arrival (signed, and not), and `BookTrader.observe` shows a trader the market just before it decides (3S.3). Every `BookTrader` has a `rate` and a `band` (1 and 0 by default): with a rate below 1 its aim goes only that share of the way to its target at a decision, and with a band above 0 it does not trade within that many lots of its aim (`BookTrader._toward`, 3S.7). Stage 4 added (3S.9): a trend of the factors (`FactorValues(trend=...)`, an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck drift of each), an index book (`index=`, `index_volatility`: one more book, the last, whose fair value is the weighted sum of the others' and a premium) and stock deals (`deals=`, `lob/deals.py`), each off by default and each with a random stream of its own (`[seed, 4]`, `[seed, 3]`, `[seed, 5]`); `FactorValues.exposures()`, the books' exposures to the factors as they are now (the `loadings` array itself with no deal on); `BookTrader.check`, by which a trader refuses a market that lacks what it trades, and `BookTrader.book_cash`, the cash of a trader's fills a book each; `BookMarket._shift`, which moves a book's resting orders with a jump of its fair value; `score_holdings`, `score_portfolio` with the P&L's risk over 100 units and the part the factors made; and `johansen_relations`' limit of 12 books (`JOHANSEN_BOOKS`), the most the critical values of the test cover.
- **`lob/crosssection.py`**: cross-sectional long/short traders over the same books (notebook 13, see 3S). A `CrossSectionTrader` scores every book, takes the scores less their projection on the ones and the books' exposures to estimated factors (`neutralize`, `estimate_factors`: the PCA of `ResidualTrader`), and holds lots in proportion, rounded. `ReversalTrader` buys the books that fell, `MomentumTrader` those that rose (over a look back that may end some decisions ago), `LeadLagTrader` those that a ridge regression on the other books' last moves says will rise, and `FlowTrader` those with the buying in their trades or their queues. `contrarian_decomposition` splits a contrarian portfolio's profit into the books' own and cross autocovariances and the dispersion of their means (Lo and MacKinlay 1990). With a rate below 1 or a band above 0 a trader goes toward its target at every decision, and not only at a rebalance (3S.7).
- **`lob/sparse_baskets.py`**: baskets chosen by Box and Tiao's predictability and not by Johansen's test (notebook 13's section 6, see 3S.8). `fit_var1` fits a VAR(1) to the books' levels, `predictability` is the share of a portfolio's variance that the lagged levels explain, `box_tiao` the portfolios of some books by predictability (a generalized eigenproblem, least first), `greedy_basket` grows a set of books one at a time (d'Aspremont 2011) and `sparse_baskets` does that from every pair, ranks the distinct sets, and gives them in `best_baskets`' shape, so a `RelationTrader` takes it as its `choose`. It tests nothing, is scale-free and searches greedily (3S.8).
- **`lob/deals.py`**: stock deals in the books of `lob/statarb.py` (notebook 15, see 3S.9). `Deals` is the settings and nothing else, frozen: `rate`, `premium`, `length`, `breaks` (the chance a deal breaks), `priced` (the chance the market gives to that), `cool` and `within`. `Deal` is one announced: its target and acquirer, the `ratio` of the acquirer's shares offered for each of the target's, when it was announced and when it ends, `outcome` and `ended_at` once over, and, hidden from traders, the time it will break if it will. The module imports nothing from `lob/statarb.py`: the deals on and over, their draws and when each book can next be in one are in `FactorValues`, which makes them afresh in `reseed`.
- **`lob/indexarb.py`**: index arbitrage over `FactorValues`' index book (3S.9). `replicate_index` gives the one relation `(-c, 1)` of the index against every book at its weight, `index_basket` a sample of a few books chosen by forward selection on levels, and `IndexTrader`, a `RelationTrader` with `legs` (0 for every book at the published weights, else a sample) and `hedged` (off: the index leg alone), which refuses a market with no index book.
- **`lob/deal_traders.py`**: the traders of `lob/deals.py` (3S.9). `DealTrader` enters each deal that is on once its gap, `r * mid_A - mid_T`, is wide enough, long the target and short the offer's ratio of the acquirer, and closes both when the deal is over, keeping a `DealRecord` for each; `NewsAwareTrader` is a `RelationTrader` that is flat in every relation holding a deal's target while it is on and for a quarantine after, and trades the rest as it would.
- **`lob/impact.py`**: a metaorder's impact against a twin run from the same book, model and random state that sends nothing (common random numbers): `warmed_market`, `paired_metaorders` (notebook 09).
- **`lob/metrics.py`**, **`lob/analysis/`**: returns (`event_time_returns`), per-order VWAP (`trades_by_order`), virtual price impact on book snapshots, ACF with white-noise bands, rank-based volatility ACF, Hurst (R/S), Hill and power-law estimators, time-averaged depth profiles, order flow imbalance (`order_flow_imbalance`, Cont, Kukanov and Stoikov 2014) with `ofi_regression`, and plotting helpers.
- **`lob/studies.py`**: microstructure studies on the stream of book events, apart from any dashboard (see 3T; notebook 14, 3U). `observe(t, kind, side, price, size, fills, aggressor, quotes_before, quotes_after, book, own)` is called once for each event that changed the book, after the engine ran: `t` is the simulation clock (never an order's or a fill's own timestamp, which lag it in a replay), `kind` is `ARRIVAL_LIMIT`, `ARRIVAL_MARKET` (only the models send it: a user's market order is a limit order at a protected price) or `CANCEL`, `size` an order's whole size on arrival (not an iceberg's peak), `quotes_before` and `quotes_after` the touch as (best bid, lots there, best ask, lots there), and `book` the book after the event, valid for the call only. Eight studies, each a `Study` with `SETTINGS` (a `Setting`: a range or options, parsed with a message that says what is wrong), `observe`, `values` (every value as of a time, `None` where undefined and never NaN), `sample` (its main value once a unit of simulation time) and `configure` and `reset` (the data cleared, the settings kept): `OFI`, `CVD`, `TradeDelta`, `VPIN`, `OrderToTrade`, `BookImbalance`, `Spread` and `Resilience`. Windows are in simulation time and exact (`_Window`: a deque and its sum, O(1) amortized), and a study that needs a full window says `None` until one has passed since its start. A `StudySet` passes events to the studies that are on (those that need fills only to events that traded, those that read the book when sampled to none) and takes the unit samples (`sample_to`, filling a gap the clock jumped over with the book as it stood). `ofi_increment`, Cont, Kukanov and Stoikov's e_n, moved here from `app/facts.py`, which imports it, with the test that holds it equal to `lob.metrics.order_flow_imbalance`. The docstrings say where a definition follows the code of VisualHFT (Apache License 2.0, github.com/visualHFT/VisualHFT; 3T.9).
- **`app/sessions.py`**: `SessionRegistry` holds one `LOBManager` per browser session, found through a random ID in an HTTP-only, SameSite=Lax cookie (tabs of one browser share it). IDs are issued only by the server. Sessions without an open stream are evicted after 30 minutes idle, and the least recently seen one beyond 50 sessions; eviction stops the session's simulation. Each manager is given a board of microstructure studies (`StudyBoard`, `attach_studies`) and one of trigger rules (`TriggerBoard`, `attach_triggers`) when the session is made; the scratch managers of a fit and a training tape have neither and pay nothing for them.
- **`app/manager.py`**:
  - `LOBManager`: One session's book, engine, model, parameters, seed, background runner and manual orders.
  - One simulation clock (`sim_time`): model events advance it by the model's own event times; each manual action advances it by `MANUAL_ACTION_DT` (0.5).
  - Executed trades are stored as one-unit bars (`deque(maxlen=100_000)`); `get_candles()` rebuilds OHLCV bars, with buy and sell volume, for any interval.
  - `reset()` restarts the random generator from the session's seed, so the same seed and parameters replay a run exactly.
  - Every move of the clock goes through `_advance`, which starts an order-book heatmap column (`HeatColumn`) at each whole unit of time it reaches: the visible depth 30 ticks either side of the mid just before the event that reached it (bids positive, asks negative), the user's iceberg reserves, and the lots traded during the unit. The most recent 600 are kept; none are taken during a collection, after which the heatmap starts over (as after a reset, with a new `heat_version`).
  - The user's fills, as maker or taker (a fill against their own order counts on both sides), are kept per unit of time (`OwnBar`); `own_trade_marks` aggregates them to the chart's interval for its markers.
  - `move_orders` moves the user's orders at one price to another as one manual action (each canceled and entered again with what it had left and the same iceberg peak, in its old queue order); `cancel_all_orders` cancels theirs, optionally on one side or at one price. `cancel_order_by_id` cancels only the user's own orders.
  - `Account` books a trader's fills by average cost (realized P&L on reducing a position, the rest marked to the mid) and scores each fill against the mid before it (edge) and 100 events later (markout, `MARKOUT_EVENTS`; in events so that models with different event rates compare like with like). The user has one (`account`), and each of their strategies another.
  - `RunningStrategy` and `start_strategy`/`stop_strategy`/`resume_strategy`/`remove_strategy`: the user's strategies (see 3M), at most `MAX_STRATEGIES`. After each model event, `_run_strategy` asks each one that is due for its plan, from `_view`: the book without the strategy's own orders (it knows which are its, so a market maker never quotes around its own quotes), and its position. Quotes are withdrawn and placed afresh; a target position is reached by a protected market order whose rest is canceled (aggressive), or by one order at the best quote, left alone while it is still the one wanted so it keeps its place in the queue (passive). Like the user's orders, theirs (`_strategy_orders`) are never canceled by the models; their fills go to their own accounts. `strategies_summary` is the leaderboard, with the user's row and one P&L sample per unit of time for each sparkline. Reset rebuilds each from its settings with a fresh account.
  - The regime and the nulls (see 3P): `_takes` and `_observe_trade` follow the spread just before each trade and the mid's change from one trade to the next; `_market_markouts` and `_flow` what the model's own resting and market orders, and the quote joiners, earn 100 events after they trade (weighted averages over the last 2,000 or so, which fit in a recording's starting state); `regime` reports them; at each markout, `_null_rate` gives the null a fill is held against (`Account.null_*`).
  - Fitting (see 3O): `training_tape` makes a training period from the session as it stands, with random numbers of its own; `fit_strategy` replays it once for each combination of the values given, the candidate trading from its start, and ranks them (`Fit`); `start_fitted` starts one, keeping its training record (`RunningStrategy.fit`).
  - Recording: `_process` records each order as it arrives (before matching changes it), `_cancel_resting` is the one path for the user's and strategies' cancellations (and records them), and engine, model and strategy changes are noted. `start_recording`/`stop_recording` keep at most `MAX_RECORDINGS`; Reset saves one in progress, and one stops by itself at `MAX_EVENTS`.
  - Playback: `start_playback` loads a recording's starting state (`_load_recording_start`, also `rewind_playback`), after which `step` takes orders and cancellations from the tape (`_step_playback`/`_apply`) instead of the model: each recorded order is entered afresh (ids mapped), as the user's or a replayed strategy's when it was. Replayed strategies (`replayed`) re-decide from the same view when they did (`_follow_decision`, so their signals and state follow along) but the tape carries their orders. The user and strategies started during a playback act as usual; a cancellation whose order is gone is skipped. `seek_playback` goes forward through the tape, or from its start to go back; `exit_playback` lets the selected model take over the book.
  - The studies, the tape, the footprint and the triggers (see 3T): `_observe` gives the board each event that changed the book, from two places, the end of `_process` (every arrival, and the model's cancels, with the order's original size and, for a cancel, the canceled order, both read before the engine ran) and `_cancel_resting` (the cancels of the user's and the strategies' orders). The quotes before are the board's `prev`, kept from the last event; when nothing traded and the order is strictly behind its side's best quote the quotes after are the same object, which saves looking at the book for most events. `_resync_studies` measures the next event from the book as it stands after what changes the book outside those two places (a reset, a playback's start) and after a time the board was off. `_advance` takes the unit samples (the last 600; at most 600 after a jump of the clock) from the book as it stood just before the event that reached the unit. The board is off in scratch managers (there is none), while collecting (the CVD, the footprint's cells and the tape still count: they live in `_record_trade`), and in a seek except for its last `SEEK_STUDY_LOOKBACK` (40,000) events, when it starts over (`_restart_studies`). `studies_version` changes on a reset, a rewind, a playback's start, the end of a collection, the end of a seek's suspension and a change of a study's settings, and the pages holding studies or tape reload; a study that raises switches the board off with one notice (`_study_failed`) and the run goes on. `_record_trade` stamps each fill with the clock, not the fill's own timestamp, into the tape's ring (`tape_ring`, the last `TAPE_FILLS` = 2,000 as (number, simulation time, price, size, side); `fill_seq` is never reset) and keeps each base bar's lots at each price (`BaseBar.cells`, {price: [lots sold into the bid, lots bought from the ask]}, for the last `FOOTPRINT_UNITS` = 12,000 bars, about 6 MB); `get_candles(footprint=..., reveal=...)` adds `cells` (for the last `FOOTPRINT_BARS` = 300 buckets) and `fair`. `_after_event` checks the trigger rules where split orders and strategies already run, after each step of the model (an idle one too), each book event of a playback and each action of the user's, and never inside `_process`; `_fire` runs a firing's actions and `_flatten` closes the user's position; `_halt` ends the batch of steps a rule paused in, `_acting` keeps a rule's own actions from checking the rules again, and a seek (`_quiet`) or a collection checks none. For the informed model, `informed_status` gives the realized share of the model's taker lots and what makers lost on the informed fills against V (`_score_informed`), `reveal_on` is a session flag and not a parameter (a parameter change rebuilds the model and reseeds it), and `_value_samples` holds V once a unit.
- **`app/live.py`**: `LiveMarket`, what the Pairs and Stat-arb views' markets have alike: their speed, run loop (`_run`, paced by the wall clock), `advance` for steps, and the streams to wake.
- **`app/pairs.py`**: the Pairs view (see 3Q.5). `TRADER_KINDS` says what each kind offers (settings, how to make one, how to describe it), and the form and endpoint are generated from it. A setting is a `Param` (a number in a range), a `Switch` or a `Choice` (named values, a drop-down: the Stat-arb view's order flow trader reads queues or trades). `PairsSession`, one per browser session (`LOBManager.pairs`, made when first needed and shut down with the session): a `PairMarket` from the settings (`MARKET_PARAMS`, applied with `configure`, which resets), its run loop (`_run`, paced by the wall clock), `advance` for steps, and up to `MAX_TRADERS` `PairsRunner`s (`start_trader`, optionally learning from the chart; `set_active`; `remove`). `_event` steps the market, notes each change of a trader's target (`marks`) and takes a sample at each whole unit of time (`_sample`: mids, fair values, both gaps, beta, and each trader's P&L, levels in the chart's terms, signal and hedge). `board`, `book_levels`, `gap_sds` and `context` are what the panels show; `history`, `samples_since` and `marks_since` feed the chart. For fitting (see 3Q.10) it says what a fit here tries (`fit_plan`, within `MAX_FIT_UNITS`) and how one candidate trades alone in a fresh market (`training_run`); the fit itself, `start_fitted` and the one-at-a-time pool are `app/training.py`'s.
- **`app/statarb.py`**: the Stat-arb view (see 3R.6, 3S.5, 3S.8 and 3S.11). `TRADER_KINDS` and `MARKET_PARAMS` say what it offers, as in `app/pairs.py`, and `CROSS_SECTIONAL` names the four kinds that score every book. The Baskets kind has a *Chosen by* choice, Johansen's test (the default) or Box-Tiao predictability (`method`; `lob/sparse_baskets.py`). `StatArbSession`, one per browser session (`LOBManager.statarb`): a `BookMarket` from the settings, up to six `StatArbRunner`s, `samples` for the chart, `mids` kept for a new trader to learn from (more than the chart keeps, as what it chooses from is longer), `test_relations`, which runs the Johansen test and Engle-Granger off the event loop, and, for fitting (see 3R.6 and 3R.7), `plan` (within `MAX_FIT_BOOK_UNITS`, leaving out what cannot trade in this many books) and `training_run`. Every kind has a trade rate and a no-trade band (`COSTS`; 3S.7), and `COST_GRID` and `SCORE_COST_GRID` are the rates and bands a fit proposes for them. Stage 4 (3S.11) gave `MARKET_PARAMS` nine settings, an index book, deals and a trend (`market_from` asks `FactorValues` for them only when one is on, `book_names` puts `IDX` last, `deals_from` makes the `Deals`), and `TRADER_KINDS` two kinds, `index` (`IndexTrader`) and `deals` (`DealTrader`), the second with no fit (`TraderKind.no_fit` in `app/pairs.py`, `NO_FIT_DEALS`). `KIND_TRADES` and `market_lacks` say what a kind trades that a market may lack (`configure` refuses new settings that would take it from a trader on the board), and `StatArbSession._unsuited` refuses it at a start and in a fit's plan; `MAX_SIGNALS` (12) and `SIGNAL_SLOTS` (17, with an index book) are how many books' signals and chips a trader shows, `signal_cap` says which, and the board and the samples read the exposures through `fair.exposures()`.
- **`app/training.py`**: fitting a trader over training runs, for the Pairs and Stat-arb views alike: `TrainedMarket` is a `LiveMarket` whose `fit_trader` runs every candidate alone in fresh markets in worker processes while the live market goes on (`cancel_fit` and `start_fitted` with it, one fit at a time on a server, whatever the view), `plan_fit` says what a fit runs within a budget, `rank_candidates` ranks the results against the user's settings, and `TrainingFit`, `fit_note`, `trades_from` and `same_market` hold and describe them. `TrainedMarket.lacks(kind, base)` is the question a fit asks of a view before it asks for values to try: whether the market lacks what a kind trades (None by default; the Stat-arb view answers it).
- **`app/recording.py`**: recordings of a session (see 3N). A `Recording` is the session as it stood when recording began (`start`: every resting order in queue order with whose it was, icebergs' peaks and reserves as they were, the accounts, the strategies' settings, accounts and `state()`, pending markouts, the clock) and a `Tape` of what followed, in typed columns (about 34 bytes an event): each order and cancellation with its origin (the model, the user, or a strategy's id), engine and model changes, and each strategy's decisions, starts, stops and removals. `to_bytes`/`from_bytes` save it as a NumPy `.npz` (columns, plus the rest as JSON), readable with `numpy.load(..., allow_pickle=False)`; `from_bytes` bounds every read and `check` validates every field of a file from elsewhere. A recording's `start` also carries the lots of a VPIN bucket (`start['studies']['vpin_v']`; older files lack it and the playback calibrates its own), so a playback measures with the live market's V.
- **`app/strategy_specs.py`**: the strategies the dashboard offers, their families (making markets, trading on a signal, a benchmark), tags, summaries, signals' names, settings and allowed ranges (reusing `model_specs.Param`, and `Choice` for the way it trades), and the values each proposes to try when fitted (`fit_grid`, `fit_default`). The Strategies card's form, its fit panel and the endpoints are generated from them.
- **`app/fitting.py`**: what a fit tries, for the Trade view's strategies and the traders of the Pairs and Stat-arb views alike: `parse_grid` reads the values from a fit panel, and `candidates` makes the combinations, leaving out contradictory ones and drawing a random sample when there are too many.
- **`app/model_specs.py`**: The models the dashboard offers (six, and manual placement), their labels, one-line summaries, default parameters, allowed ranges and notebook 05's values (`notebook=None` for the informed model, which notebook 05 does not run: its parameter panel has no notebook values to fill in, and the facts panel does not say that the notebook runs it); the model menu and parameter panel are generated from it.
- **`app/studies.py`**: the studies of one session (see 3T). `StudyBoard` holds one of each study with its settings (which stay when the session starts over while the data is cleared), the samples (one row a unit of simulation time, the last 600: the unit, the mid and each study's main value) and what the page needs: `snapshot` gives copies of plain values, the version, the settings, the latest values and the samples since the page's last, with `None` for a value that is not defined. `STUDY_SPECS` describe each study's settings as the other forms do (`Param` and `Choice`, made from the library's `Setting`s, so the form and the check of its input have one source), and `STUDY_HELP` says what each study measures, how it is defined and what to read it by. `SessionCVD` counts through seeks and collections. A board exists only in a session's manager.
- **`app/triggers.py`**: trigger rules on the studies' values (see 3T.7). `TriggerBoard` holds the rules and decides which fire from the values it is given; `Rule`, `Condition` (a study's value, an operator, a threshold), `Firing`; `validate` and `parse_rule` (the editor's form); `TRIGGER_VALUES` (the twelve values a condition can read). The manager reads the values, runs the actions and says so (`_after_event`).
- **`app/depth_chart.py`**: the depth chart under the order book: cumulative visible depth from the mid outward as SVG paths, over the same number of ticks either side of the mid (to the 30th level of whichever side reaches further, 10 to 100 ticks).
- **`app/facts.py`**: Live stylized facts. `FactsRecorder` samples each session's event stream as notebook 05 samples a `Simulation`; `summarize` computes the notebook's statistics with single-run standard errors (see 3F). The OFI increment it uses is `lob.studies.ofi_increment`.
- **`app/main.py`**: FastAPI endpoints (`/api/sim/*`, `/api/order/*` including `move` and `cancel_all`, `/api/strategies/start` (also a fit's candidate) and `fit`, `/api/strategies/{id}/stop`, `resume` and `remove`, `/api/recording/start` and `stop`, `/api/recordings/{id}/download` and `delete`, `/api/recordings/upload`, `/api/playback/start`, `seek` and `exit`, `/api/chart/*`, `/api/engine`, `/api/candlesticks` with the user's fills per bar (and running position and cash, for the P&L line), `/api/heatmap`, `/api/export/*`, `/api/snapshot`, `/api/studies` (GET; `POST /api/studies/config` changes one study's settings or switches it on or off), `/api/tape` (GET, the fills after a number), `/api/chart/footprint` and `/api/chart/reveal` (a session's flags; the charts reload), `/api/triggers` (GET the rules; POST makes one), `/api/triggers/{id}` (POST changes one) and `/api/triggers/{id}/arm`, `disarm` and `delete`; for the Pairs view `/api/pairs/control`, `speed`, `config`, `traders/start` (also a fit's candidate), `traders/fit`, `traders/fit/cancel` and `traders/{id}/stop`, `resume` and `remove`; for the Stat-arb view the same, and `/api/statarb/test`) with validated inputs (invalid values return 422), the Pairs and Stat-arb views' streams, `/pairs/events` and `/statarb/events` (`market_stream`: the chart's history and new points, the state, three panels and the notices, and apart from them the last fit's panel), and the `/events` stream (panels, bars, the user's fills per bar, heatmap columns, `studies` (the latest values and the samples since the page's last, whole after a new version), `tape` (the fills since the last push, or the ring), `triggers` (the rules and what each has seen, when it changed), `informed` (the ground truth and the Reveal switch, when it changed), `ticks` (the book at every tick near the mid, from `ticks_view`, for the tick view), controls and notices). Templates render on worker threads from copied values only.
- **`app/templates/`**: `index.html` (the views, the layout and the event-stream script), `components/chart.html` (trade-based candlesticks with stacked buy/sell volume, the VWAP, and the user's orders and fills), `ladder.html` (the order book by levels; `ladder_mode.html` its Levels/Ticks switch, `ticks_ladder.html` the tick view's frame), `depth_chart.html`, `pnl.html` (your P&L), `strategies_card.html`, `strategies.html` and `fit_result.html` (the Strategies card's form and fit panel, its pushed leaderboard, and a fit's candidates), `recordings_card.html` and `recordings.html` (the Recordings card and its list, pushed only when it changes), `model_params.html` (parameter panel), `facts.html` (stylized facts), `ofi.html`, `heatmap.html`, `lessons.html`, `guide.html` (how to read the page), `order_result.html`, `sim_controls.html`, `play_button.html`, `sim_badge.html`, `engine_toggle.html`, `order_form.html`, `active_orders.html`, `trades.html` (the tape: a static skeleton that `lob-tape.js` fills, no longer a pushed panel), `metrics.html`, `studies.html` (the study band) and `triggers_card.html`; for the Pairs view `pairs_view.html` (its forms, controls and chart) and the pushed `pairs_status.html`, `pairs_books.html` and `pairs_board.html`; for the Stat-arb view `statarb_view.html` and the pushed `statarb_status.html`, `statarb_books.html` and `statarb_board.html`; `fit_runs_result.html`, a fit's candidates on either view; and `panes.html`, the macros both take their fields, fit panel and chart controls from.
- **`app/static/js/`**: the page's own scripts (the rest are vendored): `lob-ticks.js` (the order book's tick view), `lob-ladder.js` (one-click trading, drag-to-move and the keyboard, in both views of the order book), `lob-heatmap.js` (draws the heatmap), `lob-lessons.js` (the lessons and their dock), `lob-recordings.js` (the Record button, the playback bar and the Recordings card, from the status badge's data) `lob-panes.js` (what the Pairs and Stat-arb views share: `lobPanes`, charts on one time axis with their controls, `lobMarketView`, a view's stream, controls, forms and board, and `lobFitPanel`, fitting a trader over training runs), `lob-pairs.js` (the Pairs view's lines and readout), `lob-statarb.js` (the Stat-arb view's), `lob-studies.js` (the study band), `lob-footprint.js` (the footprint on the candle chart), `lob-tape.js` (the tape), `lob-triggers.js` (the triggers card and the firings' marks on the chart) and `lob-informed.js` (the informed model's line and Reveal).
- **`scripts/build_themes.py`**: generates `app/static/css/themes.css`, the color themes, as CSS variables (see 3I).

---

## 3. Web Dashboard Features

### A. Sessions and Live Updates
1. **One simulation per browser.** Each browser gets its own simulation; tabs of the same browser share it.
2. **One event stream per tab** carries the panels, the chart and the controls. Browsers allow about six connections per host over HTTP/1.1, so a tab used to need two streams and three tabs froze the page; four tabs now work. The Pairs and Stat-arb views keep that: each shows another market, so while one is shown the tab closes `/events` and holds that view's stream in its place, which also brings the notices. What `/events` keeps current is hidden there and comes up to date when it connects again, the chart reloading the bars it missed (also on a page that opened on the Pairs view, which is why that chart loads before any connection). A test opens four tabs on the view.
3. **Tabs stay in sync**: a change to the model, parameters, seed, engine or speed in one tab appears in the session's other tabs. A tab that already shows the change keeps its "Applied" message, a parameter form someone is typing in updates once focus leaves it, and the speed slider is not moved while dragged. A panel with a request on its way (the parameter panel, the engine toggle) is not replaced by a push until the request ends, or the reply would have nowhere to go.
4. **Connection indicator** in the header: Connecting, Live or Reconnecting. If the stream comes back attached to a different simulation (server restart or expired session), the page reloads itself.

### B. Models and Parameters
1. Six models plus manual placement: Smith et al. ZI, ZI with self-exciting market orders, Mike & Farmer simplified, Mike & Farmer (2008 structure), Parlour-inspired rule, and ZI with informed traders (3T.8: a hidden value that informed traders trade on, with a Reveal switch on the chart and a line that says what share of the taker lots they sent).
2. **Model parameters panel**: a one-line summary of the current model (shown in the collapsed bar), every parameter plus the random seed. Apply validates every field and either applies all of them (continuing from the current book) or changes nothing and shows per-field messages. Parameters are kept per model.
3. **Reset Book** restarts from the seed, so the same seed and parameters replay the same run.

### C. Candlestick Chart & Volume Dual-Panel
1. **Trade-based bars on the simulation clock**: bars aggregate executed trades only. Intervals without trades are drawn flat at the last trade price (the mid-price before the first trade) with zero volume. There is no synthetic history.
2. **Intervals**: `1s` to `5m` in units of simulation time (each model's own clock, not wall-clock time). Changing the interval rebuilds every bar from the stored trades.
3. **Stacked volume**: green is buy-initiated volume, red sell-initiated.
4. **Zoom**: horizontal zoom from 2 to 32 px per bar with presets, in the toolbar between the two panels. `Fit` shows every bar (at most 32 px wide) and also recenters both charts vertically, switching their price axes back to auto-scaling after a drag. Both panels stay horizontally aligned.
5. **Whole-tick prices**: the price axis and the OHLC readout show whole ticks (102, not 102.00).
6. **Session VWAP**: an amber line on the price chart, and in the OHLC readout to two decimals. Each candle carries the VWAP at its close; bars keep the session's running volume and turnover, so the value survives truncation. No technical indicators: the models' prices are built to be unpredictable.
7. **Your orders and fills**: a dashed line in its side's color at each price where the user has orders resting ("you buy 40", "you sell 10 +90" for an iceberg's hidden rest), and an arrow on each bar in which they traded, as maker or taker: up below the bar for buying, down above it for selling, with the lots and average price. The markers follow interval changes; a small legend appears once there are any.
8. **Reloads**: a reset, a rewind or a new connection reloads the bars. Bars pushed while its answer is on the way wait for it (`heldBars` in `chart.html`): after a reset or a rewind they can be older than what the series still holds, which Lightweight Charts refuses with an error, and those the answer has not got yet must not be lost. Only the latest reload counts. A test delays the answer to force the order; without it the error showed in about one playback test in twelve. The answer and the pushes also come on different connections, in no fixed order, so a bar pushed before a reset or a change of interval can arrive after the answer that follows it: the answer carries the chart's `version` (`chart_version`, which every reset, rewind, change of interval, footprint or Reveal switch and collection moves on), each pushed bar carries the version it was pushed for (`v`, added by the stream), and the chart drops a bar of another version (`loadedVersion`). `loadedInterval` is the interval of the bars the chart holds; `currentInterval` changes at the click, before they arrive.
9. **Footprint and the hidden value**: a toggle on the toolbar draws the volume traded at each price in each bar, sold into the bid and bought from the ask, on the candles (3T.5); with the informed model, Reveal draws its hidden value as a line on them (3T.8). Fired trigger rules mark the chart beside your fills (3T.7). The zoom slider reaches 72 px a bar, from 32, while the footprint is on.

### D. Depth of Market, Controls and Performance
1. Fixed-height DOM with 10 ask and 10 bid slots, padded so the layout never shifts; mid-price and spread banner pinned in the middle. Levels holding the user's resting orders show "you N".
2. **Trading from the book**: a click on a level copies its price into the order form. With **1-click** on (the toggle in the DOM header, which rings the card in amber), it places a limit order there instead, for the size in the limit form: a buy on a bid level, a sell on an ask level, reported in a notice. Dragging a "you" badge onto another level moves those orders there (`POST /api/order/move`): they go to the back of the new level's queue, as a price change does on an exchange, and a price across the spread trades on arrival. The ladder lists only levels with orders and is pushed five times a second, so its rows shift as levels empty and fill: the book holds still while a level is pressed or dragged, and while the pointer aims at it with one-click on (refreshing every two seconds), so the level clicked is the level seen.
3. **Depth chart** under the order book (a panel of its own): the visible lots bid and offered, added up from the mid outward, as two step curves over the same number of ticks either side of the mid (to the 30th level of whichever side reaches further, always taking in both best quotes). A large resting order is a cliff in its side's curve; the user's orders are marked on its price axis.
3a. **Tick view**: Levels | Ticks in the book's header switches to a row for every price 40 ticks either side of the mid, bids left and asks right, fixed in place and updated where they are (`lob-ticks.js`, from the stream's `ticks` events). One-click trading and dragging work at any price there, empty ones included; the rows are built around the mid again by Center, or by themselves once the mid has moved 20 ticks, never while the pointer is on them. The choice is kept in this browser.
3b. **Keyboard**: the book takes the focus with Tab; the arrows move a cursor over its prices (and between bids and asks in the tick view), Enter does what a click would there, M picks up your orders at the cursor (arrows choose a price, Enter puts them there, Escape leaves them). The hint says what Enter or M would do; screen readers follow the cursor (`aria-activedescendant`). The right arrow does not step the simulation while the book has focus.
4. One compact control card: Play/Pause (optimistic), Step, Reset and a Running/Paused status on one row with the model menu and the engine toggle (switches on the server and shows the engine in use). At 1440x900 both charts are visible without scrolling.
5. Speed from 1 to 2000 events per second (default 50) on a logarithmic slider; the preset matching the current speed is highlighted.
6. Keyboard shortcuts: Space starts or pauses, the right arrow steps one event (Shift: ten), F fits the chart, 1 to 5 switch views; on the Pairs and Stat-arb views the first three act on their own books (the arrow runs them on by a unit of time), and C centers their panes. They are off while typing in a field.
7. Template rendering runs on worker threads; panels are pushed at most five times a second while running; the chart receives every bar since its last push.

### E. Order Entry
1. Limit and market orders. The Bid, Mid and Ask quick-price buttons follow the book (Mid rounds down for a buy, up for a sell).
2. Every order reports what happened under the button: resting as order #id, filled at what prices (with the average when it walked the book), what rests, or nothing filled. `/api/order/limit` and `/api/order/market` return that line as HTML (status 200); rejected input gets a message too.
3. The user's resting orders stay until they fill or the user cancels them. The models' cancellations stand for other traders withdrawing their own orders, so `LOBManager.step` skips one aimed at a manual order (counted as a step without an event). The models choose the order to cancel uniformly from the book, so this is exact thinning: their own orders keep their cancellation rate.
4. Manual market orders carry price protection, as on CME Globex: they walk the book at most N ticks (default 10, from 0 to 1,000, set in the form) past the best opposite price on arrival, and whatever is left rests there as the user's limit order, the paper's rule for an order that runs out of matches (Gould et al. p. 1712). Protection 0 fills only at the best price, like a market-to-limit order; with no opposite side nothing is placed. The result line says what was filled and what rests. The models' own market orders are unchanged: what they cannot fill is discarded.
5. Iceberg limit orders: an optional "show at most (lots)" field. The order book shows only the peak, marked "you 10 +90 hidden"; My Active Orders shows the reserve.
6. An order that trades is compared with the session VWAP from just before it: "Your average was 1.0% below the session VWAP of 101.00."
7. Fills of the user's resting orders are announced: a notice in each open tab ("Your buy order #583 filled 5 lots at 100, 3 left.") and the latest five under My Active Orders.
8. Split orders (TWAP): a market order can go out as N market orders, one every T units of simulation time. Each slice is protected; what it cannot fill rolls into the next. At the start the manager records what one market order for the whole size would have done (`sweep_estimate`); the summary compares the split's average with it and with the market's VWAP over the period. Slices wait while the simulation is paused; Stop ends a split.
9. **Cancel all** in My Active Orders cancels every resting order of the user's (`POST /api/order/cancel_all`, which can also take a side or a price) and leaves the models' alone.

---

### F. Stylized Facts (this run)
1. **What it measures**: notebook 05's seven facts on the session's own run: heavy tails (excess kurtosis of mid-price returns over 1,000 events, bar 1), volatility clustering (rank ACF of |r| at lag 1, bar 0.1), long memory in order signs (ACF at lag 100), bid-ask bounce (lag-1 ACF of trade-price changes), a hump-shaped depth profile, concave impact (log-log slope of virtual impact, bar 0.95) and power-law relative prices (a property of each model, shown as such).
2. **Same sampling as the notebook**: after 5,000 warm-up steps, the mid-price every 1,000 events with both sides quoted, the sign of every limit and market order, the last fill price of every aggressive order, and a book snapshot every 400 steps. A test replays a `Simulation` through the recorder and requires identical samples and statistics.
3. **Uncertainty from one run**: a moving-block bootstrap for the return statistics and batch means for signs, trades and snapshots, graded with the notebook's rule (3 SE "Yes", 2-3 SE "Weak evidence"). These errors are approximate and too small for series with long memory, so every reading is labeled "this run"; the notebook uses 32 independent seeds.
4. **Collect 400k / 2M**: runs that many steps as fast as possible (about 2 s and 10 s), in chunks so the server stays responsive; only the panel updates meanwhile, and the chart reloads afterwards. 400k events (one notebook run) often leave clustering open; 2M usually settle it.
5. **A new sample** starts when the model, its parameters, the engine or the book changes. Memory per session is capped (the most recent 500k signs, 100k trades, 2,500 snapshots).
6. **Notebook 05's values**: the parameter panel can fill in the values notebook 05 runs each model with (a test reads them from the notebook). The dashboard's defaults trade far more (about 29 lots per time unit against 1 for the notebook's ZI rates), which keeps the page lively but changes the facts: with the default rates, the Hawkes model's clustering sits on the bar (0.12 against 0.1 after 2M events), while with the notebook's values a 2M-event run gives the notebook's verdict on all seven facts.

### G. Order Flow Imbalance (this run)
1. From the same sample as the stylized facts, OFI (Cont, Kukanov and Stoikov 2014) is summed over intervals of 100 events: lots added at the bid or taken from the ask count as buying pressure.
2. A card plots the mid-price change against OFI for the latest 400 intervals, with the fitted line, the slope in ticks per lot, R2, the depth at the best quotes and the latest interval. On the default ZI model after 400k events: slope 0.031, R2 0.33, depth 29.7 lots. A test requires the recorder's OFI and price changes to equal `lob.metrics.order_flow_imbalance` on a `Simulation` replay.
3. Unlike the top-5 depth imbalance in the metrics strip, which one large resting order can dominate, OFI measures what changed at the best quotes.

### H. Export
1. `GET /api/export/{trades|bars}.{csv|parquet}`: every fill (the most recent 100,000; time, price, size, aggressor, maker and taker IDs, whether it was the user's order), or every bar at the current interval with its volume split and VWAP. Links on the trades card and under the volume chart.
2. Parquet needs `pyarrow` (the optional `[parquet]` extra, included in `[dev]`); without it the links are hidden and the endpoint answers 501.

### I. Views, Layout, Text Size and Themes
1. **Five views**, picked from tabs in the header: Trade (chart with its footprint, order entry, your orders, the order book and the tape, then the microstructure studies, the strategies and the trigger rules across the full width, 3T), Analyze (the heatmap, order flow imbalance and stylized facts), Learn (lessons and the reading guide), Pairs (two books of their own and pairs traders, 3Q.5) and Stat-arb (many books of their own and traders of many relations, 3R.6). The simulation controls and the top of the book stay in view on the first three; the last two, each a market of its own with its own controls, hide them. The view is kept in the address (`#analyze`) and in this browser; keys 1 to 5 switch, and the arrow keys move along the tab list.
2. **Full width, in columns**: the Trade view takes as many columns as the window has room for: one on a phone; from 1024 px two (the chart over order entry | the book over the tape); from 1280 px three (order entry and your orders | the chart | the book and the tape); from 1600 px four, the tape in a column of its own. Wrappers dissolve (`display: contents`) when their children become columns.
3. **Text size**: Small (the default), Medium or Large in the header sets the root font size to 75%, 87.5% or 100% of the browser's own. Every size on the page is in rem, so this one value scales everything, as zooming would; the charts' text follows (11 px at Large). Large is the original look.
4. **Themes**: Midnight (the original), Daylight (light, for projectors and screenshots) and Terminal (black, amber accents, JetBrains Mono), and a color-blind-safe palette that shows buying in blue and selling in orange. Every shade of the color families the templates use is a CSS variable (`--c-<family>-<shade>`, an "R G B" triplet so opacity modifiers work), generated by `scripts/build_themes.py`; a theme is one attribute on `<html>` (`data-theme`, `data-palette`). Script-drawn colors (the charts, the heatmap) read the variables through `themeColor()` and redraw on a change. All four keep text and buy/sell colors at 4.5:1 contrast or more. Text size and theme are kept in this browser and applied before the page paints.

### J. Order-Book Heatmap (Analyze)
1. The visible depth at each price near the mid over simulation time, one column per unit of time (the most recent 600; see `HeatColumn` above), with the trades as circles and the mid-price as a line. Brightness grows with depth up to twice the median in view, then moves away from the background (toward white, or black on Daylight), so a wall stands out as a bright band the price runs into; an iceberg is a level that keeps trading while what shows of it stays small. **Reveal hidden** outlines the iceberg reserves, which the visible depth leaves out; hovering gives the numbers.
2. The page loads the history from `/api/heatmap` when the view is first shown, and again when the heatmap starts over or the stream reconnects; the stream's `heatmap` events bring the new columns (throttled like the panels). Taking columns costs about 0.07 microseconds per event.

### K. Guided Lessons (Learn)
1. Seven lessons of four to six steps: price impact (a market order walking the book), walls (a large resting bid holding the price up), hidden liquidity (an iceberg trading more than the book shows), order flow imbalance (across two models), splitting a large order (TWAP against one sweep and the VWAP), making a market (a market maker's edge, markout and adverse selection, and how requoting more often costs queue priority), and trading on a signal (momentum, mean reversion and queue imbalance, crossing the spread and then resting at the best quote).
2. A lesson runs in a dock in the bottom-left corner that stays across views. Each step explains something, with numbers read from the current book; **Do it** sends the same requests the page's own controls do, **Show me** switches view and outlines the part of the page the step is about (an overlay that follows the element, so pushes that replace it do not lose the outline), and steps that can check their result mark Next once done. The lesson, the step and what the steps noted are kept in this browser, so a reload resumes; finished lessons are marked done. The lessons are written in `app/static/js/lob-lessons.js`.

---

### L. Your Position and P&L
1. A Your P&L card under My Active Orders and a card in the metrics strip (so in every view): position, its average price, the realized P&L (from selling out of a position at more or less than its average price) and the unrealized part marked to the mid, fills, and the edge against the mid of the user's resting and aggressive fills. Money is in ticks times lots (one lot gaining one tick is 1); there are no fees.
2. A P&L line under the volume chart, shown once the user has traded: cash plus position at each bar's close, exact however a bar's fills were ordered. It follows the price chart and never leads (it is made hidden, and a chart without a width has no range to pass on); the three charts' price axes take the widest one's width, so their bars line up.
3. My Active Orders shows each resting order's place in its queue: the lots and orders ahead of it under price-time priority, or its share of the level under pro-rata matching.

### M. Strategies
1. A Strategies card across the bottom of the Trade view starts quantitative strategies of the user's, up to twelve at once, each trading with an account of its own as the simulation runs. Making markets: **market making** (a bid and an ask a set number of ticks from the mid, leaning against its inventory and never across the spread; at its inventory limit it quotes only the side that reduces it), **Avellaneda-Stoikov** (2008, stationary form: around a reservation price of the mid less position x risk aversion x variance x horizon, a spread of risk aversion x variance x horizon + (2/risk aversion) ln(1 + risk aversion/k), the variance per unit of time from the mid's changes over a window) and **penny jumping** (a tick ahead of the best bid and ask when the spread allows, joining them otherwise, not stepping ahead on the side that would add to its position, and keeping its orders while they are where it wants them). Trading on a signal: **momentum** (long while a fast exponential average of the mid is above a slow one by a threshold, short while below, flat once they cross), **mean reversion** (short when the mid is a number of standard deviations above its average over a window, long when as far below, flat back near it), **breakout** (long above the highest mid of its window, short below the lowest, flat back past the middle), **queue imbalance** (long once the best bid's queue outweighs the ask's by a threshold, short in the opposite case, flat when it tips back) and **order flow imbalance** (the OFI between decisions summed over a window, in average queue lengths at the best quotes; long beyond a threshold, short below minus it, flat when it tips past zero). And two benchmarks: **random**, which now and then takes a random position, and the **quote joiner**, the null for making markets, which rests at the best bid and ask and nothing else (3P). The signal strategies trade by crossing the spread, a protected market order, or by resting at the best quote. Their orders show by tag in both order-book views.
2. The leaderboard ranks them, and the user, by P&L: a sparkline of each one's P&L, what it holds, the lots it traded, its edge against the mid at each fill, the markout 100 events later, adverse selection (the difference), its signal now and the position it wants, and Stop (its orders withdrawn, what it holds kept and marked to the mid), Resume and Remove. The board is pushed with the panels, so it holds still while the pointer or the keyboard is on a row's buttons; what a click did shows in its row at once.
3. The market-making strategy replays the earlier quoting bot fill for fill (checked on three seeds). Checked over 8 seeds of 60,000 events in the ZI model: requoting every 15 events instead of 58 raised its adverse selection from 0.20 ± 0.05 to 0.40 ± 0.04 ticks a lot, on 262 instead of 472 lots (each new quote joins the back of its queue). An earlier comparison across models, which suggested that long-memory signs raise adverse selection, did not survive matching the requote interval in events.
4. The defaults came from runs of 20,000 to 30,000 events on three seeds in two or three models. Queue imbalance first went flat whenever the imbalance came back within its threshold and traded 10 lots: in 30,000 events of the ZI model it traded about 7,000 lots and lost about 8,800 ticks (the mean of three seeds). Waiting until the balance tips, with 5 lots, cut the loss about fivefold, there and in Mike & Farmer (2008). Its signal does predict the next move (its markout beat its edge by 0.4 to 0.9 ticks a lot), but by less than crossing the spread costs. In the simplified Mike & Farmer model, whose best queues hold about 900 lots (about 20 in the ZI and 2008 models), resting orders almost never fill.
5. On their defaults, in 20,000 events of each of three models (ZI, Mike & Farmer 2008, Parlour; three seeds each), Avellaneda-Stoikov and penny jumping made money in all nine runs, and breakout, order flow imbalance and the random trader lost in all nine: their fills cost about half the spread or more (edge -0.8 to -2.0 ticks a lot) and the price did not make it back.

### N. Recording and Playback
1. **Record**, next to Reset Book, keeps the session from that moment: the book as it stands, then every order and cancellation (the model's, the user's and the strategies'), until it is stopped, the book is reset, or it reaches 500,000 events. The status badge shows REC and the count; a session keeps three recordings (Analyze, Recordings), each downloadable as an `.npz` file and uploadable again.
2. **Play back** makes the session the recording's: its book, the user's orders and account, its strategies (replayed, following the tape), paused at the start. The tape takes the model's place, so Play, Pause, Step, the speed and the keyboard work as usual (a step is an order or a cancellation), and a playback bar shows where it is, with a timeline to drag; Reset rewinds. The engines are deterministic, so a playback reproduces the session exactly: the book order by order, every fill, the user's and every strategy's account and markouts, the clock (checked from the middle of a run with an iceberg, a market order, a split order, a move, Cancel all, strategies of every kind, one stopped and resumed and one removed, an engine toggle and a model change, from memory and through a file).
3. The user can trade, and start strategies, during a playback: they meet the recorded order flow, which does not react to them, so the market then goes its own way (cancellations of orders that are gone are skipped). Starting strategies on a playback before pressing Play runs them on the same market from the same start. **Exit playback** carries on live from there with the model selected (its state starts fresh, and the random generator restarts from the seed).
4. Recording adds about 12% to the time per event; a playback replays about 200,000 events a second, so seeking back (the tape again from its start) takes at most about 2.5 s. A 500,000-event file is about 5 MB. A range input rounds the time it gives (Chrome to 15 digits), so a seek counts events a hair later as reached.

### O. Fitting a Strategy over a Training Period
1. **Fit it over a training period**, in the Strategies card, tries the combinations of the values given for the chosen strategy's settings (each spec proposes some for its main ones; at most 8 values a setting, and combinations that contradict each other are left out) on one training period: all of them, or, when there are more than a fit tries (48, and fewer over a long period, to stay within 1,500,000 candidate events), a random sample of them drawn from the seed. That is a random search, which with many settings tends to do about as well as the whole grid (Bergstra and Bengio 2012); the card counts the combinations as settings are ticked, and the result says when it sampled. Ticking a fourth of mean reversion's settings used to be refused (81 combinations, more than 48); it now tries 48 of them. The candidates are ranked by **P&L** at the end, marked to the mid, or by **P&L per unit of risk**: the mean change of its P&L per unit of time over the standard deviation of those changes. The candidates show best first (the top ten), each with its P&L, lots, edge and markout there, to start (`start_fitted`) or to put in the form.
2. The training period is a tape, as a recording is: either a recording, or a **fresh run** of the selected model from the session as it stands (its book, orders, accounts and strategies, which take part), for a number of events, with random numbers of its own from a seed. The fresh run does not use the session's own generator, so the market a fitted strategy then meets live is another draw, not the one it was fitted on (checked: the training run's events differ from the session's next ones). Every candidate replays the same tape from its start, trading for itself on it; as in a backtest on historical data, the order flow does not react to it.
3. A started candidate keeps its training record (which, of how many, by what, its P&L in training), shown in its row on the leaderboard beside its P&L since it started: out of sample. The best of many candidates on one period is likely to have been lucky there, so its training P&L overstates what to expect; fitting the random trader's seed shows this (its candidates differ only by luck).
4. A fit's results equal running each candidate by hand on the same tape (checked for a fresh run and for a recording). It runs in the event loop, a chunk at a time. Fitting momentum's three settings (27 combinations) over a 10,000-event fresh run takes about 1.3 s; all seven of mean reversion's (30 of its 1,458 combinations) over 50,000 events, the most work a fit does, took 8.8 s. A grid of more than 20,000 combinations is sampled without counting its valid ones.

### P. Is the Order Flow Paying? The Regime and the Null
1. In the dashboard's models, providing liquidity pays without skill, and the leaderboard showed it as skill: a quote joiner, which rests at the best bid and ask and nothing else, made money in all twelve runs of four models (+632 to +10,790 over 30,000 events), and the model's own resting orders earn +0.8 to +1.5 ticks a lot 100 events after they fill (60,000 events, two seeds). The ZI flow sends market orders at a fixed rate whatever the spread, and they carry no information, so whoever rests at the best quote collects what they pay.
2. **The regime line** above the leaderboard says so as it happens: whether the flow pays resting orders (amber when the model's resting orders earn more than 0.1 tick a lot, green within 0.1 of even, red below), the spread against the mid's move per trade, and what the model's resting and market orders, and a quote joiner when one runs, earn a lot. The ratio is Wyart et al.'s (2008): the average spread just before each trade over the root mean square of the mid's change from just before one trade to just before the next (`regime`). They find 1.2 to 1.6 in real small-tick markets (c = 1.58 on 68 Paris stocks in 2002, R² 0.96; 1.32 on 155 NYSE stocks in 2005; 1.17 to 1.53 on index futures), where no strategy, providing or taking, is more than marginally profitable; the argument does not apply directly to large-tick markets, where the tick can hold the spread wider. Here, with no strategies: ZI 3.2, Hawkes 3.0, Mike & Farmer (2008) 3.7, Parlour 2.7 (60,000 events, two seeds), and more with quoting strategies in the book (about 5 in ZI), which add depth at the best and steady the mid. The line's averages weigh the last 2,000 trades or so (checked against plain averages).
3. **Vs null**, on each row, is its markout a lot less that of a strategy with no skill on the same side, at the same time: for taking fills, the model's own market orders (which know nothing: the random trader comes out within 0.2 tick of them in three models, two seeds each); for resting fills, the quote joiner's, while one runs (**Add the null** puts one on the board). The model's own resting orders are no null for resting: those that improved the spread earn +0.1 to +0.2 ticks a lot, those that joined the best +0.6 to +1.5, and those placed behind it, which fill only once the price comes to them, +0.9 to +2.5, while the joiner earns +0.3 to +0.9 (40,000 events, four models, two seeds); queue position is not the reason. Measured so in three models and two seeds each: penny jumping comes out level with the joiner (-0.06 to 0.00), Avellaneda-Stoikov above it (+0.15 to +0.37, on fewer lots), momentum below the market orders (-0.01 to -0.48). Fills that come while no joiner runs are not held against one, and a row's tooltip says over how many of its lots it is.
4. The nulls' state is part of a recording's starting state, so a playback reproduces Vs null too; recordings saved before it still load (their markouts lack which side rested, and their accounts the null's sums).

### Q. Two Books for Pairs Trading (`lob/pairs.py`, `lob/copulas.py`, notebooks 10 and 11)
1. **The model.** Each book's traders place orders around its fair value (`AnchoredZIModel`), so it reprices as the fair value moves; the fair values share a common random walk, and each has a part of its own that order flow moves (impact 0.05 tick a lot) and that, with a half-life, decays back: the gap mean-reverts with that half-life. Without one, the gap wanders and impact is permanent; an `Arbitrageur` (2 ticks, 5 lots) then holds the books together (gap sd about 1.5 to 2.4 ticks instead of 12 to 50). With a half-life of 50 and an own volatility of 0.8, each book stays within about 1.7 ticks of its fair value, and the gap of the mids follows the true gap (correlation 0.94), best 4 time units late. The fair values stop at `FLOOR`, 10 ticks (3Q.12).
2. **What the books show.** Engle-Granger finds the reverting books cointegrated in 8 of 8 seeds, the wandering ones in 0 of 8, and with the arbitrageur in 8 of 8. Least squares underestimates the hedge ratio on short samples (median 0.72 over 4,000 time units, against a true 1; 0.92 over 16,000, 0.98 over 64,000): errors in A's price attenuate it. The half-life from the gap of the mids is about twice the true one at short lags (106 against 50 at lag 1), because the books lag their fair values (checked: the tracking error is persistent, lag-1 autocorrelation 0.85).
3. **The trade.** `PairsTrader` (z-score over 400 decisions, entry ±2, flat at the mean, 5 lots a leg, market orders) makes +454 over 20,000 time units (95% interval +339 to +568, 8 of 8 seeds), after crossing costs of 79% of what it earns before them (1.23 ticks a lot). It pays only with a long window and a wide gap: with a window of 400, at gap sds of 6.8 and 10.2 ticks (4 of 4 seeds each) but not 3.4; with a window of 100, never (it enters three times as often). Beside the arbitrageur it loses (-422 a run, 0 of 4) though it would earn +2,349 before costs: the arbitrageur takes the deviations first.
4. **The figures.** `quant_plots` reads daily weights and returns with linear costs, so the record has one row per decision (a card day is one time unit, 252 a card year): what the trader held after each decision, valued at the mids just before its orders, which makes its P&L before costs exactly the fills' (valued after its orders, it would also charge the trader its own impact). Its cost per unit of the record's turnover is set so it pays, in all, what crossing the spreads cost (the turnover also counts weights drifting with prices). Both cards' footnotes print the fills' P&L beside the record's: they agree. `scorecard` returns a Figure that pyplot does not know about, so the notebook displays it.
5. **The Pairs view** (key 4; `app/pairs.py`, `static/js/lob-pairs.js`) runs the same model in the dashboard, as a market of its own beside the Trade view's: its own clock, its speed (1 to 500 units of time a second, about 39 events a unit) and its own event stream. Its settings are the fair values' (whether the gap mean-reverts, the half-life, each book's own and the common volatility, impact, B's beta), an arbitrageur (its threshold and lots) and the seed; Apply starts the books over, running on if they were. A drifting beta and a stressed regime are there too (3Q.6). Its level is 1,000 for A and beta times that for B, and its common volatility 1 by default, against notebook 10's 100 and 0.3: at 0.3 the books' returns over a unit of time correlate 0.12, which is no pair. Up to six traders run in it, of the kinds in `TRADER_KINDS`, each with its own settings. A new one can learn from the chart first: it takes in the chart's mids, one every interval rounded to whole units, as far as it can use them (`learn`), so it trades at once instead of watching for a whole window first.
   - **The chart**: five panes on one time axis, one point a unit of time (the last 3,000): both mids and fair values; the gap from the mids and from the fair values, with the chosen trader's levels and markers where its target changed; its signal against its levels (a z-score, or a copula trader's two flags); its hedge against the true beta; and each trader's P&L. Levels are moved into the chart's terms at each point: the center is where the gap would be with the trader's own deviation at 0, so the gap crosses them where its z-score crosses them, whatever its hedge (a test checks a hedge of 1.2 against a beta of 1.5). A mark sits on the first point taken after the decision.
   - **The chart's controls**, as under the Trade chart: a horizontal zoom in points shown (steppers, a logarithmic slider, presets and a badge), Fit (every point, and every pane centered), Center (back to fitting what is shown, also after dragging a price axis), Now (to the newest point, following it), and a readout at the crosshair, which shows in every pane.
