With several panes there is more: each has its own vertical zoom, about the middle of what it shows, which stays centered as the lines move, unlike an axis stretched by hand (`scaled`: each line's `autoscaleInfoProvider` reports its range and returns the same one for all), panes can be hidden (the time axis stays on the lowest), and heights are S, M or L. Panes and height are kept in this browser.
   - **The board**: each trader's P&L at the mids; before costs, as if every fill had been at the mid just before it; what crossing cost it, in all and a lot; its lots, entries, positions and z. Stop keeps what it holds; Resume and Remove are there too. The arbitrageur's row is marked at mids its own trades moved. Reset and new settings start every trader again from flat; a stopped one stays stopped. The board's pushes wait while a button is under the pointer, as on the leaderboard.
   - **The chart's time axis** is mostly left to Lightweight Charts, which follows the newest point while that is in view and leaves a range the user dragged back where it is (`rightBarStaysOnScroll` makes a zoom keep the right edge, so a zoom does not stop it following; Fit does start it again). Three things it does not do are in `lob-pairs.js`. It keeps the room to the right of the newest point as points come, so a chart framed for 600 points with one in it would push every new point off to the left: until the frame is full, `shiftVisibleRangeOnNewBar` is off (`frame`, `pointsAdded`). The three panes are separate charts, and a range set on one takes effect only when it paints, so one set on every batch, or passed around among them, undoes the user's last move (zooming failed three times in four): only the chart under the user's hand passes its range on (`leader`), and panes that fall behind a batch catch up afterwards. And when the chart drops its oldest points, a view of them moves to the oldest left (`afterDropping`). Not `fixLeftEdge`: a chart pinned at its first point gains a point of its own with each new one, which shook it 18 times a second while it filled. **Setting every line at once empties them first** (`setAll` in `lob-panes.js`). In the library, `setData` on a chart's only line clears its map of points, and if the times have not changed it keeps its old sorted list, now pointing at dead objects. Once another line has joined and points have been dropped, the rows are out of order, and every paint throws "Value is null" and the chart stops. A P&L pane has only its zero line until the first trader starts, and that start sets every line again, so a first trader started on a long history froze the chart at the next drop, minutes later at the default speed (found on the Stat-arb view, and also in the Pairs view as committed).
   - **The clock** follows the wall clock: speed × the time since the last frame, at most five frames' worth, to a running target, so a frame's overshoot comes off the next. Frames that slept 50 ms on top of their work had run it about 10% slow. Six traders at 500 units a second keep up (497 a second, the page at 60 frames a second, a request answered in 2 ms).
6. **What the two options are for.** With a constant beta a dynamic hedge has nothing to track, and with a Gaussian common factor a copula has no shape to find. `beta_sd` makes beta an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process (`dF_B = beta_t dX + dU_B`, so a change of beta does not move B by itself); `stress_share` gives the common factor a stressed regime. Both are off by default, and with them off a seeded run is unchanged, number for number (checked against the code before the change, over every column, and in a test).
7. **The Kalman trader** (Chan 2013): the filter regresses B's mid on A's with a slope and level that may drift, started by least squares over the first window. `tests/test_pairs.py` holds it to exact least squares with no state noise, the local level's steady gain, and the Gaussian posterior of the whole path. It differs from Chan's in three ways. The regressor is measured from a pivot near the prices (`move_pivot`, which changes the coordinates and not the line). One setting, a memory M, sets the state noise at q = r / M², the filter's time constant. And z is the error over a running average of its size, not the filter's own variance: with a persistent gap that gave z-scores with a standard deviation near 2, so an entry of 2 was really 1.
8. **What notebook 11 finds about it.** The filter behaves like rolling least squares over about twice its memory (root mean square errors of 0.322 and 0.325 with a common volatility of 2). Two things limit any such estimate: A's own noise attenuates it, and the gap, the regression's noise, is persistent, leaving about W / 144 independent observations in a window of W. Those two predict least squares' errors within about a quarter (0.234, 0.119 and 0.060 against 0.247, 0.144 and 0.081). Total least squares does not help: about the same where it is stable, and an error above 100 where it is not. Like for like (the same filter with the slope frozen, the same span, 8 seeds, 17,500 units): with beta fixed, learning the hedge costs money (+655 against +1,055); with beta drifting by 0.3 and a common volatility of 1 there is nothing to win, as even a perfect hedge makes +941 against a fixed hedge's +915 (the filter +545); with beta drifting by 0.5 and a common volatility of 2, costs decide it (+2,036 before costs and +5 after, against +456 and +16), and in books four times as deep the filter pays (+702, 8 of 8 seeds, against +183, 4 of 8, under a ceiling of +1,264), though a z-score with a window of 2,000, at +465 (se 201), is not clearly behind it. Following the hedge while holding adds nothing measurable.
9. **The copula trader** follows Rad, Low and Faff (2016), read in the original: m = h - 0.5 for each book, added up into flags from 0 each trading period; open when one flag is above D and the other below -D; unwind when both have returned to zero (not necessarily at once: this reading is ours). Returns are over 5 units, the window 400 decisions, the period 200, margins empirical. They set D = 0.5 "arbitrarily"; here it trades too much in three of four markets, and the dashboard's default is 1 (a level chosen by looking at these runs). **What notebook 11 finds.** The chain from the market to the family works: Student t in 8 of 8 seeds under stress both ways (AIC 140 below the Gaussian), Clayton in 8 of 8 under falling stress (lower tail 0.35, upper 0), and near-ties with no regimes. Yet the family hardly matters to the P&L: chosen by AIC it adds +62 a run to a Gaussian always (se 54), against +187 to no dependence at all (se 43). The flags drift: with no fresh start their half-life is about 3,300 units against the gap's 73, mostly because a window's margins are off center, so each step has a small mean (with margins centered on the whole run, about 480), and the rest is the ranks' nonlinearity. Within a period they follow the gap's change since the fresh start (correlation 0.92), so they say nothing of where its mean is, which is the likeliest reason a z-score with a window of 2,000 beats it (+1,085 against +407, +1,118 against +327, +721 against +301), the order Rad et al. find (38 against 5 basis points a month after costs). Except in falling stress, where they come level (+248, se 157, against +191 at D = 1 and +300 at D = 2): there, in 10% of the time, the z-score does 44% of its lots, at 6.6 ticks a lot against 1.6 in calm, and the copula 7%, as ranks cap the large moves.
10. **Fitting a trader** (the panel *Fit it over training runs*; `TrainedMarket.fit_trader` in `app/training.py`, which the Stat-arb view shares, 3R.6). The Trade view's fit replays a tape the candidate cannot move. Here a trader's orders move the books, and that is part of what it pays, so each candidate trades alone in fresh markets from the view's settings (`training_run`, `lob.pairs.score_trader`). The seeds (`training_seed`) are above any the seed setting allows, so a training run is never the live market's draw. Every run gets the longest warm-up among the settings tried (`warm_up`), so all are measured over the same span of a market of the same age. `fit_plan` keeps a fit within `MAX_FIT_CANDIDATES` and `MAX_FIT_UNITS` (candidates times runs times time) by a random sample from the seed, and always includes the user's own settings, first if they are not in the grid (`plan_fit`, whose budget is the view's). The default fits take 9 to 17 s here on 15 workers at about 2,500 units a second each; a machine with fewer cores takes proportionally longer. The runs go to a spawned process pool made for the fit (`_fit_pool`, at lower priority, one fit at a time on a server, whichever view, the slot taken only once the pool is made), holding no lock, so the market on the chart runs on untouched; a test checks its random state. Progress goes out in `pairs_state` and the status panel, to every tab, with a Cancel. A Cancel, a failed run or the session's end stops the runs that have begun, with their processes (`_stop_workers`), and a worker ends when the server does, killed or not (`_end_with_server`), so neither leaves cores busy or idle workers behind. The results come by the stream as `pairs_fit`, apart from the panels so that a push never takes their buttons, and also on connect, so a reload brings them back. `rank_candidates` gives each candidate's mean, its standard error, and its difference from the user's settings seed by seed with that difference's standard error; `TrainingFit.verdict` says whether the best beats them by `CLEARLY` (2) standard errors. `start_fitted` starts a candidate, keeping its training result on its row beside its P&L a unit of time here since it had a signal (`trades_from`), with a word if the market's settings have changed since (`same_market`, the seed aside). A live rate over a short time is mostly noise: the row's training standard error is there to remind.
11. **What notebook 11's section 6 finds about fitting** (five fits at the panel's defaults, tested on 16 seeds none of the settings was chosen on, over 17,500 units; P&L from the starting settings, then taking the best in training always, then only when clearly better): the z-score +587, +1,255 and +1,255 with a common volatility of 1, +278, +856 and +856 under stress; the Kalman trader +682, +936 and +784, and +316, +553 and +463; the copula +277, +260 and +272, and +307, +225 and +296. So the z-score has the most to gain (its window of 400 is short here; fitted it is first of the three in both markets), the Kalman trader some, and the copula nothing: its fits choose settings all over the grid. *Clearly better* was called in 18 of the 30 fits and right on the fresh seeds in 16 (wrong twice, both for the copula). *Not clearly better* does not mean no better: for the Kalman trader it passed up a real gain in half its fits. The lengths come from a wider run kept out of the notebook, of 5,000 or 10,000 units with 1, 3 or 5 runs, tested on seeds 1 to 8: three runs of 10,000 did about as well as five of them in three fifths of the time, and better than three of 5,000 (for the Kalman trader +914 against +704, and +381 against +298). A t-test's threshold in place of two standard errors (4.3 at three runs) moved in only 7 of 36 fits, giving up most of the z-score's gain (+922 and +457 against +1,278 and +1,041). **Correcting an earlier assumption:** two traders on a seed share a market only until the first trade, which changes the books, so the rates, so what every later random number is used for. From then on their common factors' changes correlate about zero, and their P&Ls on a seed correlate from about 0 to 0.38, the Kalman trader's most. So comparing by seed takes out little of the luck, here and in notebook 11's section 5; the standard errors are measured from the differences, so they stay right, and about as large as with different seeds. A random stream of their own for the fair values might sharpen these comparisons; that is neither built nor tested. The same care showed the copula's default in a better light than it deserves: chosen on seeds 1 to 8, it was first of 15 there, and fourth with a common volatility of 1 (first under stress, among near equals) on 16 fresh ones.
12. **The floor.** A user who kept the Pairs tab running for a long time saw both prices go negative. The common factor is a random walk in ticks, so from 1,000 at a common volatility of 1 a typical path reaches zero after about a million units of time, roughly half an hour at top speed; the level was never "far from the floor" except over short runs. The fair values went on down (to -160 in a reproduction), while orders stop at one tick (`max(1, ...)` in `AnchoredZIModel`), so each book collapsed to one price and one side: no mid for up to 75% of the time, a meaningless gap, and a trader still paying to cross. So `FairValues` now holds both at or above `floor` (`FLOOR`, 10 ticks: the lowest at which orders up to 8 ticks from it all stay above one tick). What it takes is a push of the common factor at the end of `advance`, just as far as needed, moving A by the push and B by beta times it; `pushed` keeps the total, and the status line shows it once it is above 0. A trade's impact can dip a fair value below the floor by a fraction of a tick until the next event's `advance`, so orders never see it. Pushing the common factor rather than a book leaves the gap alone, in every setting, because the gap nets the common factor out; no random number is drawn, so a seeded run that never reaches the floor is unchanged, number for number (checked at level 60 against level 3,000, and on 40 runs in notebook 11's markets, all identical, whose lowest fair value was 2,143). What it does change is the common factor near the floor, which is no longer a martingale there. Checked in the market that collapsed: no mid missing, and the same gap sd (6.3 to 6.9 against 6.0 to 7.3), spread (1.91 to 1.94 against 1.90 to 1.92) and cost a lot (1.31 to 1.41 against 1.28 to 1.41) as far from it. **Notebook 10 had met the same flaw:** starting from 100, its longer and wandering runs had fair values down to -158, so its stored numbers changed when it was run again: beside the arbitrageur -396 to -422 (and +2,284 to +2,349 before costs), the lone trader in wandering books -1,046 to -1,232 with a window of 400 and -3,354 to -3,347 with 100, and the smallest gap with a window of 400 -196 to -204. Its main result (+454), its figures and its conclusions did not. Those rows against a market started at 3,000, which never reaches the floor, say the floor gives the right answer: -409, -1,196, -3,471 and -196 (standard errors 29 to 110). The Trade view's models have no fair value and floor their orders at one tick, so this does not arise there.

### R. Many Books and Stat-Arb (`lob/statarb.py`, `app/statarb.py`, notebook 12)
1. **The model.** As in notebook 10, each book's traders place orders around its fair value. The fair values share k common factors, random walks, through a table of loadings B, and each has a part of its own, mean-reverting and moved by order flow: F_i = level_i + sum_j B_ij X_j + U_i. A portfolio w with w'B = 0 leaves the factors out, so w'F mean-reverts: the books have n - rank(B) independent relations, and a pair is truly cointegrated only if its two rows of B are parallel. Two tables of twelve books and nine relations each: `sector_loadings(3, 4)`, a market factor and one for each sector, with 18 true pairs of 66; and `mixed_loadings(12, 3, seed=3)`, with no true pair and every relation taking at least four books. Loadings can drift around their start (`loading_sd`, with a half-life of 2,000), and then no fixed relation stays one. The floor is 3Q.12's: a push of the first factor, just as far as needed, which no relation sees and which draws no random number.
2. **A random stream of their own.** 3Q.11 left this neither built nor tested. The factors and the shocks to the own parts come on a grid of time (0.2) from `default_rng([seed, 1])`, and the order flow from `[seed, 2]`, so a seed gives the same factors and shocks whatever trades. It helps, modestly: the correlation across seeds of one relation's P&L under two settings goes from -0.03 to +0.41 with two books and +0.32 with six, and the standard deviation of their difference falls to 0.76 and 0.82 of what different seeds give, worth about one and a half times as many runs. No more, because the flow's random numbers mean different things once a trade has changed the books. The same trader earns the same here as in `lob/pairs.py`, within the noise.
3. **The traders.** `RelationTrader` trades the z-score of each of several portfolios (short above `entry`, long below minus it, flat at `exit`), its lots the size times their sum, rounded (its target: with a rate below 1 or a band it goes toward them, 3S.7). Its relations are fixed or chosen from the last `formation` decisions' mids by `johansen_relations`, `best_baskets` (the largest trace statistics among all baskets of `legs` books), `pairs_by_distance` (Gatev et al. 2006) or `pairs_by_pvalue`, and chosen again every `refit`, when a relation on the same books keeps its state and the rest close. `ResidualTrader` is Avellaneda and Lee (2010), read in the original: PCA on the correlation matrix of the moves, each eigenvector over the books' volatilities; each book regressed on those portfolios over a window; the residuals summed and fitted by a lag-1 regression; s = (X - m) / sigma_eq, with m centered across the books; a fit rejected unless b is below exp(-2 / window), their limit of half the window; open beyond 1.25, close a short below 0.75 and a long above -0.5. Hedges stay as they were on entry. `CopulaPortfolio` runs `lob.pairs.CopulaTrader` on each of many pairs chosen by distance, as Rad et al. do for all their methods, with a book allowed in several. `score_portfolio` is `lob.pairs.score_trader` with what the trader held and how much of the factors.
4. **What the tests see** (twelve runs of each market, seeds 101 to 112). Johansen finds almost none of the nine relations over 2,000 units (0.2 to 0.3), 6.2 to 6.7 over 5,000, and all nine over 10,000, which is why traders choose from 10,000. Engle-Granger over 20,000 calls every true pair cointegrated, but it cannot tell one from a pair that nearly is: in the mixed market, where no pair is, it calls 91% of those with under 0.3 of a factor left, 46% with 0.3 to 0.7 and 8% with more; a unit-root test is known to reject too often when a small random walk sits beside a large stationary part (Schwert 1989). Across sectors its 10% comes in bunches: in 4 of 12 runs about 14 of the 48 pairs at once.
5. **What notebook 12 finds.** Every trader alone, the same 20,000 units after the same warm-up, costs as paid, settings chosen on seeds 1 to 8 and reported on 12 others (P&L, its standard error, and P&L per unit of risk):

   | Trader | Sectors | Mixed |
   |---|---|---|
   | Pairs by distance, 8 | +5,701 (77), 0.070 | +3,293 (262), 0.038 |
   | Pairs by p-value, 8 | +5,698 (90), 0.070 | +4,057 (178), 0.057 |
   | Baskets of 4 books, 4 | +4,924 (114), 0.038 | +4,695 (91), 0.044 |
   | Johansen on all 12, 4 | +3,917 (77), 0.025 | +3,889 (106), 0.027 |
   | Residuals, the best here | +3,925 (139), 0.032 | +2,669 (114), 0.024 |
   | Copula pairs, 8 | +1,913 (111), 0.016 | +1,328 (163), 0.013 |
   | Residuals, the published rule | -8,976 (153) | -11,018 (182) |

   - **Pairs pay where none is truly cointegrated.** In the mixed market a pair with little of the factors left trades like a relation over these spans. Being truly cointegrated buys less exposure (baskets 0.7 against 1.6 and 2.9), not clearly more money.
   - **A leg costs more than it adds.** With one factor and weights of ±1, from 2 to 12 legs the before-cost P&L grows 2.70 times (the square root says 2.45) and costs 6.60 times (6), so the P&L is flat to six legs and falls beyond, and P&L per unit of risk falls from 0.029 to 0.004. Johansen on all twelve earns the most before costs (+12,247) and pays the most (8,330).
   - **The published residual rule does not pay here.** A round trip costs about 2.3 ticks a lot against about 4 ticks of residual, some 17 times their ratio (10 basis points against about 300). On six books, as the own volatility goes from 0.8 to 1.6 to 3.2: one pair +1,475, +3,040, +6,771; the best settings +1,988, +5,351, +11,424; the published levels on the longer window -126 (se 121), +2,705, +6,743; the published rule -3,387, -1,950, +491 (se 588). The window matters most, then the levels. Against the true two factors, one leaves the other in what it holds (11.8 of it) and makes nothing (-886, se 523); three cost a quarter of the P&L, and four about half.
   - **Breadth pays twice.** The same 20 lots in 1, 2, 4 and 8 pairs in sectors: P&L +2,251, +4,644, +5,678, +5,699; cost a lot 2.69, 1.92, 1.48, 1.19 ticks; sd a unit of time 12.7, 8.6, 6.2, 4.1; P&L per unit of risk 0.009, 0.027, 0.046, 0.069. In mixed the last goes from 0.011 to 0.042.
   - **Netting saves almost nothing,** 0.04% of lots at a decision every unit, 0.13% every 5 and 0.32% every 20, because relations seldom change at the same decision. More had been expected: what nets is what they hold, not what they trade.
   - **When the loadings drift,** everything fades (six books, 12 seeds; fixed, then a drift of 0.4): pairs +2,901 to +199 (se 315), baskets +2,604 to -288, copula pairs +751 to -129, residuals +1,955 to +505 (se 230). Choosing pairs again does not help (-51). The residuals keep their edge before costs (+2,818 against +964) but not clearly after (+305 over pairs, se 344).
   - **The scorecards agree with the fills:** +6,171 and +6,164 for the pairs, +5,356 and +5,353 for the residuals, +4,958 and +4,958 for baskets, +3,827 and +3,827 for Johansen, +2,220 and +2,197 for the copula.
6. **The Stat-arb view** (key 5; `app/statarb.py`, `static/js/lob-statarb.js`). A market of its own beside the Pairs view's, with its own clock, speed (up to 500 units of time a second) and event stream; `app/live.py` and `lob-panes.js` hold what the two share. Its settings choose sectors (up to four of up to four books, 16 in all) or mixed loadings (up to 12 books) and how many, the volatilities, half-life, impact, seed, and drifting loadings, and, since stage 4, an index book, deals and a trend (3S.11); Apply starts the books over, and is refused, with the reason, if a residual trader on the board could not trade in the new market. Up to six traders of ten kinds (`TRADER_KINDS`): pairs, baskets, residuals and copula pairs, whose defaults are those that did best in section 4 (but baskets', an entry of 1 with a trade rate of 0.25, which are notebook 13's section 6's best: 3S.8), the four that score every book (3S.5), and the two that trade what the market's extra settings add, index arbitrage and deals (3S.11). A new one can learn from the mids kept (20,000 units, more than the chart's 3,000), so it need not watch for a whole formation first.
   - **The chart:** four panes on one time axis, with the Pairs chart's controls: the books' mids, colored by sector; the signal of the trader shown, one line a relation, against its levels; what that trader holds, in lots and of the factors; and each trader's P&L.
   - **The board:** P&L, before costs, cost in all and a lot, lots, entries, what it holds, its exposure to the factors, and its relations in lots of each book (`A1 - 0.98 A2`) with their states and signals.
   - **What the tests see**, a button, runs the Johansen test and Engle-Granger on the last 10,000 units of mids, off the event loop, and says what they find against the truth from the loadings: the rank against the relations, and pairs called against pairs truly cointegrated. Past 12 books it gives no rank: Johansen's test has critical values for 12 books at most (3S.11).
   - **Choosing runs elsewhere** (`_choose_elsewhere`, one thread), so the market goes on meanwhile and the trader takes up the answer at its first decision after that. That is so for a copula trader too, whose pairs and copulas (`CopulaPortfolio._build`) are the work, and which then learns the mids that came meanwhile. With the defaults, distance and baskets take under a second at any speed. P-values take 3.7 s at the default speed and 11.6 s at top speed, when the market's thread has most of the interpreter, while the market goes on 5,751 units. A market stepped flat out, as in a test, can starve it for much longer, so the tests wait for the answer rather than for market time.
   - **The fit panel** is the Pairs view's (`fit_panel` in `panes.html`, `lobFitPanel` in `lob-panes.js`, `app/training.py`), over the books as they are now, on seeds the live market never has; 3R.7 says what it finds. It searches each kind's `fit_grid` values for the settings in its `fit_default` (window and entry for pairs and baskets, window and where to open for residuals, entry and how long to trade for for copula pairs: nine combinations of three values each, in three runs of 10,000 units), within `MAX_FIT_BOOK_UNITS` (books times candidates times runs times units of time, 16 million; a random sample of the grid if there are more). A default fit takes about a minute on 15 workers in the twelve sectors books (61 s for pairs, 65 for baskets and 69 for copula pairs, measured; 40 for residuals, whose warm-up is shorter), and only one runs at a time on a server, whichever view. Its panel says, as the Pairs view's does, that the standard error does not allow for having picked the best of the many. A trader's `warm_up` allows each decision's wait for the next event (`lob.statarb.WAIT`); a basket of one book, or of more books than there are, cannot trade and is left out of a fit, or refused.
7. **What notebook 12's section 10 finds about fitting.** The panel's own fit at its defaults: for each kind, the nine combinations of the values it proposes each trade alone in three new markets, 10,000 units over a warm-up, and there are three such fits for each kind in each market; the settings chosen, and the starting ones, are then tested on section 4's twelve seeds over 20,000 units, which no fit saw. **Where the start had room, a fit by P&L helps** (P&L from the start to the fitted settings): pairs +5,701 to +7,868 in the sectors market (gain +2,168, se 84) and +3,293 to +3,807 in the mixed (+514, se 81); baskets of three books, the panel's default then (an entry of 2, no rate; 3S.8 says what it is now) and not section 4's four, +5,100 to +6,511 (+1,411, se 85) and +4,475 to +5,527 (+1,052, se 154); residuals +3,925 to +4,629 in sectors (+703, se 197) and +2,669 to +2,805 in mixed (+136, se 98); the copula's fits lose, +1,913 to +1,744 (-169, se 90) and +1,328 to +1,209 (-119, se 57). Each se is across the test seeds with the chosen settings held fixed: it says how sure their gain is, not how sure a new fit's would be, since only three are drawn. **The gain is bought with trading:** lots 2.7 times the start's for pairs in sectors and 2.0 in mixed, 1.7 and 1.6 for baskets, 1.9 and 1.4 for residuals; exposure to the factors a little higher (pairs 0.9 to 1.3 and 2.9 to 3.5, baskets 0.2 to 0.3 and 1.4 to 1.8); and P&L per unit of risk down for pairs (0.070 to 0.062 and 0.038 to 0.036) and within 0.003 of the start for baskets and residuals. **Fitted by P&L per unit of risk it finds less.** The two objectives chose the same setting in none of the three fits in five of the eight rows. Pairs and baskets in sectors keep their P&L (-67, se 97, and +6, se 108) for 0.070 to 0.071 and 0.042 to 0.044, residuals go from 0.032 to 0.036 (+336, se 163) and pairs in mixed from 0.038 to 0.043 (+301, se 140), and the copula in sectors loses 1,014 (se 131) for a figure below its start's (0.014 against 0.016). **Fitting does not change which kind leads:** pairs in sectors, by 601 (se 139) before and 1,357 (se 112) after a fit by P&L, and baskets in mixed, by 1,181 (se 288) before and 1,720 (se 354); per unit of risk baskets and pairs are level in mixed, except after a fit by P&L. So section 4's order was no accident of how its settings were chosen. ***Clearly better* was called in 13 of the 24 fits and was right each time**, by the same measure (each of those settings beat the start on the test seeds by two standard errors of the difference). Pooled by whether the start was first of its grid on seeds 1 to 8: in the 15 fits where it was not, the best by P&L beat the start in all 15 and *clearly better* spoke in 12, passing up three better ones (which cost part of the gain: residuals in sectors +591 against +703, pairs in mixed +429 against +514, baskets in mixed +701 against +1,052); in the 9 where it was first, the best beat the start in 1, and *clearly better* spoke in that 1 and kept the copula's start every time. With nine settings truly equal the same rule says *clearly better* in 27.8% of fits (200,000 simulated, three runs each), against 9.2% for one rival chosen in advance, because it tests the best of nine as if it had been chosen beforehand: a guard against a fit with nothing to find, not a test at a stated level, and three fits to a row cannot say how often it is right. **The grids were set from a wider run on seeds 1 to 8**, kept out of the notebook, where pairs' and baskets' best entries are near 1.25 to 1.5 by P&L; the starts (entry 2, the highest of the three tried) were off it: pairs' start ranks 7th of its nine on those seeds in both markets and baskets' 3rd and 7th, while the copula's is first in both, residuals' first in mixed (4th in sectors). So where the start was first there was little to find, and a gain near zero there says that, not that fitting hurts.

### S. More Long/Short Strategies (`lob/crosssection.py`, notebook 13; stage 4: `lob/indexarb.py`, `lob/deals.py`, `lob/deal_traders.py`, notebook 15; what notebook 15 left open: notebook 16)
1. **A book of longs and shorts.** Notebook 12's traders each bet that a gap closes. These hold lots in every book at once, in proportion to a score of each. At a rebalance a `CrossSectionTrader` takes the scores less their projection on a row of ones and on the books' exposures to `factors` estimated factors (`neutralize`, by least squares), and holds `size` lots of the book with the largest weight and the rest in proportion, rounded to whole lots (halves to the even). The weights sum to 0 and have no exposure to any factor; the lots, rounded and built on estimates, keep a little of each: for the new traders in notebook 13's section 3, 1.6 to 4.6 lots of exposure to the true factors (root mean square) against about 25 lots held. `estimate_factors` is the PCA of `ResidualTrader` (the correlation matrix of the standardized moves, each eigenvector over the books' standard deviations; the exposures from regressing each book's demeaned moves on the eigenportfolios'), made again every `pca_every` (20) decisions from the last `pca_window` (500) moves. Equal scores leave nothing to hold, and a trader that holds something closes it. It rebalances every `hold` decisions and sits between (unless it trades less, 3S.7); `entries` counts books newly held long or short. It needs more books than its `factors` and one (`attach` refuses fewer), and holds nothing until it has the mids its score or its factors need (`needed`; `warm_up` is that many decisions and one more, in time). `learn` takes in past mids.
2. **Four scores.** `ReversalTrader` buys the books that fell over the last `lookback` decisions and sells those that rose, after Lehmann (1990) and Lo and MacKinlay (1990): a book's score is minus the sum of its residual moves (its moves less what the factors take of them; with no factors, its moves). `MomentumTrader` is Jegadeesh and Titman (1993): the same sum, plus, over `lookback` decisions that ended `skip` decisions ago (with a skip of 0, reversal turned over). `LeadLagTrader` (Lo and MacKinlay 1990; Hou 2007) regresses each book's move on all the books' moves one decision before, by ridge regression over the last `pca_window` moves, demeaned (the penalty is `ridge` times the regressors' average variance; a book's own lag is left out, since that is reversal, so the matrix `gamma` has a zero diagonal), and scores a book by its row of `gamma` times the last decision's moves. `FlowTrader` buys the books with the buying in their order flow, after Cont, Kukanov and Stoikov (2014), as `measure` says: `"trades"`, the change in a book's net bought lots over the last `lookback` decisions over the change in its volume (the share of what traded that was bought; 0 with no volume), or `"queues"`, the lots at the best bid less those at the best ask, over their sum, now (0 with a side empty). `contrarian_decomposition` is Lo and MacKinlay's split of a contrarian portfolio's profit at a lag into the books' own autocovariances, their cross-autocovariances and the dispersion of their means; the parts add to the profit.
3. **Seeing the flow.** `BookMarket.flow` and `volume` hold, a book each, the lots of the models' own orders that traded on arrival: `flow` signed (plus when the order bought), `volume` not. A trader's own orders are not counted. `BookTrader.observe(market)` does nothing unless a trader defines it, and is called just before `decide`, when every book has a mid; `FlowTrader` reads the counters and the queues there. Neither draws a random number, so notebook 12's markets are unchanged to the tick: against `lob/statarb.py` as it stood at e9988e9, over 2,500 units on seeds 101 and 102, the event frames, the random generator's state and the traders' lots, cash, spread paid and positions are equal (a bare market and pairs by distance in the sectors market, and a residual trader beside pairs in the mixed one), and a test gives a market with a trader that looks the path of the same market with one that does not.
4. **What notebook 13 finds** (its sections 1 to 3). The market is notebook 12's, in its two tables, *sectors* and *mixed*, and every trader takes out three factors, as many as the fair values share in either market (the sectors table has four columns but rank 3: its market column is the sum of its sector columns). Settings were chosen on seeds 1 to 8. Sections 1 and 2 use bare markets of 12,000 units on seeds 101 to 108; section 3 has each trader alone in its market, with market orders and costs as paid, over 20,000 units after a burn of 10,465 (65 more than notebook 12's, so its two traders there differ a little from 3R.5), on seeds 101 to 112.
   - **A book's residual moves bounce, then continue, then revert** (section 1: the one-unit moves less what the factors take of them, summed over non-overlapping stretches of h units; a book's sum against its own next). Over 1 and 2 units the correlation is -0.16 and -0.12 in the sectors market and -0.14 and -0.09 in the mixed: a mid that jumps with an order falls back as the book refills. Around 10 units it is +0.04 and +0.10, as a mid follows its fair value a few units late. At 25 it is -0.05 and -0.006 (se 0.005). From 50 on it reverts in both, more the longer the horizon: -0.21 and -0.18 at 50, -0.34 and -0.34 at 100, -0.44 and -0.41 at 200, as each book's own part decays with its half-life of 50.
   - **Nothing passes between the books once the factors are out.** A book's sum against another book's next is what the PCA forces (the residuals sum to about 0 across the books, so it is minus the own correlation over the books less one), to within 0.003 at every horizon.
   - **On raw moves the contrarian portfolio lives on the cross-autocovariance** (weights of 1/n, before costs, in thousandths of a tick a decision). At a lag of 1 it makes +45 (se 1.6) in the sectors market and +40 (se 0.7) in the mixed, from the cross part (+65 and +43) against an own part of -19 and -3: one book's move foretells the others' as they catch up to the factors, and with the factors in them a book's raw moves continue. At a lag of 5 it loses (-24 and -18), and at 25 it makes nothing (+0.2 and +0.1, se 0.3 and 0.6).
   - **Every signal knows something, at its own horizon** (section 2: fourteen listeners, a trader of each kind in a bare market with a size so small that its lots round to 0, so that the market is untouched, 0 lots in 16 runs; the IC is the correlation across books of its factor-neutral weights with the next decision's moves). The queues know most: 0.23 and 0.22 at an interval of 1 unit, and 0.12 and 0.11 at 5. Trade flow over 5 decisions knows 0.06 to 0.09. Lead-lag knows 0.07 and 0.05 one unit ahead and nothing, or a little less, five ahead (-0.005 and -0.021). Reversal knows 0.10 and 0.09 over 1 unit and 0.04 over 20 decisions of 5 units, but not in between, where moves continue (-0.020 and -0.045 over 5 decisions of 5 units). Momentum knows the opposite of reversal, -0.04 to -0.06. What lead-lag knows is probably that bounce by another route: the residuals sum to about 0, so the other books' moves stand in for minus a book's own.
   - **What they know is small against what trading it costs.** Per lot traded, rebalancing at every decision, the best signal earns +0.31 and +0.33 ticks (trade flow over 5 decisions of 5 units) and the queues, deciding every 5 units, +0.17 and +0.15; a lot costs 0.98 to 1.36 ticks to cross the spreads for one trader of each of four kinds rebalancing at every decision of 5 units, more the more lots it trades (1.02 at 7,513 lots to 1.36 at 28,009 in the sectors market, 0.98 at 6,460 to 1.34 at 27,613 in the mixed), as it would if bigger orders walk further into the books. So no signal pays at the turnover of a rebalance at every decision; a signal held longer earns over more decisions for each lot traded.
   - **Only the slow reversal pays, and little.** Every trader alone, 20,000 units after the same burn, costs as paid, 12 seeds none of the settings was chosen on (P&L in ticks times lots, and its standard error):

     | Trader | Sectors | Mixed |
     |---|---|---|
     | Reversal, look back 20, hold 100, every 5 (default) | +968 (106) | +868 (138) |
     | Reversal, look back 5, hold 20, every 5 | -4,137 (237) | -4,008 (176) |
     | Reversal, look back 1, hold 5, every 5 | -36,214 (318) | -35,538 (197) |
     | Momentum, look back 100, hold 100, every 5 (default) | -5,087 (137) | -4,459 (143) |
     | Lead-lag, hold 20, every 1 (default) | -38,679 (284) | -37,168 (369) |
     | Lead-lag, hold 100, every 1 | -8,196 (220) | -7,397 (217) |
     | Lead-lag, hold 20, every 5 | -7,386 (246) | -6,959 (174) |
     | Trade flow, look back 5, hold 25, every 5 | -10,798 (311) | -10,091 (209) |
     | Trade flow, look back 5, hold 100, every 1 | -8,549 (274) | -7,850 (334) |
     | Queue flow, hold 25, every 5 (default) | -7,089 (231) | -6,300 (148) |
     | Queue flow, hold 100, every 1 | -9,047 (250) | -8,658 (238) |
     | Queue flow, hold 5, every 1 | -162,450 (297) | -157,347 (207) |
     | Pairs by distance, 8 (notebook 12) | +5,706 (81) | +3,283 (271) |
     | Residuals, 3 factors (notebook 12) | +3,952 (143) | +2,686 (122) |

     Reversal over 20 decisions, rebalanced every 100, makes money on 12 and 11 of the 12 seeds, a sixth and a quarter of what eight pairs make, for fewer lots in the sectors market (1,439 against 2,387). Every other setting loses, in both markets and on every seed, so 1 of the 12 settings makes money after costs, and by two standard errors (a count over a list fixed in advance, not a test).
   - **The faster the same signal is traded, the more it loses.** The queues rebalanced every 5 decisions of one unit lose 162,450 and 157,347: 141,273 and 138,370 lots at 1.40 and 1.34 a lot, against +35,156 and +28,256 before costs. At that turnover the before-cost figure also holds the trader's own orders moving the mids it is marked at, so it cannot tell what the signal knows from what the orders did (section 2's listeners measure that without trading). Held for 100 decisions of one unit, the same signal trades about a nineteenth of the lots and loses 9,047 and 8,658; what it makes before costs (+1,168 and +1,081) is a ninth of its costs. Lead-lag and trade flow held for 100 decisions of one unit lose about as much, but make less before costs (+749 and -94 in the sectors market, +993 and +240 in the mixed); lead-lag held for 20 decisions of one unit loses 38,679 and 37,168. Trade flow, tried at two settings, does not follow the order: held for 25 decisions of five units it loses more than held for 100 of one unit.
   - **Momentum loses, as it must here:** 5,087 and 4,459, and it loses before costs as well (-2,916 and -2,466). Over its 100 decisions of five units the books' residuals revert, and the factors, random walks, have nothing to continue.
   - **The scorecards agree with the fills** (section 4, sectors, seed 101, over the run from each trader's first order): +622 and +624 for reversal, -6,638 and -6,640 for momentum, -58,236 and -58,239 for lead-lag, -9,180 and -9,188 for the queues, -16,734 and -16,729 for trade flow. Each holds at most 0.80 to 0.99 of the card's capital of 150,000 ticks times lots, never above 1.
5. **The four kinds on the Stat-arb view** (`app/statarb.py`, `CROSS_SECTIONAL`; the form, board and chart as in 3R.6). They join pairs, baskets, residuals and copula pairs among the six traders, each with its own settings. The defaults are the traders of notebook 13's section 4, but reversal's, which is section 5's best (3S.7: a look back of 50, rebalanced at every decision, a trade rate of 0.1). Of section 4's, reversal's (look back 20, hold 100) and the queues' are the best of the settings tried of their kinds in section 3, momentum's the only one tried, and lead-lag's, the one that knows something at an interval of 1 unit, loses the most of its kind (the table above). Choosing *trades* for the flow gives the table's trade flow row. The fit panel tries two settings by default and offers the rest:

   | Kind | Defaults (lots of the largest 5 in each) | A fit tries | Also on offer |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | Reversal | look back 50, hold 1, trade rate 0.1, 3 factors over 500 decisions, every 5 units | look back 20, 50, 100 and trade rate 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 1 (12) | hold 1, 25, 100; factors 0, 3, 4 |
   | Momentum | look back 100, skip 0, hold 100, 3 factors over 500, every 5 | look back 20, 100, 400 and hold 20, 100, 400 (9) | skip 0, 5 |
   | Lead-lag | hold 20, ridge 1, 3 factors, fit over 500, every 1 | hold 5, 20, 100 and every 1, 5 (6) | ridge 0.1, 1, 10 |
   | Order flow | reads queues, trades over 5, hold 25, 3 factors over 500, every 5 | hold 5, 25, 100 and every 1, 5 (6) | trades over 1, 5, 25 |

   - **What the flow reads is a choice, not a number:** `Choice` in `app/pairs.py`, a drop-down. `parse` refuses anything but its options and names them (*Reads: one of queues, trades*), the form marks the field at fault as it does a number, and *trades over* matters only to *trades*. A fit keeps what the flow reads and tries the numbers.
   - **Starting one.** *Learn from the past first* takes in the mids kept, one every interval, so the factors are estimated at once; a flow trader has no past flow or queues to learn, so it sees the order flow only from when it starts and says how many decisions it waits (the longer of what its flow and its factors need). Its settings are refused, saying why, if its factors are not fewer than the books less one, at the start and in an Apply that would leave one on the board stranded.
   - **The board and the chart for a trader that scores every book.** Each book is a relation of its own: the board lists the books by name, a chip green when the trader wants the book long and red when short, with its score in two figures (scores run from 0.03 for lead-lag to 12 for reversal), and *holding n of N* counts all N books though the board lists at most 12 (`MAX_SIGNALS`; with an index book it lists every book, up to 17: 3S.11). The chart has a line for each book's score before it is made neutral, which is why a book with a score below 0 can be held long, and no entry or exit levels; the legend says what the score is.
   - **Fitting.** The fit panel is the others', over the books as they are. A combination with too many factors for the books is left out of the grid, and a fit with none left is refused with the reason, as is one from the user's own settings if their factors are not fewer than the books less one. Notebook 13 does not fit these kinds, so whether a fit helps them is not tested.
6. **The four stages, all built.** Notebook 13 was planned in four stages. Stage 1, four cross-sectional signals traded as they are, is 3S.1 to 3S.5; stage 2, trading the same signals less (going part of the way to the target, with a band inside which it does not trade, after Gârleanu and Pedersen 2013 and Davis and Norman 1990, since turnover is what stops every signal here), is 3S.7; stage 3, sparse baskets (choosing baskets by Box and Tiao's predictability, after d'Aspremont 2011, and not by Johansen's test), is 3S.8. **Stage 4, an index book, deal events and a persistent factor, is built too (2026-11-12), and is points 9 to 11:** its library (9), what notebook 15 finds (10) and its part of the Stat-arb view (11). Its results are in a notebook of their own, notebook 15, and not in more sections of notebook 13, which takes 80 minutes to run, so that every change to it would mean all of it again. Each of the three features is off by default and the notebook turns on one at a time, so that its markets are notebook 13's with one thing added and not a new market. What notebook 15 left open (reading the news on a trader's own pairs, why the pairs chosen in the market with a risk premium are worth less, and sector index books) is notebook 16's, with no change to the library (point 12).
7. **Trading less (stage 2).** Section 3 found that the faster a signal is traded the more it loses. Stage 2 asks whether the same signal can be traded less: by holding longer or entering later, by going only part of the way to the target at a decision (a *rate*), or by not trading within a *band* of lots around the aim. Notebook 13's section 5 asks it of seven traders in the sectors market, and in a test on section 3's twelve seeds of four families in both markets.
   - **The library** (`BookTrader` in `lob/statarb.py`; `CrossSectionTrader.decide`). Every `BookTrader` has a `rate` above 0 and at most 1 and a `band` of 0 or more, 1 and 0 by default (`_check_costs` says *a rate above 0 and at most 1, and a band of 0 or more* when one is made). It keeps an **aim** (`_aim`), in lots that need not be whole, which goes `rate` of the way from where it was to the target at each decision (partial adjustment, Gârleanu and Pedersen 2013). `_toward(target)` gives the whole lots to trade in each book: the gap from the position to the aim less the band (Davis and Norman 1990), `sign(gap) * max(|gap| - band, 0)`, rounded with halves to the even. The aim keeps what the rounding takes, so the share of the gap to the target that is closed is `rate` on average however few lots there are, and a last lot to go is not left for ever. **A book whose target is 0 is closed all the way to the aim, whatever the band** (the zero-target close), so a band does not keep a position that the rule has closed. A band applies to each book alone, so one leg of a relation can sit inside it while another trades. With a rate of 1 and no band the aim is the target and the move is the gap to it.
   - **Where it trades.** In a `StateTrader` (`_become`, orders netted) the aim goes toward the lots the states ask for, `size` times the states' sum with the weights, rounded; asking again with the same states goes on from where the last moves left it, and with no relations left it closes everything at once, whatever the settings, and forgets its aim. A `CrossSectionTrader` that goes all the way trades at a rebalance and sits between, as before; with a rate below 1 or a band above 0 (`_cost_aware`) the rebalance only sets its target (`_target`), and its aim and position go toward it at every decision from when it can trade. `netted_lots`, the lots its relations wanted that offset each other in one order, is counted when cost-aware against what the states last asked for (`_asked`) and not against the position, which holds lots not yet traded that netting did not save; with the defaults it is counted as it was. `entries` counts the states asked for and not the trades made: with a rate or a band a trader may never reach the lots a state asks for, and `score_portfolio` says so. A trader that does not net its orders (`netted=False`) refuses a rate below 1 or a band above 0 when made (*cost-aware trading needs netting*), whether its relations are fixed or chosen; the cross-sectional traders always net.
   - **The defaults are what they were.** Against `lob/statarb.py` and `lob/crosssection.py` as they stood at 86bea8e, twelve traders (pairs by distance, chosen again, and not netted; baskets of three; residuals; copula pairs; reversal, momentum, lead-lag, the flow of the trades, and the flow of the queues at two holds), each alone and all together in both markets on seeds 1 to 3 over 4,000 units of time: 78 markets and 144 trader comparisons, none differing in the event frame, the number of events, or any trader's cash, lots, spread paid, entries, netted lots, position and states, and every trader traded in every run. The tests hold each kind to the same, number for number, on one seed against the code of 86bea8e from git (skipped if git cannot show it).
   - **Not Gârleanu and Pedersen's trader**, as `BookTrader`'s docstring says. For one signal that decays at a steady rate their aim is the mean-variance portfolio of the signal now, scaled down the more the faster the signal decays and the more trading costs. Here the target is the trader's own rule's, scaled to `size` whatever the signal's strength or decay: for a signal of one kind it points as their aim would, but how much it holds is the user's choice, not the costs'. Their costs grow with the square of the lots traded, for which going part of the way is best; for costs in proportion to them, as a spread's are, a band is (Davis and Norman 1990); crossing the spreads here costs some of each, half a spread for every lot and more the further an order walks into a book. So the rate and the band show what going part of the way and a band do to these traders, and not what their costs make best.
   - **What notebook 13's section 5 finds: the frontier** (seven traders each alone in the sectors market, seeds 101 to 108, 10,000 units of time after each trader's own warm-up and 100 more, one setting changed at a time from the whole way at every decision, up to 15 settings a panel; the values were chosen on seeds 1 to 8, which none of these seeds did). **Reversal pays on every curve, and at a look back of 50 more than at 20.** At 50 a rebalance at every decision loses 10,056 (se 166) on 16,207 lots; holding for 25 decisions makes +1,662 (se 68) on 2,700 lots, a rate of 0.1 +2,308 (se 65) on 3,468, a band of 2 +1,322 (se 38) on 3,553, and a band of 1 held for 25 decisions +1,772 (se 92) on 1,774, each positive on all eight seeds; at 20 the best of each curve is lower (+686, +1,333, +177 and +785). **The queues, lead-lag and the trades never pay, however they are traded less**: 0 of 8 seeds at every setting. The least loss is at a hold of 400, 1,102 (se 141), 1,376 (se 124) and 288 (se 71) on 896, 877 and 177 lots, with almost nothing before costs (+153, -260 and -71); the lowest rate, 0.02, loses 4,341, 3,371 and 1,838, and the widest band, 4, 12,995, 19,252 and 1,816. **For pairs the start is hard to beat** (entering at 1.25, +3,856, se 103): a rate of 0.5 makes +4,288 (se 97), about three combined standard errors more, and no band beats none. **Residuals gain most** (opening beyond 1, +1,947, se 215, on 5,235 lots): a rate of 0.25 makes +3,029 (se 126), a band of 1 +3,046 (se 208) and a band of 1.5 +3,045 (se 337), each positive on all eight seeds.
   - **A lot costs about a tick at every rate below 1**, 0.99 to 1.04 against 1.05 to 1.56 at a rate of 1 and no band, **because a rate's orders are of one lot** (a table after the test, the sectors market, seeds 101 to 103: every order of a trader with a rate is for one lot, but 5% of the pairs' lots, in orders of two or three; a one-lot order pays half the spread, 0.98 to 1.02 ticks whoever sends it; a hold sends orders of 2.7 to 4.8 lots on average for the lot in them and a band of 0.5 or 1 of 2.4 to 3.7, and a lot's cost grows with its order, from 0.98 in one-lot orders to 1.42 in orders of six or more for reversal held for 25, and to 2.52 for pairs entering at 1.25), **which does not decide the P&L**: the queues at a rate of 0.02 pay 1.01 a lot and lose 4,341 (se 50) on 5,135 lots, about what a hold of 100 loses, 4,410 (se 254), on 3,636 lots at 1.38. **A rate or a band holds less, and a longer hold does not**: in the five cross-sectional panels 25.3 to 26.4 lots at the start and 24.7 to 27.2 at every hold, and 8.7, 5.4, 3.2, 1.7 and 3.4 at a rate of 0.02. **A band raises the exposure to the factors, and a rate does not**, in the panels of reversal, the trades, pairs and residuals (reversal 1.6 to 3.3 lots at a band of 2, pairs 1.3 to 4.1 at 2, residuals 2.1 to 3.8 at 1; a rate stays within 0.2 of the start); in the queues' and lead-lag's panels, which start at 4.8 and 5.2, both lower it. The best point of a curve is the best of three to five on the seeds that report it, so it flatters its curve.
   - **What it finds: the test** (both markets, seeds 101 to 112 with section 3's burn and span; each family's section 3 row, then its best alone, with a rate and with a band, chosen on seeds 1 to 8 by P&L summed over both markets; reversal and the queues decide every 5 and 1 units, pairs and residuals every 5; P&L in ticks times lots and its standard error across seeds):

     | Trader | Sectors | Mixed |
     |---|---|---|
     | Reversal, look back 20, hold 100 (section 3's) | +968 (106) | +868 (138) |
     | Reversal, look back 50, hold 25 | +3,485 (142) | +3,430 (138) |
     | Reversal, look back 50, rate 0.1 | +4,364 (109) | +4,203 (68) |
     | Reversal, look back 50, hold 25, band 1 | +3,790 (149) | +3,398 (150) |
     | Queues, hold 100 (section 3's) | -9,047 (250) | -8,658 (238) |
     | Queues, hold 400 | -2,430 (244) | -2,087 (236) |
     | Queues, rate 0.02 | -8,823 (68) | -9,058 (66) |
     | Queues, band 4 | -25,862 (98) | -24,786 (66) |
     | Pairs by distance, 8, entry 2 (section 3's) | +5,706 (81) | +3,283 (271) |
     | Pairs, entry 1.25 | +7,808 (91) | +3,917 (343) |
     | Pairs, entry 1, rate 0.25 | +8,639 (105) | +4,484 (367) |
     | Pairs, entry 1.25, band 0.5 | +7,390 (94) | +3,686 (324) |
     | Residuals, 3 factors, enter beyond 1.5 (section 3's) | +3,952 (143) | +2,686 (122) |
     | Residuals, enter beyond 1.25 | +4,625 (131) | +2,982 (137) |
     | Residuals, enter beyond 0.5, rate 0.1 | +7,223 (232) | +6,544 (154) |
     | Residuals, enter beyond 1, band 1 | +6,768 (150) | +6,037 (152) |

     **Most of the differences below compare a family's bests and not one setting changed**: only reversal's band, added to a hold of 25, and pairs' band, added to an entry of 1.25, change one thing. Paired by seed (one trader less another, on the same market and seed; sectors, then mixed):
     - **Reversal at a look back of 50 pays more than three times what section 3's does, and a rate pays most.** Alone it makes +2,517 (se 147) and +2,562 (se 156) more than section 3's row, on 12 of 12 seeds in both, and +0.015 and +0.018 per unit of risk (section 3's small grid did not try a look back of 50 or a hold of 25). A rate adds +879 (se 208, 12 of 12) and +773 (se 151, 11 of 12) to that, and +0.017 and +0.018 per unit of risk, on 12 of 12 in both. A band adds +305 (se 145, 8 of 12) and -31 (se 138, 7 of 12) on about two thirds of the lots (3,602 and 3,378 against 5,418 and 5,145).
     - **The queues lose in every row** (0 of 12 seeds in both markets), and holding longer loses least: a hold of 400 is +6,616 (se 336) and +6,571 (se 408) better than section 3's row, on 12 of 12 seeds. A rate of 0.02 loses about what section 3's row does, and against the hold of 400 it is -6,392 (se 258) and -6,972 (se 235), on 10,313 and 10,416 lots against 1,830 and 1,817; a band of 4 loses more. At these values a rate or a band trades the queues more than a longer hold does, and loses more.
     - **Pairs earn more by entering earlier, on more lots and less per unit of risk.** Entering at 1.25 is +2,102 (se 84, 12 of 12) and +634 (se 99, 12 of 12) more than section 3's row, on 5,997 and 3,090 lots against 2,387 and 1,478, and -0.008 and -0.003 per unit of risk (0.062 against 0.070 in the sectors market). **A rate adds a little, and a band takes a little away:** entering at 1 with a rate of 0.25 is +832 (se 86) and +566 (se 72) more than entering at 1.25 alone, on 12 of 12 seeds in both; a band of 0.5 is -418 (se 101) and -232 (se 63), on 1 of 12 in both. Per unit of risk no pairs row beats section 3's in the sectors market (0.062, 0.068 and 0.066 against 0.070); in the mixed market only the rate's does (0.040 against 0.038).
     - **Residuals gain a little from entering earlier, and a great deal more from a rate or a band.** Entering beyond 1.25 is +674 (se 96, 11 of 12) and +296 (se 88, 10 of 12) more than section 3's row, and -0.002 and -0.003 per unit of risk. A rate (entering beyond 0.5, at 0.1) adds +2,597 (se 207) and +3,562 (se 151) to that, and a band (beyond 1, at 1) +2,142 (se 160) and +3,055 (se 179), on 12 of 12 seeds in both markets, and +0.009 and +0.018, and +0.012 and +0.020 per unit of risk; the band does it on fewer lots (6,366 and 6,244 against 9,129 and 8,880). Both beat section 3's row per unit of risk (0.039 and 0.042 against 0.033 in the sectors market, 0.039 and 0.041 against 0.024 in the mixed), though the rate holds 35.5 and 36.3 lots against section 3's 15.1 and 16.7, and the band raises the exposure to the factors to 3.8 and 3.5 lots (against 1.6 and 1.9).
     - **A rate's lots are the cheapest in every family**: 1.00, 1.01, 1.00 and 1.00 ticks in the sectors market (reversal, the queues, pairs, residuals) and 0.96, 0.97, 0.95 and 0.96 in the mixed, against 1.03 to 1.39 in every other row. It does not make a rate pay by itself: the queues' rate pays 10,418 of costs on 10,313 lots, about what section 3's row pays (10,214, on 7,340 lots). **The two markets agree**: every difference has the same sign in both, on P&L and per unit of risk, except reversal's band on P&L, which is small in both.
   - **Three cards** (the sectors market, seed 101, as in section 4): reversal with a rate of 0.1 (look back 50), eight pairs entering at 1 with a rate of 0.25, and the residuals opening beyond 1 with a band of 1, each deciding every 5 units. Each record makes what its fills made, to within 1: +5,705 and +5,706, +9,294 and +9,295, +9,359 and +9,360. **The residuals' card holds 1.10 times its capital of 150,000 at its most**, above 1 on 0.1% of days (the reversal and pairs cards 0.72, on 0.0%); none of section 4's cards held more than 0.99, so on those days its figure is leveraged. The limits of section 5 are in section 7.
   - **On the Stat-arb view** (`app/statarb.py`). Every kind has a *Trade rate* (0.01 to 1) and a *No-trade band* (0 to 10 lots), `COSTS`, after the lots and before *Decide every*, and their defaults, 1 and 0, leave every default trader as it was but reversal's, and baskets' from 2026-11-11 (a test builds each kind with its defaults and checks that it is not cost-aware, and that reversal's and baskets' are; the proof above is of the library). **Reversal's default is section 5's best**, at the user's word after stage 2: a look back of 50, a hold of 1 and a trade rate of 0.1 (+4,364 in the sectors market, against +968 for the look back of 20 held for 100 that was its default in stage 1), and a default fit tries its look back (20, 50, 100) and its rate (0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 1), twelve combinations at its hold of 1, where a rate matters; its board line says *rebalanced at every decision* for a hold of 1. A value out of range is refused with the field at fault, and every kind trades part of the way when started with one. **What a fit proposes for them:** `COST_GRID` for the four kinds of relations (pairs, baskets, residuals and copula pairs), rates of 0.1, 0.25, 0.5 and 1 and bands of 0, 0.5, 1 and 2, and `SCORE_COST_GRID` for the four that score every book, rates of 0.02, 0.05, 0.1 and 1 and bands of 0, 1, 2 and 4, the values that mattered on seeds 1 to 8 in section 5, which has no baskets, copula pairs or momentum, so theirs are borrowed from the kinds it has (pairs and residuals for the relations; reversal, the queues, lead-lag and the trades for the signals). **A fit tries them only when ticked**, but reversal's rate (neither is in the other kinds' `fit_default`, so their default fits are as they were, and the panel's grid for each can be edited); with them ticked the grid is larger, and a random sample of it is taken within `MAX_FIT_BOOK_UNITS`. **The board's description says how a trader trades less**, and only when it does (`_trading_less`): for a trader of relations a sentence of its own, *It goes 0.25 of the way to its target at each decision and does not trade within 1 lot of that, except to close a book.*, and for the four that score every book a clause ending its sentence, *, going 0.25 of the way to its target at each decision and not trading within 1 lot of that, except to close a book*. The summaries of pairs, residuals and reversal (the third field of each `TraderKind`) say what section 5 found for the kind; of the defaults only reversal's changed in stage 2, and baskets' after stage 3 (3S.8). **The Pairs view's traders** (`lob/pairs.py`) **have neither setting.**
8. **Sparse baskets (stage 3).** Notebook 12's baskets come from Johansen's trace test (`best_baskets` tests every combination of a few books; `johansen_relations` takes the vectors on all of them). Stage 3 asks whether choosing them another way trades better: by Box and Tiao's (1977) *predictability*. Fit a VAR(1) to the books' levels; a portfolio's predictability is the share of its variance that the books' lagged levels explain, about 1 for a random walk and near 0 for noise, so the least predictable portfolio is the most mean-reverting. Over all the books it has a leg in each; asking for few legs is d'Aspremont's (2011) NP-hard sparse problem, which he attacks with a greedy search and `lob/sparse_baskets.py` follows. Notebook 13's section 6 asks what each chooser picks in the two markets, and how the baskets trade with section 5's rate.
   - **The library** (`lob/sparse_baskets.py`; it draws no random number). `fit_var1(levels, ridge=0)` fits x_t = A x_{t-1} + e_t by least squares to the levels less their means and gives `A` and `gamma`, the covariance of the lagged levels (X0'X0 over T - 1; `ridge` is a share of the average variance added to the diagonal, and a singular fit gets the minimum-norm answer); it needs `n + 2` rows, since at `n + 1` the fit is exact. Box and Tiao's `gamma` is the covariance of the levels under stationarity, which is the same; d'Aspremont's sample version divides by the current levels' covariance, and on eight real runs the two least eigenvalues agree to within 0.002 on 2,000 rows (0.01 on 400). `predictability(w, a, gamma)` is w'A gamma A'w / w'gamma w, which does not depend on the scale of `w`. `box_tiao(a, gamma, books=None)` solves the generalized symmetric eigenproblem M w = lambda gamma w, with M = A gamma A', on the books given (all if none), and gives the eigenvalues, the predictabilities, least first, and their rows of weights (zero outside the books). `greedy_basket(a, gamma, start, legs)` grows a set of books from `start` one at a time, each time adding the book that gives the least smallest eigenvalue (ties to the lowest index), and gives `(lambda, books, row)`. `sparse_baskets(history, count=1, legs=3, every=5, ridge=0)` grows a basket from every pair of books (at `legs` 2 the pairs themselves), keeps each distinct set once, ranks the sets by least predictability, and gives `count` rows of weights with the largest leg 1, the shape of `best_baskets`, so a `RelationTrader` takes it as its `choose`; as in `best_baskets`, the baskets that share no book with a better one come first. With `legs` 0 it gives the least predictable portfolios of all the books (`box_tiao`), dense, like `johansen_relations`, at most as many as there are books.
   - **What it leaves out**, as its docstrings say. Any test of whether a basket is a relation: with `legs` of 2 or more it gives the `count` baskets asked for (fewer only when the distinct sets run out) whether or not any is one, where Johansen's choosers give only what the trace test accepts, and a book added for a small gain in sample can bring a factor in, as a third book from another sector does in a market of sectors; with `legs` 0, the rows past the number of true relations are not relations. A floor on a basket's swings: predictability is scale-free, so it does not see a swing against what the legs cost to trade, and Cuturi and d'Aspremont (2013) add a variance threshold so that a basket is not chosen for swinging too little to pay for its legs. d'Aspremont's other searches, a semidefinite relaxation and an exact one: the greedy search is the cheapest and can be far from the least predictable set of its size, as he notes, since the best sets of different sizes need not nest. And lags beyond the first. It also rewards unpredictable noise in the mids as much as mean reversion of the fair values, which is dormant here, where every book's order flow is alike and a mid's lag behind its fair value cancels within a relation.
   - **How it was checked.** The specification had a premise review before anything was built, which corrected it in eleven places (among them that the mixed market's best triple nearly is a relation, that predictability is of the whole vector's past and not of the portfolio's own lag, and that the mids' deviation from the fair values is a lag, which cancels within a relation). `tests/test_sparse_baskets.py` has 88 tests, 86 when it was built and two more after an audit that found six problems, all fixed: two docstring numbers that did not survive recomputation, the reason given for the `n + 2` rows, the defaults of `sparse_baskets` and the tie-break of its ranking untested, and the view's description wrong at 2 legs and at all the books with a count above the books, with its hover hint unpinned. Each rule is held to a computation made in the test and not by the code: `np.linalg.lstsq` for the fit, ridge as extra rows, `np.linalg.eigvals` of the inverse of `gamma` times M for the eigenproblem, and plain loops for the greedy step, the ranking and the order the baskets are picked in. A mechanical sweep of the library's lines (every comparison, arithmetic operator, number, negation, `.T`, `if` test and `min`, `max` or `sorted` call, changed one at a time in a copy of the repository) caught 178 of 184 changes, and the 6 survivors are equivalent (changes that cannot alter a result).
   - **On the Stat-arb view** (`app/statarb.py`). The Baskets kind has a *Chosen by* choice, first among its settings (a `Choice`, as the flow trader's *Reads*): *johansen*, the default, which builds the trader as it was (a test holds each of its choosers to `best_baskets` or `johansen_relations` with the same arguments), or *box-tiao*, which gives the `RelationTrader` `sparse_baskets(count, legs, every=1)` as its `choose`. *Books each* then means 2 to rank the pairs, 3 or 4 to grow baskets from every pair, or 0 for the least predictable portfolios of all the books; 1 is refused as before, and anything but the two choices is refused naming them (*Chosen by: one of johansen, box-tiao*). The formation, window, entry, exit, lots, rate, band and interval mean what they did. A fit keeps the choice and tries the numbers (a `Choice` is not in a grid, so a default fit is as it was), and a candidate started from a fit keeps it. The board's description says how the baskets are chosen and that there is no test (*Up to 4 baskets of 3 books grown greedily from every pair by Box-Tiao predictability (d'Aspremont 2011), the least predictable first and none rejected, from 2,000 decisions; ...; there is no test: it always gives baskets.*), and a hover hint on the choice says what each does. The kind's summary (the third field of its `TraderKind`) says what section 6 found, with its numbers. **The default is section 6's best for Johansen's baskets of 3, at the user's word on 2026-11-11, after stage 3:** an entry of 1 and a trade rate of 0.25 (it was an entry of 2 and no rate), which earned +2,044 (se 132) and +2,012 (se 176) more than the old default in the sectors and mixed markets, on more than two and a half times the lots (below). Johansen is still the chooser, and the other settings are as they were (four baskets of 3 books, a formation of 2,000, a window of 400, an exit of 0, 5 lots, every 5 units); pairs, residuals, copula pairs and the other kinds are unchanged, and pairs keep an entry of 2 and a rate of 1 (the Baskets kind takes the entry of `Z_PARAMS` and the rate of `COSTS` with another default, by `dataclasses.replace`, and a test holds that the shared ones, which pairs use, are as they were). The kind's summary says so, and the board's description of a default baskets trader ends *It goes 0.25 of the way to its target at each decision.* A default fit still tries the window and the entry (1, 1.5 and 2: the default is now first of its entries, where it was last), with the rate held at 0.25, and the grids a fit proposes for the rate and the band (3S.7) are as they were; that a fit helps from this start is not tested.
   - **What notebook 13's section 6 finds: what each chooser picks** (seven choosers, each on the history that a trader of section 6's trading cell passes to its chooser at its first choice: the mids at its first 2,000 decisions, 5 units apart and then the wait for the next event, with the trader alone in its market, so the table is of the traded baskets (a trader sends no order before its first choice, so the history does not depend on its chooser, entry or rate); a chooser that keeps a copy of what it is given records the history, and an assert in the cell checks, on seed 101 in both markets, that the rows of Box-Tiao's 4 of 3 on it are the weights that a trader of it holds after its first choice, which is all the notebook checks (an independent check outside it found a real trader's first weights equal to the chooser's rows on every seed, 101 to 108, in both markets, for Johansen's and Box-Tiao's baskets of 3 and 4 books and for the dashboard's default); seeds 101 to 108; pairs by distance, as in section 5, and Box-Tiao's, 8 pairs each; Johansen's and Box-Tiao's four baskets of 3 books and of 4 (*4 of 3*, *4 of 4*); and Box-Tiao's four baskets of all twelve books, *4 of all*). The table also gives, for each chooser, the distinct *books* that a seed's rows span and the most books that two of them *share*. **Box-Tiao's baskets are the least predictable at 3 legs and more, but by little**: every chooser's mean is 0.873 to 0.965, where a random walk's is about 1, and Box-Tiao's 4 of 3 and 4 of 4 have 0.885 and 0.875 against Johansen's 0.891 and 0.889 in the sectors market and 0.911 and 0.896 against 0.922 and 0.917 in the mixed; its pairs have 0.957 against 0.965 for pairs by distance in the mixed market and not less in the sectors, 0.901 against 0.899; more legs lower it (0.901, 0.885, 0.875 and 0.873 at 2, 3, 4 and 12 legs in the sectors market). **Every chooser's baskets leave little of the true factors in the sectors market, 0.012 to 0.036 on average** (the leftover exposure ||w'B|| / ||w||, 0 for an exact relation; no basket leaves more than 0.084), and Johansen's leave less than Box-Tiao's: 0.012 against 0.022 at 3 legs (at most 0.050 against 0.083) and 0.015 against 0.026 at 4. **In the mixed market, where no pair is a relation, pairs leave the most**, 0.252 for pairs by distance and 0.334 for Box-Tiao's on average (at most 1.450), against 0.041 to 0.087 for every chooser of baskets; Box-Tiao's mean is lifted by a few pairs whose smaller leg is a small part of the larger, so that the pair is nearly a single book, which a chooser with no test of a relation can pick (its largest, 1.450, is two and a half times pairs by distance's 0.580), and an independent check outside the notebook found that its typical pair leaves less than pairs by distance's; and Box-Tiao's baskets leave less than Johansen's at the same legs: 0.055 against 0.087 at 3 and 0.043 against 0.056 at 4 (without Johansen's largest of the 3, 0.548, its other 31 rows average about 0.07). **More legs swing less per lot, and Box-Tiao's swing less than Johansen's at 4 legs**: 2.85, 2.31 and 1.46 ticks a lot at 3, 4 and 12 legs against Johansen's 2.75 and 2.49 in the sectors market (so more at 3 there), and 2.75, 2.26 and 1.41 against 2.97 and 2.62 in the mixed. So the least predictable chooser, the 4 of all, swings least per lot, which predictability does not see. **Box-Tiao's baskets span fewer books than Johansen's, and at 4 legs in the sectors market share more**: its 4 of 3 span 8.9 and 9.6 books (sectors, mixed) against Johansen's 10.0 and 10.1, and its 4 of 4 9.4 and 10.2 against Johansen's 12.0 and 12.0; the most that two baskets share is the same at 3 legs (2.0 and 1.4), and at 4 legs 3.0 against 2.4 in the sectors market and 2.6 against 2.5 in the mixed (one book on one seed of eight); its eight pairs span all 12 books on every seed and pairs by distance 9.2 and 9.0. **Box-Tiao chooses more than ten times as fast**: 0.01 and 0.02 seconds for its 4 of 3 and 4 of 4 in both markets against Johansen's 0.23 and 0.50 in the sectors market (0.20 and 0.45 in the mixed), and 0.00 for its pairs and 4 of all; medians of one call on one core, measured on the laptop with the other seeds' choices running, so to compare with each other and not as times to expect. Every chooser gave the rows asked for on every seed (8.0 pairs or 4.0 baskets a seed), Johansen's too, in the mixed market where the best triple only nearly is a relation.
   - **What it finds: the test** (both markets, seeds 101 to 112 with section 3's burn and span; 2.5 lots of each pair and 5 of each basket's largest leg, deciding every 5 units; P&L in ticks times lots and its standard error across seeds; every row positive on 12 of 12 seeds in both markets). Each chooser's entry and rate were chosen on seeds 1 to 8 as section 5's were, by P&L summed over the two markets, from entries of 0.75, 1, 1.5 and 2 and rates of 1, 0.25 and 0.1 (a grid the notebook does not print), but for two rows: the baskets row that was the dashboard's default then, and Box-Tiao's 4 of 4 at an entry of 1, set like for like with Johansen's. None is as much as two standard errors from 0. Per unit of risk the 4 of 3 differ by -0.002 (se 0.002, 5 of 12) and +0.000 (se 0.002, 6 of 12), and the 4 of 4 are behind, -0.006 (se 0.002, 2 of 12) and -0.007 (se 0.002, 1 of 12), for the same P&L on more lots (11,133 and 9,135 against 9,166 and 8,156) and more held (32.8 and 28.1 lots against 28.0 and 26.8). Part of that is the weights: an independent check outside the notebook found that for one lot of the largest leg Box-Tiao's baskets of 4 hold more lots in their other legs than Johansen's (in the mixed market many of Johansen's have a leg too small to hold at this size), and that the extra lots add risk and costs without adding P&L (the picks show their swing per lot lower, 2.31 and 2.26 against 2.49 and 2.62). The picks also show Box-Tiao's baskets of 4 spanning fewer books (9.4 and 10.2 against 12.0 and 12.0) and, in the sectors market, sharing more of them (3.0 against 2.4; in the mixed market 2.6 against 2.5 is one book on one seed of eight), so some of its four are near-copies of one relation, as the same check found on seed 101 in the sectors market, which a count of books does not measure. That check found that on four seeds the extra lots held account for all of the extra risk of Box-Tiao's trader in the mixed market and for most of it in the sectors market, where the overlap adds a little, within the noise of four seeds, so the lots, not the books, are the larger part of the gap. The baskets of 3 span fewer books too (8.9 and 9.6 against 10.0 and 10.1) but share the same, and per unit of risk the two do not differ. **This holds for the seeds drawn.** Johansen's chooser can hand a trader a basket with no relation in it (it takes first the baskets that share no book with a better one, so its fourth of four baskets of 3 from twelve books can be a triple apart from the first three, taken whenever its trace statistic passes the 95% value). The picks, which are the traded baskets on seeds 101 to 108 (the notebook's assert covers one chooser on seed 101, as above), show none of Johansen's 4 of 3 leaving more than 0.050 in the sectors market (0.012 on average, against Box-Tiao's 0.022 and at most 0.083), and 0.087 on average and at most 0.548 in the mixed (against Box-Tiao's 0.055 and 0.168); the notebook does not show what the other four seeds picked, or how much of a paired difference, none of these as much as two standard errors from 0, comes from any one basket.

On seeds 1 to 8, the seeds that chose the entry, the same comparison went the other way in the sectors market, by under two standard errors (an independent check outside the notebook, whose figures it does not print), so it is not an edge they showed; and entering at 1.5 instead of 1 gives Box-Tiao's 4 of 4 about the same P&L on about two thirds of the lots (+6,984 on 7,207 lots against +6,697 on 11,133 in the sectors market; unpaired).

Per unit of risk it is -0.000 (se 0.001, 4 of 12) and +0.002 (se 0.001, 7 of 12): it makes more mostly because it holds more. **These settings are now the Baskets default** (the Stat-arb view paragraph above), at the user's word on 2026-11-11, after stage 3.

In the sectors market no basket row reaches the pairs' +8,639, and the pairs' 0.068 per unit of risk is above every basket row's 0.029 to 0.042; in the mixed market every basket row but the entry-2 one (the default when the section was run) is above the pairs' +4,484, at +5,925 to +6,839, on 6,086 to 9,779 lots against 3,673 and with 1.1 to 2.1 lots of exposure to the factors against 3.7, as no pair there is a relation. **Box-Tiao's pairs** make less than pairs by distance in the sectors market and about the same in the mixed, -469 (se 134, 1 of 12) and +207 (se 254, 7 of 12), and a little more per unit of risk in both, +0.003 (se 0.001, 8 of 12) and +0.009 (se 0.003, 9 of 12); its pairs span all 12 books on every seed and pairs by distance 9.2 and 9.0, and they hold about the same lots (19.0 and 18.7 against 18.8 and 19.0), so here the chooser that spans more books is a little ahead per unit of risk on the same lots (whether that is why is not tested). **The 4 of all**, the least predictable chooser, makes the least of the baskets' rows but the entry-2 one (+5,984 and +5,925, on 10,562 and 9,779 lots), and holds the most exposure in the sectors market, 1.6 lots.

The table of picks says the same in the mixed market (a leftover of 0.055 against 0.087 at 3 legs, 0.043 against 0.056 at 4), where the leftover is the larger part of what is held.
