In the sectors market the picks go the other way (0.022 against 0.012 at 3 legs, 0.026 against 0.015 at 4), but every chooser's leftover there is small (0.012 to 0.036 on average, at most 0.084) and what a trader holds of the factors is whole lots that do not net to 0 within a sector: a vector of one sector rounded to lots is an exact relation when its legs round to a net of 0 and a lot of exposure when they do not, and with a rate of 0.25 the position is the rounded aim on its way to a rounded target. An independent check outside the notebook found that on seeds 101 and 102 the aim of either 4 of 3 trader held no exposure to the factors while its position held some, more for Johansen's than for Box-Tiao's, and that Box-Tiao's is the lower because many of its baskets of 3 are pairs as held (their third legs, small weights from another sector, round to no lot) and many of its baskets of 4 are two near-equal pairs, which round cleanly where Johansen's within-sector vectors do not; Johansen's 4 of 3 hold the most of any basket row in the mixed market, 2.1 lots, and leave the most of any chooser of baskets, 0.087. **A lot costs about a tick in every row with a rate**, 1.00 to 1.04 in the sectors market and 0.95 to 0.99 in the mixed, and more in the default's.
   - **One card** (Box-Tiao's four baskets of 3 books, entry 1, rate 0.25, 5 lots of the largest leg of each, deciding every 5 units; the mixed market, seed 101, which the cards of sections 4 and 5 did not use; the form of section 4's). It is the row with the most P&L in the mixed market. Its record makes +7,818 and its fills made +7,822, 4 apart where section 5's cards were within 1 and section 4's 2 to 8; it holds at most 0.95 of its capital of 150,000, above 1 on 0.0% of days, so its figure is not leveraged. The table's figure for the same run, over the last 20,000 units only, is +7,684, against +6,839 for the mean of the twelve seeds. The limits of section 6 are in section 7.
9. **An index, deals and a trend (stage 4: the library).** The market of notebooks 12 and 13 has nothing that continues, no book that is the sum of others and no event, so three of the eight strategies agreed with the user had nothing to trade: index arbitrage, a merger-style trade on deals, and momentum that can pay. Stage 4 gives `FactorValues` three options (`lob/statarb.py`, `lob/deals.py`), each with a dial that starts at, or near, nothing to find (the index's does not quite: `index_volatility` 0 still leaves a premium, as the bullet on what the reviews established says), and three traders to turn them against (`lob/indexarb.py`, `lob/deal_traders.py`). The question for each, which notebook 15 asks (10), is how far the dial must be turned before the trade pays its costs. The specification had two reviews before anything was built (the bullet on what the reviews and the audit established, and the history entry for 2026-11-12), and the numbers below that are marked seeds 1 to 8 were measured on the seeds settings are chosen on.
   - **The trend** (`FactorValues(trend=0.0, trend_half_life=500.0)`; `tests/test_trend.py`). `trend` is one number, or one a factor, each 0 or more: the standard deviation of each factor's drift, in ticks a unit of time. A drift m_j is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process around 0, m_j(t + h) = d m_j(t) + trend_j sqrt(1 - d^2) z with d = exp(-ln 2 h / `trend_half_life`) and z standard normal, and a factor's move over a step of h gains m_j h. It starts from its stationary law (m_j = trend_j z), so no burn is wanted for it, and it has a stream of its own, `default_rng([seed, 4])`, drawn in blocks of `(BLOCK, k)`; with every trend 0 nothing is made or drawn and `_move` is what it was. With a volatility of 1, a drift sd of 0.05 and the half-life of 500, about a fifth of the variance of a move over 100 units is drift (0.193 in closed form, 0.193 simulated, se 0.0002) and one such move correlates 0.176 with the next (0.177, se 0.0008); over the default trader's own horizon, 100 decisions of 5 units, the drift is half the variance (0.501) and successive moves correlate 0.32, so that a trend of 0.05 is an easy test for a momentum trader and the part of the dial that tells something is below it (the premise review's reading). **The point, and its limit.** The own parts are stationary (they must be, or no relation exists), so the factors are the only thing that can continue, and momentum here can pay only by holding a factor. `MomentumTrader(factors=0)` does: in the sectors market, on seeds 1 to 8, the correlation of what it holds of each sector (less the three sectors' mean) with that sector's move over the 500 units before is +0.54 at a trend of 0 and +0.69 at 0.05, with about 20 lots of exposure to the factors of about 31 held. With `factors=3` it takes the trending factors out and loses, trend or no trend (-2,339, se 162, and -2,258, se 185, on seeds 1 to 8; its factor part +49 and +171). So the claim, as the sheet has it, is how the market is built and not something a run finds: here the only thing that can continue is a factor, which is Ehsani and Linnainmaa's (2022) account of momentum, and the market has no room for the other reading in the literature, momentum in the residuals (Blitz, Huij and Martens 2011; Grundy and Martin 2001). A trend in the own parts is not built: as an integrated drift it would make every own part wander and end every relation (it is `loading_sd` by another name), and one that keeps them stationary (an own part whose shocks are themselves persistent, reverting more slowly than the drift's half-life) would change what reversal and pairs see at the half-life of 50 (reasoned, not simulated).
   - **The index book** (`FactorValues(index=..., index_volatility=0.0)`; `tests/test_index_book.py`). `index` is `True` (the sum of the books) or one weight a book, each 0 or more and adding up to 1 or more. The market then has one more book, the last (`m` books are the others and `n = m + 1`), whose fair value is F_idx = c . F + P, the weighted sum of the others' and a premium P. Its loadings are c . B, its level c . level and its own part c . U + P, so `(-c, 1)` is a relation whose spread is the weighted own parts and the premium, and every relation among the books is still one. **The index is a book like the others, `scale = sum(c)` times over** (`FactorValues.scales`): its model reaches 8 ticks times the scale (`AnchoredZIModel(reach=max(1, round(8 * scale)))`, and its starting book is spaced by the scale), its premium is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process with the books' half-life and the standard deviation of an own part of volatility `index_volatility` times the scale, and **what a lot taken does** is this: a lot taken in a book moves the index by its weight times what it moved the book, and a lot taken in the index moves its premium by `scale * impact` and nothing else. The premium has a stream of its own, `[seed, 3]`, one block of normals a refill whatever `index_volatility` is, so that the dial is the same shocks scaled. The books' factors and shocks are those of the same seed without the index (the draws and the slices of `z` use `m`), but in a market their order flow is not, and through the impact their fair values are not either (the flow has one stream for all the books, and one more book changes which book each event falls to: the same fair values, from the first sample, differ by an sd of 1.6 to 1.7 ticks), so a comparison by seed is paired in the shocks only. The floor looks at the `m` books and holds the index from below without pushing it, so `F_idx = c . F + P` holds above the floor, and a lot taken can leave the index under it until the next step, as it can a book. With `index_volatility` 0 **the premium is not 0**: the order flow's own market orders in the index book move its fair value and make a premium with a standard deviation of about 1.2 ticks a unit of scale at any `index_volatility` (0.05 * sqrt(1.5 * 11 / (2 ln 2 / 50)) = 1.22; seeds 1 to 8, bare markets: 14.8 ticks for the sum index and 1.17 for weights of 1/12), so the dial's first step, 0.2, adds as much premium as is already there. A premium of exactly 0 takes `index_volatility=0` and `impact=0` both (the strict control), and the dial is labeled by the premium's sd in all and not by `index_volatility`.
   - **Two conventions for the weights, and why the library takes any.** The first design had weights of 1/m, scale 1: an index quoted like a single book, so that replicating it takes m lots of the index against one of each book. The premise review found that the scale is no convention but decides the answer (seeds 1 to 8, the sectors market, the same premium, rule and exposure): with weights of 1/12 the dial's break-even was near an `index_volatility` of 0.4 and with the sum, one lot of the index against one of each book, below 0. The difference is what the index leg costs. Twelve lots in one ordinary book cost about 18 ticks (1.43 to 1.53 a lot, as the order walks the book) against 12 for the basket's twelve one-lot orders in twelve books, where one lot of the scaled book cost 7.7: with weights of 1/m the index is the dear leg, the reverse of an index future, and a headline number for strategy 6 would be a statement about that choice. A mid follows a fair value of 14 ticks a root unit as closely as one of 1.15 (the same R^2 at every horizon, a shorter lag), so the first design's argument for it, that the order flow could not follow a sum index, did not hold; what a thin book does at reach 8 is a spread of 13 ticks and an empty side now and then. Weights of 1/m also hold their hedge only at multiples of m, since each leg is rounded to whole lots on its own (weights of 1/12: sizes 1 to 6 hold no hedge, 7 to 17 one lot of each book, 1.71 times the hedge asked for at 7 and 0.71 times at 17, and 18 to 30 two), and a rate below 1 breaks it, the index leg leading and the hedge arriving in one lump (no hedge on 9% to 50% of the decisions with index lots, at rates of 0.5 and 0.25). **So `index=True` is the sum**, where one lot of the index against one of each book is exact at any size and any rate, and **the library takes any weights** (0 or more, adding up to 1 or more: a sum under 1 is a book quoted in parts of a tick, whose bid and ask fall at one price, and is refused; a sum within a billionth of 1 is 1, so that `[1/7] * 7` is a book like the others), so that the notebook can show both, because what the index leg costs decides whether replication pays.
   - **The deals** (`lob/deals.py`, `FactorValues(deals=Deals(...))`; `tests/test_deals.py`). An acquirer A offers `ratio` r of its shares for each of a target T's. While the deal is on the target's fair value is a mix of what the offer is worth, r F_A, and what the target is worth alone, S_T (its level, factors and own part, which go on moving underneath): F_T = pi r F_A + (1 - pi) S_T, with pi = exp(-q (time left)) the probability of completion the market prices. It prices a break at a constant hazard q = -ln(1 - `priced`) / `length`, so pi is 1 - `priced` at the announcement; the deal in fact breaks, at the true hazard p = -ln(1 - `breaks`) / `length`, at a time e / p after its announcement (e one standard exponential) if that is at or before its end, and then F_T falls back to S_T at once. At the end the deal completes: the target's level takes the difference r F_A - S_T, so that it goes on from the offer's value with its own loadings and own part, and its fair value does not jump. **Announcement.** r = (S_T + `premium`) / F_A, and the fair value jumps by pi_0 times the premium, so that the gap r F_A - F_T opens at (1 - pi_0) times it: 24 ticks with a premium of 60 and `priced` 0.4. At each grid step one uniform is drawn, and below `rate * step` three more, whatever follows (one for the target, one for the acquirer and e), so the number of draws depends on time alone. **The settings**, `Deals` (frozen, and one may be given to any number of markets): `rate` 0.002 announcements a unit of time over the whole market (a rate times the step of 1 or more is refused, so at most one a step), `premium` 60 ticks, `length` 500 units (rounded to a whole number of the fair values' steps, at least one, and the hazards use the rounded length so that the share that breaks is exactly `breaks`), `breaks` 0.2 and `priced` 0.2 (each from 0 to below 1), `cool` 100 and `within` True. The library's own deals have `priced` equal to `breaks` (nothing to earn); the view's have 0.4 and 0.2 (3S.11). **Who.** The target is drawn among the books in no deal and past their cooling time (never the index); the acquirer among the same books other than the target, kept to those whose row of loadings is parallel to the target's when `within` and there are any (the same sector, in a table of sectors), else among all (a mixed table has no parallel rows; with many deals at once the target's sector may have no free book and the acquirer then comes from another, which happened in 0 of 3,179 deals at a rate of 0.002 over 200,000 units a seed, seeds 1 to 8); with fewer than two books free nothing is announced. Within a sector because across sectors a target's exposures become a mix of two sectors' for the deal's life and switch back at completion, so that what the pairs see could not be told from a change of factor structure, and the gap's wander over a whole deal is 3.5 times as large (33.5 against 9.7 with a premium of 60, seeds 1 to 8, across two sectors); within a sector the rows stay parallel, and `rank` and every relation but the target's own level are untouched. **A lot taken** in the target moves its own part by the impact as always and its fair value by (1 - pi) times it, and a lot in its acquirer moves the target by pi r times it: impact is through S_T alone, since a target near completion is a claim on r acquirer shares and pressure in it should not move its value. A completion also moves the index's level by its weight times the shift.
   - **The cooling time, and why a broken deal's books stay out until its planned end.** A book is in one deal at a time and is free for a new one from the deal's planned end plus `cool`, whether it completed there or broke before it. That is so that the dial of breaks has the same deals at every value: `breaks` is turned with `priced` held fixed (since `priced` sets pi_0, so the announcement's jump and the gap, and with them what a fixed entry in ticks does, the rows of a dial of `priced` would be different markets), and a break time is e / p for the one exponential e, so the deals that break at a lower `breaks` are some of those that break at a higher one, deal for deal. The audit found that with a broken deal's books freed at the break plus `cool`, the first deal that broke at one value and not at another freed its books at another time, and the same uniforms then picked other books: in the sectors market of 3 sectors of 4 books, at a rate of 0.002, a premium of 60, a length of 500, a cooling time of 100 and `priced` 0.4 (20,000 units, seeds 1 to 8, 329 deals at every value) the share of deals shared with `breaks` 0.2, by time, target and acquirer, was 0.915 at 0.0, 0.976 at 0.1, 0.897 at 0.3, 0.836 at 0.4 and 0.769 at 0.6, and with the books out until the planned end 329 of 329 at every value. So which books are free depends on the time alone, and two markets of one seed that differ in `breaks` alone have the same deals (the same two books, announced at the same times) but for how each ends, and so not the same ratios, jumps or levels, which follow the fair values. A break is applied at the first grid point at or after its time (a break in the last step breaks), and a deal that ends on the grid completes there.
   - **A jump shifts the book** (`BookMarket._shift`). At an announcement and at a break a target's fair value jumps by tens of ticks, and a book follows its fair value only through the anchored traders' crossing limit orders, which take about 10 units to eat one side of the book whatever the jump. So whoever trades first takes the jump less about 1.3 ticks a lot, and a jump is free money unless the book is shifted. Measured by the premise review on seeds 1 to 8 (twelve sectors books, every 100 units every book's fair value moved from outside by 15, 25 or 40 ticks, 960 jumps of each size, nobody trading): what a 5-lot order in the jump's direction earns against the new fair value at the first event is +68.7 (se 0.5), +119.1 (0.6) and +193.6 (0.6), 13.7, 23.8 and 38.7 a lot, and still +41.9, +88.4 and +161.2 five units later; real orders through `BookMarket._trade` agreed (+68.4, se 0.9, for 15). So at each step, after `fair.advance_to` and before the event is drawn and before any trader decides, every book in `fair.gapped` has all its resting orders moved by the jump, rounded: the same orders, ids kept, time priority kept within and across prices, a bid no lower than 1 tick and an ask no lower than 2 so that none meets another, a jump under half a tick moving nothing. The book is changed directly and not through `submit`, so it counts no event, flow, volume or impact; its rate is taken again, `total` is taken again before the event's uniform is drawn (the draws are the same, and the event cannot fall through to the last book on the old rates) and `mark` is called. With nothing gapped `step` is what it was. `gapped` takes (target, +pi_0 times the premium) at an announcement and (target, -pi times the gap) at a break, and the index, when a book it holds jumps, with its weight times the jump summed over the step, when that is half a tick or more. **What it does**, in the same market at a rate of 0.002, a premium of 60, a length of 500, `priced` 0.4 and `breaks` 0.2, on seeds 1 to 8 (482 announcements and 97 breaks over 30,000 units a seed, nobody trading): a 5-lot order in the jump's direction at the first event after an announcement earns -1.55 a lot (se 0.16) against the fair value, against -1.24 in ordinary times, and after a break -2.13 (0.34); with the shift turned off (a rate of 0.004, 236 announcements and 53 breaks, 8,000 units a seed) it earns +34.80 (0.22) and +45.05 (2.05). The sum index's book: -12.26 (1.51) after an announcement against -10.44 in ordinary times, and +27.35 (2.01) with the shift off. Nothing is handed to anyone, at the first event or at 0.5, 1, 2.5, 5, 10 or 15 units later, and a trader deciding at every event (346,854 decisions) never saw `gapped` with anything in it: the book has always shifted before a new deal or its outcome can be read. A rebuilt book, the other way to do it, was given up: it hands a trader a little (a 5-lot order paid 1.00 a lot in it against 1.24, for 2 to 5 units, since its mid is the fair value to half a tick where a book's is normally 2.5 to 2.8 ticks off).
   - **Off by default, a stream each, and the proof.** Each option is behind a branch the default never enters: `reseed` makes `[seed, 4]` only with a trend, `[seed, 3]` only with an index and `[seed, 5]` only with deals, and nothing is made, drawn from or read otherwise, so the default path through `_move` and `trade` has the lines it had, behind branches it does not enter, and no default array changes shape or becomes a vector. What each draws depends on time alone (the trend a block of `(BLOCK, k)` a refill, the premium a block of `BLOCK` whatever `index_volatility` is, the deals one uniform a step and three more at an announcement), so who is in a deal, and when it is announced, ends or breaks, are the same whatever trades (the same seed bare and heavily traded, 16 of 16 pairs: the deals' timeline, the drift path, the premium's shocks and all four generators' end states equal). What depends on trading is a deal's ratio and the completion's shift, the premium, the own parts and the fair values, and none of it feeds back into a draw. **With all three off the market is that of 42ae383 to the tick.** A harness that reads `lob/statarb.py` and `lob/crosssection.py` out of git at 42ae383 and plays the same scenarios in both (kept outside the repository; 13 scenarios, a bare market and each kind of trader alone and several together, on each of seeds 1 to 8 for 1,500 units) made 104 comparisons and none differs, in the event frame bit for bit, the events and time, both generators' bit states, the flow and volume, the fair values and their parts, the rates, every resting order, and each trader's account, lots, spread paid, entries, netted lots, position, states and score. The view's default market, against the library's at 42ae383 (30 quantities on seeds 1 and 2), and notebook 13's traders on seed 1 (the whole `score_portfolio` dict) are equal too. `tests/test_stage4_identity.py` holds the same comparison in the suite, against `git show` (skipped if git cannot show it), and holds `lob/book.py`, `lob/pairs.py`, `lob/order.py` and `lob/matching.py` byte-equal to 42ae383. The proof was itself broken seven ways on copies (the own parts' decay times by 1 - 1e-9, one more draw a block, an extra draw in `step`, `ddof=1` in the deviation, `exposures()` returning a copy, `book_cash` as a dataclass field, the exposure read from the mean loadings) and each was caught, `exposures()` returning a copy only by the test of its identity and the mean loadings only by the identity test's market with drifting loadings, which no script or earlier test saw, since a value-based comparison cannot see an array that was copied.
   - **The traders** (`lob/indexarb.py`, `lob/deal_traders.py`). **`IndexTrader`** is a `RelationTrader` (the z-score of its spread over `window` decisions, short above `entry`, long below minus it, flat at `exit`) with two more fields. `legs` 0 trades the one relation of every book at the index's published weights (`replicate_index`: `(-c, 1)` with its largest leg 1), and `legs` above 0 the one relation `index_basket` finds from that many books in the last `formation` decisions' mids (forward selection on levels, by least squares with a constant, each time adding the book that lowers the residual sum of squares most). `hedged` off reads the same spread and z-score, so the same states and entries, and holds the index leg alone, which carries what the index holds of the factors. `size` is lots of the relation's largest leg: baskets, with the sum index. It needs no `choose` or `weights` and refuses them; `check` (which `BookMarket.add` calls, and the view's start) takes the weights from the market and refuses a market with no index book ("this market has no index book") or more legs than books; `formation` is the window unless given, so `learn`, `warming` and `warm_up` work as for any trader that chooses. A refit chooses again, and the full replication, with a leg in every book, goes flat at each and takes a position again when its z-score says so. **A sample needs as many legs as the books have factors** (seeds 1 to 8): on levels, forward selection picks one book a sector with 3 legs (coefficients 0.31 to 0.34 of the scale, 0.04 of a factor left in the spread), and with 1 or 2 legs leaves a factor in, whose fit within the history looks tighter than it is after it (sd of the spread 13.8 in the history and 26.6 after with one leg, 8.1 and 15.7 with two, 3.3 and 3.9 with three); in the mixed market one book alone nearly is the index (0.083 left), so fewer legs than factors is not a clean line there. At the default `formation`, the window of 400, a sample's hedge was sized for 0.73 to 0.95 of its index lot, and at a large premium the index leg rounded to 0 lots on two seeds of eight; the triage of the exploration reran it with `formation=2000`. **`DealTrader`** (a `StateTrader`) has a row for each deal that is on, +1 at the target and -r at the acquirer, not rescaled, and a state each (1 in the deal, 0 out), made again whenever the deals on change, a deal that goes on keeping its state (a deal is told by its target, acquirer and the time it was announced). It enters a deal whose gap in mids, r * mid_A - mid_T, is at least `entry` ticks (8 in the library, 0 on the view), holding `size` lots of the target and `rint(size * r)` of the acquirer short, and stays in until the deal is over; then it closes both **all the way at that decision, whatever its `rate` and `band`**, and again at each decision until both are flat. `limit` holds at most that many deals at once, the widest gap first (0: any number). Each deal it entered becomes a `DealRecord` (target, acquirer, announced, entered, ended, outcome, `lots`, the most it held of the target, `pnl` and `flat`, the time of the first decision at which both books were flat) once it is over and flat: `pnl` is the change in `book_cash` of the two books from the decision it entered at to that one, the spread paid in it, and is the deal's alone if both books were flat at entry. They are, unless closing is slow, since a book is out of the next deal for `cool` units after the planned end; a deal entered while a book of its own was not flat, and any deal still being closed in those books, is counted in `not_flat_at_entry` and has no record (it was 0 in every run), and a deal over before the trader decided is never entered. `signal()` is the gaps in ticks, `levels` is `(entry, 0.0)`, and it has nothing to learn (`learn` takes none, `warming` is None) and starts at its first decision. It is Mitchell and Pulvino's (2001) trade, a little most of the time and a large loss when a deal breaks. At fair values, with `priced` equal to `breaks`, one lot of the target, unhedged, from just after the announcement to the end earns 0 on average (+0.011, se 0.088, at 20%, and -0.107, se 0.093, at 40%, within a sector, 200,000 deals each); hedged with r lots of the acquirer, a completed deal earns (1 - pi_0) times the premium exactly (+24 with a premium of 60 and `priced` 0.4, on every one of 119,641 to 160,158 deals) and a broken one -pi_0 G_0 - (G(t) - G_0) with G = r F_A - S_T (-36 at the break and -35.97, se 0.03 to 0.12, on average), so that with `priced` 0.4 and `breaks` 0.2 it is worth +12 (+12.055, se 0.054, simulated; on average over deals, with no trend): the tests hold these at fair values, where they are exact, and in mids hold the same lot pair's before-cost figure to 12 within three standard errors, its lag behind the fair values to between 1 and 4 ticks and the spread its four orders pay to between 3 and 5. **`NewsAwareTrader`** is a `RelationTrader` that is flat in every relation holding a book that is the target of a deal that is on or ended less than `quarantine` ago, and stays flat until then, and trades the rest as a `RelationTrader` does (number for number when there are no deals, and with `Deals(rate=0)`, deals that never come). A deal moves its target's fair value by tens of ticks for a reason that has nothing to do with the relation a pair was chosen for, and a completed deal never comes back. The default `quarantine` is `taking(window)`, 412 units at a window of 400 and an interval of 1 and 2,012 at an interval of 5, since the window takes each decision's wait for the next event too (it was `window * interval`, which left two or three decisions from before the deal's end in the window when the quarantine ended). **Only the target's relations are quiet by default (`acquirers=False`)**, because only the target's fair value moves with a deal here: on seeds 1 to 8 (a trader of 8 pairs of 2.5 lots alone in the sectors market, deals at a rate of 0.002 with `priced` 0.4 and `breaks` 0.4, a quarantine of 2,012 units, 20,000 units after a burn of 10,465) a reader quiet in the target alone was +1,064 (se 108) better than one that also kept out of the acquirer, which bought nothing; `acquirers=True` is the rule for a market where acquirers move too, as in a real one. With a `rate` below 1 it closes a quarantined relation at its rate and is not flat at once.
   - **`score_holdings`, and what `pnl - factor_part` holds.** `score_holdings(market, trader, burn, span, horizon=100.0)` gives what `score_portfolio` gives (which now runs through a shared `_score`, with the same keys and the same values, and reads `market.fair.exposures()` where it read `loadings`) and `deviation_long`, the sd of the P&L's changes over successive stretches of `horizon` units (a whole number of 1 or more) over the square root of `horizon`; `risk_adjusted_long`, the mean change a unit over that (both 0 with fewer than two stretches); and `factor_part`, the sum over the span's unit samples of what the trader held of each factor at the sample before (`position @ market.fair.exposures()`) times that factor's move since (`fair.factors`). The reason: `score_portfolio`'s `deviation` and `risk_adjusted` are of one-unit changes, over which a mid barely moves with its fair value (R^2 0.01), so for a trader that holds factors they flatter it and shortchange a neutral one: on seeds 1 to 8 a factor holder's sd of P&L changes a root unit is 9.5 to 10.1 over one unit and 20.3 to 25.6 over 500 and a neutral trader's 7.2 and 4.7, so the one-unit figure flatters the first by 2.1 to 2.5 times and shortchanges the second by 1.5 to 1.7 (`deviation_long` is 22.8 against a `deviation` of 10.1 for momentum with no factors, and 6.3 against 7.3 with three). With a trend of 0 the factor part is 0 on average (the factors are random walks, and what is held is decided before they move), so a stretch that shows otherwise is chance: on seeds 1 to 8 the first 6,000 units after the burn favor a momentum trader's factor part by about +1,200 (+1,189, se 282, 8 of 8, with no deals and no trend; over the next 6,000 units -94, se 258, with deals and +292, se 306, without), and no setting should be chosen or judged on such a stretch alone. **`pnl - factor_part` is the rest, and is not the own parts and the costs alone** (the first docstring said so, and the audit corrected it): it holds the books' own parts (an index's premium among them), what deals did to what the trader held (the jumps, the drift of pi, the shifts of the levels), what its orders made or lost by trading at mids that were off their fair values together with its own impact on the lots it had just taken, the spread paid, and the change between the span's two ends in what its position is marked off the fair values. Over 6,000 units after a burn of 2,600 on seeds 1 to 8 (mean, se): momentum with no factors in a trend of 0.05 has a rest of -806 (122) and own parts less costs of -1,050 (89), +244 (81) apart; a `DealTrader` (deals within sectors at a rate of 0.004, `priced` 0.4 and `breaks` 0.2) a rest of +534 (173) against -859 (81), +1,393 (199) apart, which is the deals. Of the first, about half is fair value less mid where the orders traded (+103, se 75) and half the trader's own impact on the lots just taken (+96, se 7); the column is to be called the rest, and its parts not named. The factor part itself is as stated: against the exposures before each grid step times that step's factor moves, it differs by +13 (se 21) of 4,782, +15 (se 19) of 3,139 with drifting loadings and +39 (se 23) of 634 with deals across sectors (`within` off, at the same rate and chances), where `exposures()` jumps within a unit.
   - **`exposures()`, `book_cash`, `check` and the Johansen guard.** `FactorValues.exposures()` is what a lot of each book holds of each factor, books by factors, as they are now: **the `loadings` array itself, not a copy, while no deal is on** (a test holds that, and `score_portfolio` through it keeps every number). With deals on it is a copy in which a target's row is pi r (its acquirer's row) + (1 - pi) (its own), the same mix its fair value is of theirs, and the index's row is c times the books' rows after that. `BookTrader.attach` makes `book_cash`, one number a book (a plain attribute like `_aim`, not a dataclass field, which would move the position of every subclass's fields), and `BookMarket._trade` adds each fill to `trader.book_cash[book]` beside `trader.cash`, which stays its own running sum: what each book's fills paid into the cash, negative for a buy (a test holds the sum equal to `cash` for each of 12 kinds). `BookTrader.check(market)` does nothing, and `BookMarket.add` calls it before `attach`: a trader that needs something of the market (`IndexTrader`, an index book; `DealTrader`, deals) raises `ValueError` there, with a sentence a user can read, and is not added. **`johansen_relations` raises `ValueError` for more than `JOHANSEN_BOOKS` = 12 books.** statsmodels (0.14.6) has Johansen critical values for 12 variables at most; with 13 or more `cvt` is NaN, `lr1[0] <= nan` is False and `trace_rank` counted a rank from it (on one draw of unrelated random walks, a truth of 0, it counted 1 in 13 books and 4 in 16, and 1 in 12 books, where the table exists, as well). So the market of 4 sectors of 4 books (16) was already wrong at 42ae383, in What the tests see and in a Baskets trader of all the books by Johansen; the guard is a repair off the default path, no run of 12 books or fewer changes, and `best_baskets` (a few books at a time) and `sparse_baskets` work with any number. An index makes the default 3 sectors of 4 books a market of 13.
   - **What the reviews and the audit established, that a reader of the code needs.** **No rule that trades a spread of mids earns nothing before costs**, so "earns nothing before costs" is no control's test here: the mids' own bounce and lag are in every before-cost figure of such a rule, and a rule marked at mids earns each reversion that nobody can trade (the index's rule, applied offline to the mids' spread with the premium exactly 0, made +1,770, se 67, on 57 entries a run of 8,000 units, seeds 1 to 8, from a spread whose sd is 0.83, where the same rule paid about 70 for each entry; 12 lots of an index of weights 1/12, on a prototype of the index book built outside the library). A control is judged by "loses after costs, on every seed" and by differences along the dial paired by seed. **The two index conventions are one position and not one trade.** In fair values they are the same market (the factors, own parts and the books' fair values equal to the bit, the premium of the sum index 12 times the other's to 1e-13) and hold the same position at every sample, but a basket's index leg costs 7.8 ticks to cross in the sum index against 17.9 in the 1/12 one, and for five baskets 10.6 a basket against 35.1, and the trader's own lots move the premium it trades 12 times as much in ticks of a basket (a full entry of s baskets pushes it by 0.6 s ticks in the sum index and 7.2 s in the other, against a premium sd of 32.6 in both). At one basket they differ before costs by -104 (se 68) and in costs by -609 (se 83), paired by seed on seeds 1 to 8 with `index_volatility` 0.4: the index leg's cost is the difference. At five baskets the 1/12 row made 31.5 entries against 16.8 and +4,549 (se 710) more before costs, and with `impact=0` 19.1 against 17.1 and +657 (se 299), so the extra is its own push: the two are compared at one basket, the leg's cost is quoted for a basket and not a lot, and the 1/12 row's before-cost column and entries are not those of the same trade. **A deal trader's records at a run's end lean toward broken deals.** `outcomes` holds a deal once it is over and flat, and a deal that breaks is over early where one that completes takes its whole length, so of the deals announced in the last `length` of a run the records hold the breaks and not the completions: their mean lot pair, and their share broken, lean toward breaks, and the scorecard's P&L, which marks the open deals, does not. Counted from the library's deal history over 200,000 units a seed (`breaks` 0.2, where a lot pair is worth +11.9 at fair values), the mean is low by 0.44 at a span of 10,000 and by 1.25 at 3,000, and by 0.54 and 1.87 at `breaks` 0.4, and the share broken among deals over by the end is 0.209 against 0.202 announced at 10,000 and 0.223 against 0.202 at 3,000. So a per-deal figure takes only the deals announced at least `length` and two decisions before the end. **The target's mid lags its rising fair value.** The target's fair value drifts by q pi G a unit while a deal is on (18.4 / `length` to 30.7 / `length` ticks a unit) and a mid lags its fair value by about 8 units, so the trader enters before the lag has built and leaves while it is there: lot pair in mids less at fair values, from the first sample after the announcement to the first after the end, -2.21 (se 0.12) at a length of 100 and -0.62 (0.13) at 500 (seeds 1 to 8, nobody trading, `priced` 0.4 and `breaks` 0.2; 714 deals at a rate of 0.008 and 476 at 0.002), 2.6% of a completed deal's 24; in a market (deals at a rate of 0.004, 8,600 units a seed) a `DealTrader`'s records made +18.66 (se 0.26, n 182) a completed lot pair and -42.94 (se 1.02, n 58) a broken one, against +24 and about -37 at fair values. It goes as 1 over the length, so a dial in `length` would have it built in, and below a length of about 100 the catch-up after a completion is worth more than a round trip costs (reasoned, not measured). **Completed deals lift the books' levels.** Each completed deal adds its r F_A - S_T (about the premium) to the target's level for good and nothing takes it from the acquirer: the twelve levels gain 0.0948 (se 0.0022) ticks a unit in all at the defaults, which is `rate` times (1 - `breaks`) times the premium (0.096), about +950 on one lot of every book over 10,000 units, and less the higher `breaks` is. It is not in `fair.factors`, so `factor_part` does not hold it: it is in the rest of anything that is net long or short, which a dollar-neutral trader holds none of on average. **`index_volatility=0` still has a premium** (above), so no row of the dial is "nothing to find". **A deal's ratio is far from 1 near the floor.** `premium` is in ticks whatever the price, and r = (S_T + `premium`) / F_A: at a level of 3,000 it is 1.02, and at a level of 40 and a volatility of 3 it ran from 0.5 to 9.0, a target with a ratio above 3 sitting 18.3 ticks from its mid on average, so deals mean little there (the view's level is 1,000). **What no library trader reads:** `Deal._breaks_at` is private by name only, and `fair.deal_settings.breaks` and `fair.drift` are public, so a trader written against them would know what no trader can. **The features compose:** 69 runs of 3,000 units (every pair of the three and all three; plain, with loadings that drift, with no half-life and at the floor; the index as the sum and as 1/m; random, momentum, deal, news-aware and index traders), checked after every grid step and every order that took lots, had no exception, no crossed or locked book and no non-finite value, and every identity (the mix, the index's fair value against `max(floor, c . F + P)`, the index's `own`, `common`, `level` and `loadings`) held to 1e-6; the notebook's sections turn on one feature each, and the view allows all three.
   - **What it leaves out.** A fixed-weights index trader with no chooser (a chooser is what `learn` and `warming` work through), an index half-life of its own (the premium takes the books'), the rebuild of a book at a jump (the shift replaced it) and a fit grid for the deals kind (3S.11). Section 7 says what the model leaves out.
10. **What notebook 15 finds.** Each of its three sections turns on one feature of 3S.9 and nothing else, in notebook 12's two tables (the index and the deals in the sectors market, the deals again in the mixed market at three values of their dial, the trend in both), with every trader alone, using market orders and paying what crossing the spreads costs. P&L is in ticks times lots after costs over 20,000 units of time, and every figure is a mean over seeds 101 and up (12 where a table has them, the eight of `FEW`, 101 to 108, where it varies one thing) with its standard error over the seeds and the number of seeds on which it was positive. No setting was chosen on those seeds. Every one (a trader's, the dial's values, the burn) was chosen on seeds 1 to 8 and held fixed along the dial: the index trader's window of 400, entry of 2, exit of 0, rate of 1 and one basket, at `index_volatility` 0.4 in the sum convention and the sectors market; the deal trader's announcements of 0.004 a unit, entry of 0, 5 lots and rate of 1, at `breaks` 0.20 and not for its P&L; and momentum's look back and hold of 100 decisions of 5 units, at a trend of 0.05 in the sectors market, out of 15 settings screened on the books-free route's P&L. The burns are 2,100 units for the index (10,100 for the sampled hedge's rows), 600 for the deals, 2,925 for the trend, and 10,465, notebook 13's, for the pairs rows. The notebook has been run three times in full (2026-11-12, 13 and 14, in 41, 44 and 45 minutes), and each rerun printed again, to the digit, every line of the run before it, apart from timing lines and, in the third, 16 lines relabeled on purpose. Three cells were added after the first run: the three-book sample with the books' own-part shocks off, and the same pairs in the other world, each repeating a check made on seeds 1 to 8 before the first run, and the 1/12 index's gap before costs at the top of the dial on eight other seeds, added because the first run showed it. The third run added prints to existing cells (among them the lots held on the same pairs and the split of the pairs' P&L by where it was made, bare markets of the own parts and spreads, and the mixed market's route on 24 more seeds, added because the first run showed that market's first windows low). Where a figure was looked for after a run, the text says so. The findings, in the notebook's order:
   - **Index: the dial does not start at nothing, so its axis is the premium measured** (section 1: the sectors market, one basket, 8 seeds). With `index_volatility` 0 the order flow's own market orders in the index book still make a premium, a standard deviation of 14.7 ticks of a basket (bare markets, no trader), growing to 20.2, 32.0, 37.5, 58.1 and 113.0 for the sum index at 0.2, 0.4, 0.5, 0.8 and 1.6 (19.6, 30.9, 38.4, 57.7 and 112.6 for the 1/12), which puts both conventions on one axis. The strict control, with the books' impact off as well, has a premium of exactly 0 but is another market in other ways too (the own parts stop answering orders), so it is a point apart and not the left end of the line.
   - **The dial in both conventions** (the same position in the same fair values at one basket, differing in the book the index leg is traded in; P&L with its se, and the seeds on which it was positive):

     | Premium sd (ticks of a basket), sum / 1/12, and `index_volatility` | Sum index | 1/12 index | 1/12 less sum, paired |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | strict control: no premium, no impact | -2,100 (47), 0 of 8 | -6,395 (130), 0 of 8 | -4,295 (135), 0 of 8 |
     | 14.7 / 14.7, 0 | +7 (24), 5 of 8 | -2,322 (105), 0 of 8 | -2,329 (113), 0 of 8 |
     | 20.2 / 19.6, 0.2 | +467 (42), 8 of 8 | -1,202 (48), 0 of 8 | -1,669 (38), 0 of 8 |
     | 32.0 / 30.9, 0.4 (the chosen point) | +1,214 (48), 8 of 8 | -118 (42), 1 of 8 | -1,331 (80), 0 of 8 |
     | 37.5 / 38.4, 0.5 | +1,556 (65), 8 of 8 | +427 (44), 8 of 8 | -1,128 (87), 0 of 8 |
     | 58.1 / 57.7, 0.8 | +2,620 (98), 8 of 8 | +1,664 (111), 8 of 8 | -956 (153), 0 of 8 |
     | 113.0 / 112.6, 1.6 | +6,186 (223), 8 of 8 | +4,246 (102), 8 of 8 | -1,940 (200), 0 of 8 |

     An entry costs 40.5 to 45.2 in the sum index and 69.6 to 83.6 in the 1/12 (40.6 and 71.4 at the chosen point).
   - **Where each breaks even, and what the index leg's cost does.** The sum index at the lowest point of the dial makes +7 (se 24, 5 of 8 seeds positive), 0.3 of a standard error from 0 (a plain ratio), so it breaks even about there, at a premium of 14.7, and every seed is positive from 20.2 (+467, se 42). That is not a bracketed break-even: the dial has no point below 14.7 with the books' impact on, and at the 84 ticks of P&L a tick of premium that its first two points give, two standard errors are about 0.6 of a tick of premium (2 times 24, over 84), so the figure is 14.7 give or take about that on a straight line, and how the line runs below it is not shown. The 1/12's P&L crosses 0 at a premium of 32.5 ticks of a basket (31.6 to 33.4, 5% to 95%, a bootstrap over the seeds; every one of 2,000 draws had a crossing), between -118 (se 42, 1 of 8 seeds positive) at 30.9 and +427 (se 44, 8 of 8) at 38.4. So the book that the index leg is traded in moves the break-even by a factor of about 2.2 (32.5 over 14.7, a plain ratio) for the same position in the same fair values, and the figure belongs to that convention and to this simulator's books, not to an index. At the chosen point the 1/12 is 1,331 (se 80, 0 of 8) below the sum, with 1,533 (se 79) more in costs, on an entry of 71.4 against 40.6, and its own orders push the premium it trades twelve times as far (a full entry of one basket moves it by 7.2 ticks of a basket against 0.6, which is 0.23 and 0.02 of the premium's sd; arithmetic from the settings, not a measurement). The 1/12's interval holds the seeds' spread and the premium's measurement error, not how the line bends between its two points.
   - **The strict controls lose on every seed, and make over 4,000 before costs.** With no premium and no impact the sum index makes -2,100 (se 47, 0 of 8 seeds positive): +4,333 before costs less 6,433 in costs, on 142.4 entries at 45.2 each; the 1/12 -6,395 (se 130, 0 of 8): +4,708 before costs less 11,102 in costs, on 152.2 entries at 72.9. That is the bounce of the index book's mid and not an edge (the sum's 4,333 before costs is more than it makes before costs at any dial point up to 0.8, 4,012, where it makes +2,620 after them), so "before costs" is not read as an edge here or in the other sections.
   - **The surprise at the top of the dial, and what other seeds showed.** At 1.6 the 1/12 made 791 (se 259) less before costs than the sum in the same position on the dial's eight seeds, where on seeds 1 to 8 the two made about the same. It was noticed for being large, and eight other seeds, run after the first run showed it, give +243 (se 246) and the sixteen together -274 (se 218), so the gap before costs is not a finding. The gap after costs is in all sixteen (-1,525, se 169, 0 of 16 seeds positive), and the dearer book's costs, 1,251 (se 69) higher, are most of it (82%, a plain ratio).
   - **What the twelve legs cost and buy** (the sum index, at 0.4 and 1.6). Without the legs the trader makes +1,109 (se 196, 8 of 8 seeds positive) more at 0.4 and +1,184 (se 435, 6 of 8) more at 1.6, its costs 930 (se 107) and 842 (se 56) lower. Before costs the two differ by +180 (se 210) and +342 (se 444): the legs added nothing that eight seeds can see, though a gain of a few hundred is not ruled out. What they buy is risk: over 100 units 6.91 without them against 1.92 with them at 0.4 (3.6 times, a plain ratio) and 10.15 against 7.03 at 1.6, the P&L per unit of that risk 0.017 against 0.032 at 0.4, and a standard error of the mean P&L of 205 against 48. The factors have no drift in this section, so the exposure is risk with nothing expected to be earned by it.
   - **A hedge from three books, and where its edge comes from** (burn 10,100; the full replication makes +1,196, se 44, 8 of 8 in this window). A sample of the three books that best explain the index, one in each sector, at four lots each, beats the full replication by +567 (se 77, 8 of 8 seeds positive). The gain is made before costs (+740, se 147) on as many entries (-0.2, se 1.6), and costs are 173 (se 82) higher, so it is a different trade and not a cheaper hedge; its risk over 100 units is higher (2.66 against 2.00). Why it pays is the books' own parts: the sample's four lots of each of three books leave their own parts in its spread, so the trade is the index trade plus a pairs trade inside three books. With their shocks off (`own_volatility` 0, which leaves the own part that the order flow makes: an sd of 1.2 ticks of a lot against 4.8) the sample is 90 behind the full replication (se 50, 1.8 standard errors, so not separated from 0; 3 of 8 seeds positive) where with them on it is ahead by 567, and most of its extra risk goes with them. On seeds 1 to 8 the gain was +532 (se 68) and went with the shocks too; the cell that runs the check here repeats it and was not added because of anything the first run showed. It is a property of this market's own parts and not a result about sampling an index, and since taking the shocks out changes the order flow as well as the fair values, it does not show that they are the whole reason. In fair values with no trader the full replication's spread has the premium's sd, 32.0 ticks of a basket, and the sample's is larger, 44.1; with the shocks off the premium is 31.8, the full replication's spread 31.8 and the sample's 32.1, so the sample's is 0.3 above the full replication's where with the shocks on it is 12.1 above (plain differences). Read as a premium, a spread of 44.1 is priced by the dial at 683 more than one of 32.0 (a plain interpolation between its rows at 37.5 and 58.1), of the order of the sample's +567: a reading of the spread's size and not a decomposition. Samples of two books and of one cannot cancel the factors (the true factors leave 6.12 and 8.49 lots, against 0.18 for three) and make -95 (se 427) and -144 (se 474), which eight seeds do not separate from 0, and 1,291 (se 403) and 1,340 (se 476) less than the full replication; across their seeds their P&L follows the factor part (correlations +0.95 and +0.98), though not every seed's sign.
   - **Sizes and rates.** Three baskets of the sum index make +2,693 (se 101, 8 of 8) against +1,214 for one: 2.2 times the P&L for three times the size, since before costs they scale 3.0 times and costs 3.6 times, and the risk over 100 units 2.85 times (5.48 against 1.92). Trading less does not help: a rate of 0.25 at three baskets lowers costs by 1,183 (se 280) and gives up 1,785 (se 461) before costs, a paired -602 (se 225, 2 of 8 seeds positive), and an entry of 1 with a rate of 0.25 at one basket is 1,413 (se 114, 0 of 8) below the chosen setting. Three baskets of the 1/12 lose 6,318 (se 513, 0 of 8), 9,012 (se 532) below the sum at the same size, where three times the one-basket gap would be 3,993: a different trade, on 57.1 entries against 41.1, in which by arithmetic from the settings an entry's own orders move the premium by 21.6 ticks of a basket, 0.70 of its sd (against 0.23 at one basket and 0.06 and 0.02 for the sum index at three baskets and one); the notebook does not measure how much of the loss that is. All of it is eight seeds at one point of the dial, with no size above three baskets and one rate.
   - **Deals: the control and the dial** (section 2: the sectors market, announcements at 0.004 a unit, 12 seeds). Where the price of a break is the true chance (`breaks` 0.40) the deal trader loses: -2,379 (se 348, 0 of 12 seeds positive), of which 1,984 is costs (1.25 a lot) and -395 is before costs, on a share of ended deals that broke of 0.415. The same seed has the same two books announced at the same times at every value of the dial, and what breaks at one value breaks at every higher one (983 deals compared, asserted), so the dial changes how a deal ends and nothing else:

     | `breaks` | Share that broke (counted) | P&L (se) | Seeds positive | A lot pair (se) | c / (c - b) |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 0.40 (the control) | 0.415 | -2,379 (348) | 0 of 12 | -6.24 (0.97) | 0.308 |
     | 0.35 | 0.363 | -1,094 (284) | 2 of 12 | -2.93 (0.95) | 0.310 |
     | 0.30 | 0.305 | +271 (275) | 7 of 12 | +0.56 (0.90) | 0.311 |
     | 0.25 | 0.246 | +1,652 (329) | 12 of 12 | +4.00 (0.85) | 0.308 |
     | 0.20 | 0.200 | +2,747 (346) | 12 of 12 | +6.78 (0.79) | 0.308 |
     | 0.10 | 0.080 | +5,520 (333) | 12 of 12 | +13.77 (0.53) | 0.317 |
     | 0.00 | 0.000 | +7,370 (214) | 12 of 12 | +18.42 (0.11) | n/a |

     Costs are 1,984 to 2,047 at every row, so the dial moves what a deal is worth and not what the trade costs. The trade crosses 0 near a true chance of 0.30, and from 0.25 every seed is positive.
   - **The break-even, by each route, and why they agree.** The line through the seven means puts it at a true chance of 0.310 (bootstrap sd 0.010, 95% 0.290 to 0.331); the same line against the counted share of ended deals that broke, at 0.316 (bootstrap sd 0.004, 95% 0.307 to 0.324); and the deals' own completed and broken lot pairs, c / (c - b), at 0.311 and 0.308 (bootstrap sd 0.003 each, at the rows 0.30 and 0.25). They agree because every row is the same two outcomes, a completed lot pair of +18.42 to +18.65 and a broken one of -41.26 to -41.81 from 0.40 to 0.20 (-39.79 at 0.10), weighted by the share that broke, and c / (c - b) is the share at which a lot pair makes nothing. The deals' way is the tighter because which deals happened to break drops out of the ratio, while the line in the nominal `breaks` carries that luck: the dial's own arithmetic gives its root an sd of 0.014 over twelve fresh seeds, the spread to read it with, where against the counted share, with that luck taken out, it is 0.004. The counted share is not quite the deals' own unit. From 0.40 to 0.20 it leans 0.003 to 0.004 toward the broken against the deals with a kept record (0.3048 against 0.3012 at the 0.30 row, where 971 deals ended and 966 have a record, and the five without one all broke; at 0.10 the two are 0.0001 apart, the other way), and the line's root against the share among the kept records is 0.312 (bootstrap sd 0.004, 95% 0.304 to 0.320), so in the deals' own unit the ways agree to within 0.004 (0.312, 0.311 and 0.308). At the break-even the edge, 60 x (0.40 - 0.311) at the deals' own figure, is 5.3 ticks a lot pair, of which 5.00 is the cost of the lot pair (four spreads at 1.25) and the rest, about 0.3, is what a lot pair loses before costs at a fair price (the 0.30 row's printed means at a share of 0.40 make -5.34 a lot pair: -5.00 of cost and -0.34 before costs). So the price of a break must exceed the true chance by about 0.09 (0.40 less 0.311) to pay for the lot pair, of which 0.083 (5.00 over 60) is the four spreads.
   - **What a deal makes: many small gains and a few larger losses.** At 0.20, over 966 deals, a completed deal makes +18.57 a lot pair (sd 3.22) and a broken one, 19.6% of deals, loses 41.72 (sd 8.52, worst -69.20), which is 2.2 completed deals; the skew is -1.57, the hit rate 80.4%, the profit factor 1.83 and the worst deal -346 ticks x lots. A seed's total (+892 to +4,562, pooling about 81 deals) shows none of that, and neither do the two risk figures (0.014 over one unit and 0.019 over 100). The shape is built in by the simulator's break and is not evidence about real mergers.
   - **The mixed market.** The mixed market gives the sectors market's dial to within noise (8 seeds, at 0.40, 0.20 and 0.00): -2,242 (se 589, 0 of 8), +2,592 (se 630, 8 of 8) and +7,400 (se 271, 8 of 8), with paired differences from the sectors market of -118, +42 and +107 (se 283, 184 and 114). A broken deal's loss has the same mean but is twice as spread (an sd 1.96 and 2.39 times as large, ratios of printed sds), as the gap between the two books' fair values wanders 2.38 times as far there; two ratios of one size are not a decomposition.
   - **Late entry is the mids' noise.** Entering at a gap of 24 ticks in the control makes 744 more than entering at the first decision (se 137, 8 of 8 seeds; -1,380 against -2,124), but on the 259 deals entered late a lot pair is +1.54 (se 0.23) more in the mids and -0.28 (se 0.11, 2.5 standard errors below 0, a plain ratio) at fair values alone, so what the mids add to a late entry is positive on all eight seeds (+1.81, se 0.13). That is the mids' bounce, not an edge. By the model's arithmetic a deal still on at the later time has drifted toward the offer, which a late entry misses, so the negative sign is what one should expect.
   - **The records at a run's end lean toward broken deals.** A deal that breaks is over early where one that completes takes its whole length, so the records a run ends with hold the breaks and not the completions. In a window of 1,500 units 0.229 of them broke against 0.194 of the cut's, and the bias in a lot pair is -2.224 (se 0.475), shrinking to -0.254 (se 0.104) at 10,000 and -0.139 (se 0.074) at 20,000. Every per-deal figure above uses the deals announced at least a deal's length and two decisions before the end; in the whole run of 20,600 units the cut drops 3 of 969 records and moves a lot pair from 6.66 to 6.78.
   - **Pairs and deals: what deals cost a pairs trader** (8 seeds, burn 10,465, announcements at 0.002 a unit, half the deal trader's rate). At a fair price deals take 3,659 (se 344, 8 of 8 seeds positive) from the pairs trader that does not read them: +2,044 (se 272, 8 of 8) against +5,703 (se 118, 8 of 8) with none, about 64% of its P&L (3,659 over 5,703, a plain ratio), on about half the entries (138.4 against 272.4) and more than twice the risk over 100 units (7.42 against 3.29). At a fair price there is no bet to lose, so the loss is not a premium given up, and the notebook shows the loss and not its mechanism. The no-deals row is notebook 13's pairs row, the same runs, and not a replication.
   - **The readers against the blind trader.** A reader keeps out of a deal's target, either for the deal's life (quarantine 0) or until the step has left its window (the default, 2,012 units). Each reader less the blind trader of the same world, at 0.40 and 0.20 on the pairs each chose in its own world and at 0.20 on the pairs of 0.40 held, and the shift of that difference across the dial (0.20 less 0.40) on each set of pairs:

     | Reader less blind | At 0.40, own pairs | At 0.20, own pairs | At 0.20, pairs of 0.40 held | Shift, own pairs | Shift, pairs held |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | out for the deal's life | -119 (217), 4 of 8 | +429 (256), 5 of 8 | +617 (193), 8 of 8 | +548 (367), 5 of 8 | +737 (202), 7 of 8 |
     | default quarantine | -692 (164), 0 of 8 | +88 (295), 3 of 8 | +132 (205), 4 of 8 | +780 (278), 6 of 8 | +824 (200), 8 of 8 |

     On each trader's own pairs reading the news bought nothing that can be told from nothing, except that at the fair price the default quarantine cost 692 (4.2 standard errors from 0, on 85.9 entries against 138.4). The shifts on own pairs are +548 and +780 (only the second is separated from 0, by 2.8 standard errors, a plain ratio), and on one set of pairs +737 and +824 (3.6 and 4.1 standard errors), 136 and 49 below 873, the bet's arithmetic on the pairs held (next bullet; plain differences), so that what ignoring the news costs depends on whether the gap is fairly priced survives holding the pairs fixed, for both readers: a reader avoids a bet that the blind trader bears.
   - **The bet, in lots and in the targets' books.** Of the 37.2 deals a run announced in the span and over by its end at 0.20, the blind trader holds the target of 24.1 two decisions after the announcement (short in 20.4, long in 3.8), and 57.9 lots net short in all (at 0.40, 22.6 of 37.5 deals and 80.1 lots, where the bet is fair), so that the bet's arithmetic at 0.20 is 12.0 ticks a lot times 57.9, 694. Its P&L is 1,207 (se 178, 0 of 8 seeds positive) lower at 0.20 than at 0.40, on every one of the eight seeds. Split by where it was made (in the targets' books while the deals are on, at the decisions' mids and before the spread paid, and everywhere else), 686 (se 194) of the fall is in the targets' books, about the bet, and 521 (se 125) is everywhere else. The readers fall too (659, se 301, and 427, se 180), though they hold none of a target while its deal is on, and their falls are made everywhere else. A seed's two worlds have 3 to 6 of eight pairs in common, so the fall is not the cost of the bet alone. With the pairs of 0.40 held in the market of 0.20 the blind trader's fall is 761 (se 186, 0 of 8 seeds positive), which is 112 less than the bet's arithmetic on the 72.8 net short lots it holds on those pairs, 873 (0.6 of the fall's standard error; a plain difference and ratio), with the targets' books 880 lower (se 193) and everywhere else 119 higher (se 185): the fall is about the size of the bet. The readers' are -24 (se 115) and +63 (se 40), so their falls on their own pairs were the pairs. The pairs of 0.40 do better in the market of 0.20 than the pairs its own mids gave, by 446 (se 143), 635 (se 242) and 491 (se 166) for the three traders, on 7 of 8 seeds each: one comparison seen three times, since a seed's traders share their pairs. The same-pairs test was made on seeds 1 to 8 before the first run, expecting that the fall with the pairs held is the bet, which holds here; the lots and the split were added after that run. Why the pairs of 0.40 do better in the market of 0.20 is notebook 16's section 2 (point 12).
   - **What can and cannot be concluded about reading the news.** On the same pairs at 0.20 the reader out for the deal's life is ahead of the blind trader on every seed (+617, se 193, 8 of 8), and the default reader is not (+132, se 205, 4 of 8). The lead is about the bet the reader does not hold, and the split measures that: in the targets' books while the deals are on it is +885 (se 316, 6 of 8) on the pairs held, against 873 by arithmetic, and +693 (se 295, 7 of 8) on the blind trader's own pairs, against 694, and everywhere else the reader is 268 behind (se 257, 4 of 8) and 264 behind (se 201, 2 of 8), neither separated from 0. On its own pairs +429 is 265 below the bet's arithmetic of 694, about its standard error, and +617 is 256 below 873 (plain differences). Avoiding an overpriced bet is how reading the news pays in this market, and that is not a reason to discount the lead. The reason is the comparison a trader would face: on each world's own pairs the lead is +429 (se 256), 1.7 standard errors from 0, which eight seeds do not separate, and the 3.2 standard errors on the pairs held are on another world's pairs, which a trader cannot choose; the part in the targets' books alone is 2.3 standard errors from 0 on its own pairs (+693, se 295, a plain ratio), but it is the bet, and everywhere else the reader is behind (by 264, se 201). So it is a lead and not a finding that reading the news pays a trader: it is shown to be worth about the bet where the pairs are held, and whether it pays a trader on its own pairs at this premium is not shown here (notebook 16 tests it on 24 other seeds: point 12). There are two values of the dial, so no line and no break-even; the readers keep out of targets only (this simulator's rule); and the table is about quarantines at this rate, not about reading the news in general, and no comparison with the deal trader, whose rate is twice as high.
   - **Trend: the dial, the break-evens and the route they come from** (section 3: momentum with the dollar alone taken out, burn 2,925, the market on 8 seeds at one window of 20,000 units a seed). The route's gain is the books-free route's long-run factor part less its value at trend 0 (36 seeds at 0.02 and 0.03, and at 0 where it is 0 by construction; 12 at the other points; 8 in the mixed market), and the net is the market's P&L less the route's factor part of the same window plus that gain:

     | Trend | Sectors: a window a seed | Route's gain | Net | Mixed: a window a seed | Route's gain | Net |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 0 | -3,649 (1,406), 2 of 8 | +0 | -4,094 (309) | -3,857 (518), 0 of 8 | +0 | -3,294 (143) |
     | 0.01 | not run | +463 (97) | -3,401 (259) | not run | +289 (28) | -2,934 (109) |
     | 0.02 | -2,116 (1,266), 2 of 8 | +2,013 (94) | -1,619 (266) | not run | +994 (29) | -2,160 (96) |
     | 0.03 | +175 (1,293), 5 of 8 | +4,335 (140) | +742 (294) | -2,824 (401), 0 of 8 | +2,159 (54) | -924 (124) |
     | 0.04 | not run | +7,096 (377) | +3,812 (431) | not run | +3,543 (69) | +523 (95) |
     | 0.05 | +7,613 (1,384), 8 of 8 | +10,332 (471) | +7,359 (516) | -298 (454), 3 of 8 | +5,112 (86) | +2,155 (159) |
     | 0.1 | +30,040 (2,249), 8 of 8 | +28,606 (783) | +26,691 (834) | not run | +13,692 (213) | no net |

     In the sectors market the net crosses 0 at a trend of 0.0269 ticks of drift a unit of time (resample sd 0.0012, 5% to 95% 0.0251 to 0.0289, all 2,000 resamples cross), between 0.02 and 0.03. There the drift is 22.5% of the variance of a factor's move over the look back and two successive moves correlate 0.15, so most of what a factor does over 500 units is still its random walk. The market's own windows, one a seed, put it at 0.0292 (sd 0.0051, 0.0198 to 0.0347), an interval that holds the net's figure and is too loose to read it from. The mixed market breaks even at 0.0364 (sd 0.0006, 0.0352 to 0.0374), with the gain of a trend about half the sectors market's (0.47 to 0.52 of it from 0.02 up); its own windows alone have no crossing. Both are a little higher than seeds 1 to 8 led one to expect (0.0269 against about 0.025, and 0.0364 against 0.0337). It is a long-run break-even: the factor part is the route's long run in fair values and the rest is the market's, from its eight windows, which now set its precision. The interval is the seeds' sampling spread and holds neither how far the line bends between 0.02 and 0.03 nor the settings, which move it: a look back of 200 broke even about 0.004 lower on seeds 1 to 8, so the break-even is this trader's and not momentum's.
   - **What the books-free route is, and what it can and cannot claim.** It is the same trader given the fair values alone on its own grid of 5 units, with no books and no costs, holding its position between decisions; its factor part is what it held of each factor times the factors' moves, summed in blocks of 20,000 units from the same burn, so that its first block is the market's window. It is needed because one window's factor part scatters by more than the gain near the break-even (a block's sd is 2,826 against a gain of 1,936 at 0.02). On 24 more seeds the gain agrees with the dial's twelve (+2,052, se 107, against +1,936, se 190, at 0.02; +4,406, se 158, against +4,195, se 284, at 0.03). It matches the market's factor part for the dollar-alone trader window by window (correlation 0.965 or more at the values with a trend, a gap of 1.0% to 9.3% of it, none as much as two standard errors from 0, positive here and negative on seeds 1 to 8). It claims the expected factor part of this rule in fair values and, through the net, the market's mean P&L, and nothing about costs, the rest or risk; it is not used for a trader that takes factors out.
   - **The factor part is the P&L and the risk.** At 0.05 the paired P&L rises +11,262 (se 1,018, 8 of 8 seeds positive) over trend 0, of which the factor part is +10,362 (se 947), while the trader holds about the same factors along the dial (an exposure of 18.67 at 0 and 20.04 at 0.05): the trend decides whether holding them pays. The risk over 100 units is 1.96, 2.19 and 2.65 times the one-unit risk at 0, 0.05 and 0.1 (plain ratios), so the one-unit figure flatters (0.037 against 0.017 per unit of risk at 0.05), and the 100-unit figure still flatters a drift that lasts 500 units.
   - **Taking factors out.** At 0.05 the trader neutral to one estimated factor as well as the dollar keeps 62.3% of the dollar-alone trader's factor part in the sectors market (+6,714 against +10,783) and makes +3,576 (se 836, 8 of 8 seeds positive) against +7,613; in the mixed market it keeps 104.4% (+2,434 against +2,331) and makes -157 (se 673, 3 of 8) against -298. Neutral to three it keeps 1.8% and 3.7% and loses on every seed (-4,574, se 230, and -4,113, se 183), and a trend of 0.05 adds only +487 (se 185, 8 of 8) to the sectors market's. The first estimated factor is, to the figures printed, the direction the dollar already takes out (a cosine of its eigenportfolio with the vector of ones of 1.00 in the sectors market and 0.97 in the mixed). What the trader projects out is the books' exposures to it: in the sectors market they have a cosine of 1.00 with the vector of ones and a tilt of 0.08 of their size, which lies partly along the sector factors' loadings (cosines 0.57, 0.45 and 0.43); in the mixed market a cosine of 0.97, a tilt of 0.24 and a cosine of 0.95 of the tilt with the market factor's loadings. `neutralize` removes the direction of an exposure row whatever its size, so a small tilt in a row of nearly ones is taken out whole, which fits the 37.7% (100 less 62.3) taken out in the sectors market (the tilt lies partly along the sector factors, which trend) and the mixed market's nothing (the tilt is the market factor's own, which never trends). The notebook does not print how much of the factor part lies along the tilt, so it does not show that this is the reason.
   - **The mirror, and the traders that take every factor out.** Reversal makes -11,289 (se 1,250, 0 of 8 seeds positive) at 0.05 in the sectors market, with a factor part of -10,974 against momentum's +10,783, and -2,619 (se 403, 0 of 8) in the mixed, with -2,339 against +2,331. The two added together make -3,676 (se 182) in the sectors market, with a factor part of -191 (se 173), a P&L before costs of -47 (se 139) and costs of +3,629, so the sum is about minus their costs: no trader here is paid for the factor part without carrying its risk. A trend of 0.05 changes pairs by -77 (se 151, 5 of 8 seeds positive) and residuals by -224 (se 269, 3 of 8), which eight seeds do not tell from nothing; but those standard errors are 2.6% and 6.7% of the P&L, so a change of a few percent could not have been seen. The pairs row at trend 0 is notebook 13's row, the same runs (+5,706, se 81), and not a replication.
   - **The first windows, in both markets, and what other seeds showed.** In the sectors market the first windows were not shown to be lucky: on the dial's twelve seeds the route's first block, which is the market's window, sat above its seed's other blocks by 234 to 3,687 at the values with a trend, and the market's own eight windows were -215, -573, -616 and -339 at 0.01 to 0.04, +88 at 0.05 and +3,323 at 0.1, none as much as two standard errors from 0 by either of the two the cell prints (the paired one over the seeds, and the one from all the blocks, of which the larger is the one to lean on); on seeds 1 to 8 they were 1,430 to 5,903 above at the same values. In the mixed market the eight windows at 0.05 made -298 (se 454, 3 of 8 seeds positive) where the net makes +2,155 (se 159), because the route's first block sat 2,538 below its seed's other blocks, 3.46 times the standard error from all the blocks (733), the larger of the two and the one to lean on, and 5.59 times the paired one (454). On 24 other seeds, run after the eight showed it, the first block less the other nine is +2 (se 235), -144 (se 298), -256 (se 346) and -379 (se 390) at 0, 0.03, 0.04 and 0.05, none as much as one standard error from 0, and the eight's -2,538 at 0.05 is 2,159 below the 24's, 3.6 and 2.5 times the standard error of that difference (plain calculations, by the paired standard errors and by the ones from all the blocks). The 32 together are 919 below (se 353) at 0.05, which leans toward the gap because the eight are in it. Nothing in the model makes a first block differ from the others, so the shortfall reads as these eight seeds' luck, though one of a few hundred is not ruled out on other seeds. It does not move the break-even: the gain that sets the crossing is the same on the 24 to within its standard error (+2,171, se 81, and +3,552, se 116, at 0.03 and 0.04, against the eight's +2,159, se 54, and +3,543, se 69).
   - **What the three have in common, and where they differ.** Each trade loses where the dial says there is nothing to find (the strict controls, -2,100 and -6,395; a true chance of 0.40, -2,379; a trend of 0, -4,094) and pays once the dial is turned some way from there, and how far depends in part on what the trade costs: the same index position breaks even at a premium of about 14.7 ticks of a basket through one book and 32.5 through the other, an entry costing 40.6 and 71.4 and the trader's own orders pushing the premium twelve times as far in the second (by arithmetic from the settings); the deal trader's edge at its break-even is 5.3 ticks a lot pair, of which 5.00 is the cost of the lot pair; and at no trend 2,105 of momentum's rest of -4,070 is costs, the remainder being what the books' own parts and timing do against a trade that follows them. The three dials are in three units and each break-even belongs to its trader's size and costs, so none says that one trade is easier than another. What the P&L looks like differed. The index and the deals pay on every seed once they pay (every seed positive from a premium of 20.2 for the sum index and 38.4 for the 1/12, and from a true chance of 0.25 for the deals), though the deals' steadiness is in the total, and a broken deal loses 2.2 completed ones. Momentum rides a factor: in the sectors market one window a seed is positive on 5 of 8 seeds at 0.03 (+175, se 1,293) and on 8 of 8 at 0.05, and in the mixed market on 3 of 8 at 0.05 (-298, se 454) where the net is +2,155 (se 159). The control is not nothing in any of the three: the order flow's own premium of 14.7 ticks at the dial's 0, and +4,333 before costs at the sum index's strict control; a counted share of broken deals of 0.415 against the price of 0.40; and a window a seed at no trend of -3,649 (se 1,406) against a net of -4,094 (se 309). In all three before costs holds the mids' bounce.
   - **What the fresh seeds showed against what seeds 1 to 8 led one to expect.** Several figures sat where seeds 1 to 8 led one to expect: the chosen index row, +1,214 against +1,145 (se 38); the 1/12's break-even, 32.5 (31.6 to 33.4) against 33.0 (32.0 to 34.0); the sum index at the dial's lowest point, +7 (se 24, 5 of 8) against -30 (se 58, 3 of 8), both within noise of 0; the three-book sample's gain, +567 (se 77) against +532 (se 68), which goes with the own parts' shocks (-90, se 50, 3 of 8); and the deals' c / (c - b), 0.311 and 0.308 against 0.305 to 0.309, with the line's 0.310 against a forecast of 0.305 with an sd of 0.014. Two checks made on seeds 1 to 8 before the first run were repeated and agree (the sample's edge goes with the shocks; with the pairs held the blind trader's fall is about its bet). Others did not. The 1/12 less the sum before costs at 1.6 was -791 (se 259) where about 0 was expected, and shrank on other seeds (+243, se 246; -274, se 218, on the sixteen). The deals' standard errors at 0.30 and 0.35 are 275 and 284 against a forecast of 320 to 385, though 346, 329 and 348 at 0.20, 0.25 and 0.40 are inside it. The reader with no quarantine is +429 (se 256, 5 of 8 seeds) against the blind trader at 0.20 on its own pairs, where seeds 1 to 8 had +732 (se 267, 7 of 8), and +617 (se 193, 8 of 8) on the pairs held, where they had +645 (se 275, 7 of 8). A late entry's lot pair at fair values is 0.28 less (se 0.11) than the first decision's, where seeds 1 to 8 had +0.00 (se 0.10); the two differ by 1.9 times the standard error of their difference (0.15, the root of the two squared standard errors), which is not as much as two. The sectors market's first windows were not shown to be lucky (-616 to +3,323 against 1,430 to 5,903 above on seeds 1 to 8), and the mixed market's were low, which 24 other seeds did not repeat. The market's factor part against the route's is positive here and was negative on seeds 1 to 8, none as much as two standard errors from 0. The first estimated factor takes out 37.7% in the sectors market where about half was expected, and nothing in the mixed market where some was. None of the figures that differed was predicted, and apart from the 1/12's gap at 1.6 none has been repeated on other seeds, so none is a finding beyond these seeds. The notebook makes many paired comparisons and corrects none of them for how many, so one difference of two to three standard errors is a lead and not a result; the reader's +617, 3.2 standard errors on a set of pairs held for the purpose, was set up beforehand on seeds 1 to 8 and is a lead because those pairs are another world's and a trader's own pairs do not separate it.
   - **What it does not show.** That any of the three trades pays in a real market, or where its break-even is in one: each is the cost structure of this simulator's books and, for the index, of one convention for the index book's liquidity (14.7 against 32.5), at one trader's size and costs, in three units that are not on one scale. For the index: a bracketed break-even for the sum index; that the premium is what the rule trades; that a sampled hedge is as good in general; that eight seeds settle the legs' worth; and anything about the mixed market, other windows, other half-lives of the premium, sizes above three baskets or a rate other than 0.25. For the deals: whether a premium of this size exists in a real market; the break-even to better than the interval printed; other sizes, lengths, premiums or levels (the break-even moves by -0.012 at 2 lots and +0.020 at 10 through the cost of a lot pair alone; the level here is 3,000, and at a level of tens of ticks deals mean little); the skew as a scorecard sees it; whether a late entry is a way to earn; how much of a broken deal's wider spread in the mixed market is the gap's wander; what the pairs table says of reading the news in general; and how much of the blind trader's fall on its own pairs is the bet and how much the pairs, beyond the split, which is at the decisions' mids, before the spread paid, and divides the rest no further. For the trend: that momentum pays in a market like a real one, or which explanation of momentum is right (only a factor can continue here); a break-even for other settings or drifts; the long run from the market's windows (one a seed); the factor part's standard error for the traders that take factors out (+193 and +86 are not tested against 0); why one estimated factor takes out what it does; any effect of a trend on pairs and residuals beyond "none large enough to see"; risk beyond 100 units; and the mixed market's break-even beyond what its interval holds (the market was run at 0.03 and 0.05, not at 0.04, where the crossing lies between). All of it is eight to twelve seeds of one simulated market, with settings chosen beforehand on a few small grids on seeds 1 to 8 that the notebook does not print, market orders only, no fees, no financing and no passive orders, each trader alone, and no comparison corrected for how many are made. Three of these notebook 16 takes up (point 12): whether reading the news pays a trader on its own pairs, why the pairs of the market with a risk premium are worth less, and sector index books.
11. **Stage 4 on the Stat-arb view** (`app/statarb.py`, `static/js/lob-statarb.js`, `static/js/lob-panes.js`, the `statarb_*` templates and `panes.html`; the form, board and chart as in 3R.6). Nine market settings, for three features, and two kinds of trader, built after the library. The summaries of the pairs, momentum, index and deals kinds, the hover texts of the premium's volatility, the true chance of a break and the factor trend, and the guide's Stat-arb passage quote what notebook 15 printed, each with its market and with its level of 3,000 against the view's 1,000, and a test pins each figure word for word; no other summary or hint quotes one.
   - **The market's settings** (`MARKET_PARAMS`), in one collapsed `<details id="statarb-more">` inside the settings form, which stays one form, in two columns, with a hover hint on each; it opens if any of the three is on, and a reload keeps it open:

     | Setting | Default | Range | What it does |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | An index book | off | on or off | `FactorValues(index=True)`: one more book, `IDX`, the sum of the others, with a premium of its own |
